# Rule of Carnage Episode Notes

Detailed design findings from **83 distinct videos** in the six selected playlists. The notes retain **689 findings**, including context, mechanisms, tradeoffs and practical prompts. Repeated ideas are retained where an episode adds a different argument or example.

Start with [[Rule of Carnage Skirmish Game Design Reference]] for the thematic guide. Use [[Rule of Carnage Episode Index]] to find a title, duration, playlist or capture method.

**How to read these notes.** Discussion paragraphs paraphrase the hosts' arguments and examples. A **tradeoff to consider** records a qualification or analytical implication; a **design prompt** is an editorial application, not a quotation or an adopted Settlements rule. Examples of other games report the discussion, not an independent verification of their current rulebooks. Timestamp links lead to supporting passages; broad arguments can extend beyond the linked range.

**Source limits.** These are captured captions, including automatic speech recognition. Names and isolated technical terms can be wrong. Collection checks verify file integrity and timing coverage; they do not verify every word against audio. Introductions, promotions, jokes and repeated digressions are omitted unless they contribute design information. The UKGE walk-around is indexed even though its captured speech provides little substantive design material. This is a synthesis, not a verbatim transcript edition.

## How to Design a Tabletop Miniatures Deployment Phase

**Source:** [How to Design a Tabletop Miniatures Deployment Phase](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=we5XkK7IS28) · **Video ID:** `we5XkK7IS28`

Terrain setup and conventional model deployment; the promised follow-up covers alternatives, so this is not a complete survey of deployment design.

### Terrain setup guidance

Terrain rules must teach what board the game expects; a vague prompt leaves the first game's quality to chance. A broad description delegates layout to intuition, while precise constraints may be impossible with varied home terrain.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Precision can be brittle; descriptive freedom risks an unfair or unplayable table.

**Design prompt:** Match guidance to audience experience and use concrete examples without assuming standardized terrain.

[Supporting discussion, 6:35–7:37](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=we5XkK7IS28&t=395s)

### Terrain reference images

A representative table image communicates density, scale, and open space efficiently. Players infer relative terrain size and empty area from a visual example.

**Tradeoff to consider:** An image gives an example, not a universal layout for every collection or table.

**Design prompt:** Show a playable table and state which key variable the image demonstrates, especially density.

[Supporting discussion, 8:55–9:57](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=we5XkK7IS28&t=535s)

### Flexible terrain as hobby inspiration

Terrain-generation procedures can be useful even when players cannot reproduce every generated setting literally. Unusual locations can prompt players to imagine and build new terrain themes rather than arrange generic pieces.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Specific generators may be impractical for immediate play when players lack the required terrain, yet still serve creative goals.

**Design prompt:** Clarify whether terrain guidance targets balanced setup, scenario variety, or hobby inspiration; avoid presenting aspirational output as ready-to-play.

[Supporting discussion, 11:39–12:43](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=we5XkK7IS28&t=699s)

### Terrain supplied with a game

Low-cost paper terrain can establish a baseline layout and teach intended density. Examples cited included inexpensive terrain footprints that players could later replace with sturdier pieces.

**Tradeoff to consider:** A starter set improves consistency and onboarding but limits the initial visual palette and adds components.

**Design prompt:** Where terrain access is a barrier, provide or specify a minimal starter footprint and permit equivalent substitutes.

[Supporting discussion, 13:21–14:27](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=we5XkK7IS28&t=801s)

### Deployment has strategic weight

Deployment can decide outcomes before players understand the game, especially with hard counters or slow movement. A unit placed opposite the wrong enemy may never reach its useful matchup; positioning also determines objective access and aura coverage.

**Tradeoff to consider:** It creates planning but can punish new players for knowledge they lack.

**Design prompt:** Assess repositioning speed and matchup constraints; provide onboarding guidance when early mistakes are hard to recover from.

[Supporting discussion, 15:45–16:49](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=we5XkK7IS28&t=945s)

### Deployment zones as narrative

Opposite-edge deployment communicates two forces approaching and creates an opening beat before close combat. The gap makes early turns an approach, evoking distinct arrivals and delaying full engagement.

**Tradeoff to consider:** This familiar story and tension may be useful, but designers can remove it when it does not suit the desired engagement.

**Design prompt:** Choose edge zones because their arrival story and approach period support the game, not by inheritance.

[Supporting discussion, 20:46–21:48](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=we5XkK7IS28&t=1246s)

### Player counts beyond two

Two-sided deployment can break down in multiplayer by making one participant the exposed middle player. When two opponents are nearer to one player than each other, their forces can converge on that target.

**Tradeoff to consider:** The conventional arrangement is legible for two sides but may distribute pressure poorly among three independent players.

**Design prompt:** Test deployment with objectives and player count; examine whether positions or incentives cause predictable pile-ons.

[Supporting discussion, 23:01–24:05](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=we5XkK7IS28&t=1381s)

### Alternating versus whole-side deployment

Alternating placement enables counter-placement; whole-side placement can shorten setup for large games. Taking turns lets each player react to visible choices, but large force counts make the sequence long enough that players may switch to placing the rest at once.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Alternating placement adds reactive choices at a time cost; placing whole forces cuts handling but changes those opportunities. Simultaneous placement is a separate option.

**Design prompt:** Estimate placement count and setup burden at maximum game size, then preserve intended tactics without overwhelming setup.

[Supporting discussion, 25:54–26:58](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=we5XkK7IS28&t=1554s)

### Familiar defaults and deliberate design

Conventional deployment is quick, recognizable, and narratively clear, but familiarity alone is insufficient reason to keep it. Opposing edges establish a no-man's-land; designers should consider those effects before retaining the procedure or experimenting.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Keeping the norm reduces explanation cost; changing it may fit the game better but requires learning.

**Design prompt:** State the purpose of the selected deployment method and test its consequences rather than treating genre habit as mandatory.

[Supporting discussion, 26:39–27:43](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=we5XkK7IS28&t=1599s)

**Episode-specific limits**

- Captions' substantive content ends around minute 31 despite 34:32 metadata.
- Auto-captions contain occasional errors; garbled names are not relied on.

## Designing a Movement Phase in Tabletop Miniatures Games

**Source:** [Designing a Movement Phase in Tabletop Miniatures Games](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=mX0cDWlOTuM) · **Video ID:** `mX0cDWlOTuM`

Movement systems, movement choices, terrain interaction, and height; the captions include post-closing material.

### Movement expresses game identity

Movement is a defining part of miniature game identity, and can be understood as free or momentum-based. Free movement lets figures travel within a distance; momentum movement constrains facing, turning, or sequence, making vehicle-like motion central.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Free movement is easy to operate but can make positioning interchangeable; momentum adds character and constraints with more rules overhead.

**Design prompt:** Choose the movement model to express what the game is about, not only to copy a familiar genre convention.

[Supporting discussion, 2:26–3:33](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=mX0cDWlOTuM&t=146s)

### Movement freedom and recovery from positioning errors

How easily two units can swap positions during play is a useful indicator of how much the game values maneuver. In formation games, crossing or reorienting can consume much of a game; in loose skirmish systems, swapping sides may cost little movement.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Easy repositioning supports fluid tactics but reduces the lasting consequences of initial placement; difficult maneuver makes positioning consequential but can lock players into mistakes.

**Design prompt:** Assess the cost of repositioning and decide whether the game should reward long-term formation planning or rapid tactical adaptation.

[Supporting discussion, 4:14–5:21](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=mX0cDWlOTuM&t=254s)

### Unbounded movement can break choices

Even when movement is not meant to be the main phase, extreme freedom can erase meaningful positional risk. The cited infinite-movement ability let a powerful unit appear wherever useful, eliminating the cost of choosing a bad position and destabilizing play.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Movement abilities can feel exciting and expressive, but unlimited reach can bypass intended counterplay and decisions.

**Design prompt:** Stress-test maximum movement abilities against powerful units, objectives, and positional mistakes; ensure range still imposes a meaningful cost.

[Supporting discussion, 6:36–7:40](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=mX0cDWlOTuM&t=396s)

### Pressure makes free movement meaningful

Free movement can still support careful decisions when destinations interact with enemy pressure, sight lines, or control areas. The speakers cite aura radii, scheme-marker radii, and projected sight lines as ways that otherwise simple movement changes exposure and influence.

**Tradeoff to consider:** These constraints create meaningful positioning without complex movement geometry, but they require clear rules for how the pressure areas work.

**Design prompt:** If movement is geometrically simple, locate tactical depth in the consequences of destination, such as enemy reactions or objective control.

[Supporting discussion, 8:33–9:39](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=mX0cDWlOTuM&t=513s)

### Design for meaningful movement decisions

A movement phase needs a reason for players to care about where and how they move. The discussion describes passive fire, front arcs, and exposure as mechanisms that make a destination dangerous or make a turn affect weapon options.

**Tradeoff to consider:** A movement phase with no consequential choices can feel like pushing pieces through a neutral step; adding consequences requires balancing and explanation.

**Design prompt:** Identify the decision a player should face during movement and connect it to the game's broader tactical goals.

[Supporting discussion, 10:01–11:04](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=mX0cDWlOTuM&t=601s)

### Keep movement rules inside their complexity budget

Refusing a movement option can protect a coherent system when the option forces many exceptions elsewhere. The rejected sideways movement idea would interact with final-position, slides, and collisions, requiring additional rules around each combination.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Allowing the option adds expressive action; refusing it preserves consistency and avoids expanding rules complexity for a narrow effect.

**Design prompt:** Before adding a special movement mode, list the other rules it touches and compare the resulting complexity with simpler ways to achieve the same fiction.

[Supporting discussion, 24:58–26:04](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=mX0cDWlOTuM&t=1498s)

### Simple collision and terrain handling

The speakers praise a small, unambiguous terrain vocabulary: impassable barriers and passable rough ground that costs extra movement. In the example, solid walls stop movement and scrubby rubble doubles the movement cost; Gaslands instead uses hazard tokens for rubble interaction.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Simple categories are memorable and fast; more detailed terrain effects can add texture but create extra handling and adjudication.

**Design prompt:** Start with a few clear movement categories and add special interactions only when they create a distinct play experience.

[Supporting discussion, 27:19–28:23](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=mX0cDWlOTuM&t=1639s)

### Terrain needs depend on the fiction

Movement interaction with terrain should fit what the moving entities are and what the game treats as important. Spaceships may cross a planet without movement obstruction but risk a damage test; infantry games may make walls and routes central to movement.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Uniform terrain rules are easy to reuse, while genre-specific handling better supports fiction and tactical focus.

**Design prompt:** Decide whether terrain obstructs, slows, or merely threatens units based on the game's fiction and intended decisions.

[Supporting discussion, 29:40–30:43](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=mX0cDWlOTuM&t=1780s)

### Height and movement scale

Height rules matter when vertical scale changes movement or visibility, but can add confusing detail when all figures occupy roughly the same scale. The speakers contrast human-scale models where individual heights create needless complexity with games where a giant robot must clearly see over infantry-scale cover.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Ignoring height simplifies play but can create implausible LOS outcomes; detailed relative height can be confusing and burdensome.

**Design prompt:** Tie height rules to meaningful scale differences in the setting and check that tall entities behave intuitively around terrain.

[Supporting discussion, 31:30–32:31](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=mX0cDWlOTuM&t=1890s)

### Template movement and tactical uncertainty

Preselected movement templates can shift the key decision from geometric execution to prediction and bluff. X-Wing's concealed dials hide planned maneuvers; after revealing movement, a limited action choice creates a small window to respond to partial game-state information.

**Tradeoff to consider:** This reduces measurement detail and makes prediction central, while restricting players to preplanned movement options.

**Design prompt:** When using templates or committed orders, make the hidden commitment and the later response window the intended source of movement tension.

[Supporting discussion, 17:41–18:44](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=mX0cDWlOTuM&t=1061s)

**Episode-specific limits**

- The captions include post-closing material and isolated transcription errors; findings focus on the substantive movement discussion.
- Line of sight is announced as a later topic in the episode, but this review covers only movement-related material.

## Designing Line of Sight in Tabletop Miniatures Wargames

**Source:** [Designing Line of Sight in Tabletop Miniatures Wargames](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=yTltjVX1SJg) · **Video ID:** `yTltjVX1SJg`

True and abstract line of sight, cover, terrain categories, and complexity choices.

### True line of sight and miniature modeling

True line of sight can make a model's decorative pose or silhouette a tactical liability. Players judge visibility from the model's apparent viewpoint and visible body; banners, flailing limbs, or elaborate hair can become targetable or expose the miniature.

**Tradeoff to consider:** It is intuitive to write and can suit three-dimensional tables, but punishes modeling choices and creates disputes about what counts as the body.

**Design prompt:** If using true LOS, decide whether model customization should affect gameplay and specify visible-target thresholds clearly.

[Supporting discussion, 3:47–4:49](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=yTltjVX1SJg&t=227s)

### True LOS is socially negotiated

Physical sightline judgments depend on perspective and agreement, so rules need more precision than simply asking what a player can see. Players cannot occupy a model's exact eye position, and viewing angle, two-eye perspective, and table height can yield different conclusions.

**Tradeoff to consider:** A cooperative pair can resolve ambiguities quickly, but a ruleset that relies on goodwill may produce arguments between other players.

**Design prompt:** Test LOS wording with players who do not share assumptions; resolve perspective disputes procedurally rather than leaving them to table consensus.

[Supporting discussion, 8:04–9:09](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=yTltjVX1SJg&t=484s)

### Cover thresholds under true LOS

Percentage-based cover rules are difficult to apply consistently to irregular miniatures and viewpoints. Judging whether 25%, 50%, or another fraction is obscured depends on angle and whether limbs, gear, or the whole model define the measured area.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Granular cover bands promise precision but can force players to invent a ruling for each sightline.

**Design prompt:** Avoid numerical visibility thresholds unless the game supplies a repeatable measurement method and clearly defines the model silhouette being measured.

[Supporting discussion, 10:20–11:22](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=yTltjVX1SJg&t=620s)

### Abstract line of sight

Abstract LOS replaces eyeballing the model with geometric tests between bases and defined terrain. Players draw a line between base shapes and apply terrain categories, such as blocking or obscuring, to determine visibility.

**Tradeoff to consider:** This reduces perspective arguments, but requires terrain definitions and can feel like a tactical overlay rather than direct visual judgment.

**Design prompt:** Use abstract LOS when consistent adjudication is a priority and the game can support clear base and terrain definitions.

[Supporting discussion, 5:58–7:02](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=yTltjVX1SJg&t=358s)

### Terrain categories for sight and cover

A small reusable vocabulary can cover movement, visibility, and protection without designing every obstacle from scratch. The discussion distinguishes blocking, clear, and partially visible terrain, then separates whether a target can be seen from whether terrain reduces damage or accuracy.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Standard categories save explanation, while game-specific cover matters when shooting through different materials is central to play.

**Design prompt:** Write generic terrain categories once and customize them only when the game's tactical focus makes distinctions like hedge versus wall meaningful.

[Supporting discussion, 13:08–14:12](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=yTltjVX1SJg&t=788s)

### Cover may be unnecessary

A game does not need cover modifiers simply because it includes shooting and terrain. Gaslands includes an optional-feeling minimal cover penalty, while Billion Suns omits cover because its fiction and combat already provide other decisions.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Removing cover saves modifiers and avoids unintended interactions; it can disappoint players if the setting strongly signals that cover should matter.

**Design prompt:** Ask whether cover creates a distinct desired decision, and omit it if other positional systems carry the game's tactical weight.

[Supporting discussion, 14:55–15:59](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=yTltjVX1SJg&t=895s)

### Model motion versus static snapshots

A game's time model affects how intuitive LOS and cover rules feel. Simultaneous vehicle motion can make an apparent snapshot misleading because both participants are moving and later movement events have not resolved yet.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Snapshot-based rules are easier to adjudicate; fluid motion can support cinematic action but makes static cover assumptions less natural.

**Design prompt:** Choose whether attacks resolve against static positions or during movement, and align LOS timing and cover rules with that choice.

[Supporting discussion, 16:04–17:08](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=yTltjVX1SJg&t=964s)

### Abstract LOS and vertical terrain

Abstract LOS is straightforward on flat, similarly scaled tables but becomes hard to teach and remember when levels and varied heights matter. Ledges, windows, roofs, and model height require additional rules for where sight lines originate and whether they pass over edges or walls.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Detailed rules can resolve vertical cases consistently, but their complexity may overwhelm players and be forgotten during play.

**Design prompt:** Set the normal table expectation explicitly; use simple abstract LOS for mostly flat play and add vertical rules only when elevation drives the experience.

[Supporting discussion, 26:44–27:48](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=yTltjVX1SJg&t=1604s)

### Terrain scale and LOS abstraction

LOS abstraction should reflect the scale and relative size of units in the game. A single 'obscuring' classification may work for similarly sized figures, but fails when a small wall blocks infantry and is negligible to a giant machine.

**Tradeoff to consider:** One common category simplifies interaction, while scale-specific categories better represent heterogeneous units at additional rules cost.

**Design prompt:** Check terrain classifications against the smallest and largest entities players can field before adopting a universal LOS rule.

[Supporting discussion, 26:09–27:10](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=yTltjVX1SJg&t=1569s)

### Complexity budget for LOS

The right LOS system depends on how much complexity the core experience can afford. Flat tables make clean abstract rules inexpensive; multi-level terrain and highly differentiated units increase the number of edge cases and memory burden.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Precision is valuable when LOS is the main game, but can turn an already demanding tactical game into a slog when added peripherally.

**Design prompt:** Price the rules and player attention required by LOS against the fun it returns; keep it sharp where it defines the game and simplify elsewhere.

[Supporting discussion, 33:38–34:41](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=yTltjVX1SJg&t=2018s)

**Episode-specific limits**

- Captions have isolated transcription and proper-name errors; findings rely on clear passages.
- Speaker opinions about preferred systems are preserved as design judgments, not universal empirical claims.

## Designing Your Combat Phase

**Source:** [Designing Your Combat Phase](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=j5w0IB2qeGk) · **Video ID:** `j5w0IB2qeGk`

Combat timing, ranged and melee procedures, active-player advantage, and participation in resolution.

### Combat phase and turn timing

A boxed combat phase can support morale breaks and cascading unit routs that are difficult to time in alternating activations. If one unit breaks and another has not activated, allowing it to exploit the rout creates sequencing questions; resolving movement before combat creates a shared event window.

**Tradeoff to consider:** A dedicated phase makes multi-unit consequences legible but can constrain activation structures; embedding attacks in activations gives local flow but complicates simultaneous morale effects.

**Design prompt:** Choose combat timing alongside morale and break rules rather than treating phase structure as a cosmetic turn-order choice.

[Supporting discussion, 4:09–5:10](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=j5w0IB2qeGk&t=249s)

### Combat timing alternatives

Combat can be a phase with alternating attacks, an initiative-ordered phase, or an action inside each model's activation. These alternatives let players respond during combat, use stats to order attacks, or take attacks as individual actions rather than resolving every attack together.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Alternation creates preemption choices; activation-level attacks integrate actions but may make combat less simultaneous.

**Design prompt:** Select the timing structure that creates the desired response window and fits the game's activation system.

[Supporting discussion, 8:43–9:45](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=j5w0IB2qeGk&t=523s)

### Weapon range and table scale

Weapon ranges in skirmish games are often compressed to suit table size, which affects weapon balance and the game's atmosphere. If real ranges dominated a dining-table board, movement and weapon range distinctions could become uninteresting; effectively unlimited range creates a different tactical problem.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Compressed ranges make weapons relevant across a small board but sacrifice scale realism; long or unlimited ranges change exposure and positioning.

**Design prompt:** Set ranges against actual board size and movement rates, then assess whether the resulting range bands support varied strategies.

[Supporting discussion, 11:36–12:40](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=j5w0IB2qeGk&t=696s)

### Ranged versus melee effectiveness

If ranged combat is as effective as melee, players may have little incentive to close distance; harder-to-achieve melee can justify greater payoff. Ranged attacks usually have longer reach and more target choice, whereas melee requires approaching and often committing to one target.

**Tradeoff to consider:** A melee premium rewards risk and supports varied strategies, but excessive advantage can make shooting an inferior choice.

**Design prompt:** Compare expected outcomes against the movement and exposure costs of reaching melee, and tune the relative payoff to the intended battlefield.

[Supporting discussion, 13:57–15:00](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=j5w0IB2qeGk&t=837s)

### Multiple resolution steps

Hit, wound, and save rolls are conventions, not natural requirements; each step should earn its time and design value. Combining armor saves into additional hull points simplified Gaslands, while a separate save step offered more tuning space and a familiar procedure.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Fewer rolls speed resolution but remove a point of player agency or design variation; extra rolls may feel familiar yet lengthen combat.

**Design prompt:** Keep a resolution step only when its distinct choice, agency, or balance function outweighs its handling cost.

[Supporting discussion, 16:14–17:18](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=j5w0IB2qeGk&t=974s)

### Defender agency

A defender-facing roll can make being attacked feel less passive, even when it adds another resolution step. The speakers describe removing saves as unpleasant because the attacker rolls and immediately removes the defender's models, leaving the target player no response.

**Tradeoff to consider:** A defensive roll costs time and can reduce attacker control, but gives both players participation in the outcome.

**Design prompt:** Consider whether each side gets meaningful agency during attack resolution, especially when a hit removes a model outright.

[Supporting discussion, 21:23–22:28](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=j5w0IB2qeGk&t=1283s)

### Engagement and disengagement

Engagement range and disengagement rules shape area control and create pressure around models. Committing a model can hold an opponent in place or protect a location; the ability to leave determines whether that commitment persists.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Sticky engagement creates tactical tension, while easy disengagement preserves mobility; either can frustrate if unstated.

**Design prompt:** Define base contact or engagement distance, how combat starts, and what it costs to disengage before writing detailed attack procedures.

[Supporting discussion, 23:14–24:16](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=j5w0IB2qeGk&t=1394s)

### Active-player advantage

A default bonus for charging or acting first is optional; if absent, the game needs a clear alternative source of payoff or explanation. Advantage can emerge from timing, preparation, or system-specific resources rather than a flat charge bonus; in the cited vehicle example, the moving attacker can use skid results unavailable to the stationary target.

**Tradeoff to consider:** A flat bonus is easy to understand but can reward aggression mechanically regardless of context; emergent advantage is subtler and may surprise players.

**Design prompt:** If the active player receives no inherent combat bonus, make the tactical reason legible and show where the advantage comes from in play.

[Supporting discussion, 28:02–29:06](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=j5w0IB2qeGk&t=1682s)

**Episode-specific limits**

- The transcript explicitly says some baseline rules are illustrative rather than authoritative; this review treats examples as examples.
- The promised follow-up on unusual combat systems is outside this episode.

## We finally talk about DICE!

**Source:** [We finally talk about DICE!](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=dv4mal1zLN8) · **Video ID:** `dv4mal1zLN8`

Probability design, rerolls, modifiers, pools, spikes, custom dice, and opposed tests.

### Design probabilities before mechanics

Begin dice design by deciding the chance of success a situation should produce, then choose mechanics that yield that probability. The speakers describe specifying target rates for equal or unequal skill before selecting a die, modifier, or reroll structure.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Probability-first design clarifies intent, but still requires checking how critical results and other mechanics alter the distribution.

**Design prompt:** Record expected success rates for representative cases, then verify them after adding rerolls, modifiers, or critical effects.

[Supporting discussion, 3:56–4:59](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=dv4mal1zLN8&t=236s)

### Die size and available design space

Equal success rates on different dice can behave differently once critical results, fumbles, and rerolls are included. A d6 and d20 can each offer a 50% target, but the frequency of extreme faces differs and changes the frequency of special outcomes.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Small dice are simple but provide fewer gradations; larger dice support finer probabilities but require other mechanics to be recalibrated.

**Design prompt:** Choose die size based on the full resolution system, not only the base chance to succeed.

[Supporting discussion, 5:58–7:00](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=dv4mal1zLN8&t=358s)

### Modifiers and unequal die sizes

A fixed modifier has a larger relative effect on a small die than on a large die. A +1 shifts a d4's possible outcomes more substantially than a d10's, so mixed die sizes can make the same bonus unequal.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Modifiers are easy to explain, but may distort balance across die sizes and can consume a small die's limited outcome range.

**Design prompt:** Avoid applying identical numeric bonuses across different die sizes without checking their relative probability effect.

[Supporting discussion, 8:59–10:02](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=dv4mal1zLN8&t=539s)

### Modifier count and mental arithmetic

Many varied modifiers add lookup and calculation burden, especially when players combine large values. The speakers contrast simple fixed modifiers with systems where several plus and minus values must be added during a fast tactical moment.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Modifiers provide tuning granularity, but each additional value asks players to remember or calculate more at the table.

**Design prompt:** Limit modifier variety and test the arithmetic in a live turn; consider rerolls or advantage structures if they express the same distinction more simply.

[Supporting discussion, 13:49–14:54](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=dv4mal1zLN8&t=829s)

### Dice pools and probability curves

Dice pools permit different aggregation rules and special spike results; evaluate the exact operation rather than assuming all pools share a curve. The episode discusses highest-die-plus-bonuses in 7TV, counted top faces and a destiny die in Carnevale, and success counting elsewhere. Mathematical clarification: sums, success counts and maxima have different distributions.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Pool rules can stabilize some outcomes or add rare spikes; the claim that dice pools universally flatten probability is too broad.

**Design prompt:** Specify whether dice are summed, counted, or compared by highest result before evaluating probabilities.

[Supporting discussion, 21:47–22:54](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=dv4mal1zLN8&t=1307s)

### Dice pool size and legibility

Choose a pool size that remains easy to read and handle; the speakers prefer a moderate number of visually countable successes. They contrast single-roll arithmetic with sorting large handfuls and object to large d20 pools on statistical and practical grounds.

**Tradeoff to consider:** More dice can reduce relative variability for some operations, but the speakers’ roughly twenty-dice requirement is not a mathematical necessity.

**Design prompt:** Test handling and probabilities for the actual aggregation rule; do not import a universal minimum dice count.

[Supporting discussion, 25:54–26:59](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=dv4mal1zLN8&t=1554s)

### Exploding results and bounded spikes

If high faces can generate extra successes, a fixed cap can preserve the dramatic effect while limiting repeat-roll handling. Gaslands' six counts as two successes; some other systems reroll sixes repeatedly, producing potentially unbounded chains and extra physical sorting.

**Tradeoff to consider:** A cap prevents runaway resolution and speeds play; repeated explosions create greater unpredictability.

**Design prompt:** Prefer a defined maximum when an unlimited chain adds little thematic value beyond the first spike.

[Supporting discussion, 29:31–30:36](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=dv4mal1zLN8&t=1771s)

### Criticals and differentiation

Exploding successes can widen a small die's ability to distinguish elite and ordinary units, while automatic hit or miss faces may shrink it. A bonus can make elites more likely both to hit and to trigger an exploding result; automatic results remove faces from the normal range.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Critical mechanics add dramatic separation but can amplify already strong units; automatic results simplify outcomes while reducing granularity.

**Design prompt:** Check how critical faces interact with skill bonuses and the remaining die range before layering them onto a small die.

[Supporting discussion, 29:59–31:03](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=dv4mal1zLN8&t=1799s)

### Custom dice as experience design

Custom dice can improve readability and thematic feel, but are most distinctive when their physical structure does real mechanical work. Symbol faces can turn addition into counting; colors can encode strength; coordinated symbols can explain movement templates; non-transitive dice can encode cyclic matchups without a lookup.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Custom dice reduce mental arithmetic and can be tactile, but require extra components and may constrain the design space.

**Design prompt:** Use custom dice when their symbols, colors, or face relationships improve play or enable mechanics that ordinary dice cannot express as cleanly.

[Supporting discussion, 38:08–39:13](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=dv4mal1zLN8&t=2288s)

### Opposed rolls

The speakers question bare opposed rolls that only identify a winner and prefer opposed systems where stats or comparison tables shape the matchup. They discuss lookup tables preserving hit chances for inferior stats rather than letting raw die-plus-stat differences make a matchup impossible.

**Tradeoff to consider:** A second roll adds handling but may provide valued participation or result information. Equal dice are a 50/50 contest only with a symmetric tie rule; defender-wins ties are not a coin flip.

**Design prompt:** Specify tie resolution and whether margin or defender participation matters before replacing opposed rolls with one roll.

[Supporting discussion, 42:59–44:06](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=dv4mal1zLN8&t=2579s)

**Episode-specific limits**

- Transcript covers a broad introductory survey, not a complete catalogue of dice systems; opinions on custom dice are personal design judgments.
- Auto-captions garble technical names in places; uncertain examples are described generically.

## Activation Systems in Miniature Games

**Source:** [Activation Systems in Miniature Games](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=kW6isAJlFng) · **Video ID:** `kW6isAJlFng`

Whole-force turns, alternating activations, random pulls and hybrid planning

### Activation families

Treat whole-force turns, alternating activations and random pulls as different starting points with adjustable parts. A player may act with an army, a unit, a detachment or whatever a token/card permits.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Initiative, activation quantity and action restrictions interact even if the rulebook separates them.

**Design prompt:** Specify who acts, with what, how much and what ends the opportunity.

[Supporting discussion, 0:40–5:45](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=kW6isAJlFng&t=40s)

### First-player advantage

Whole-force turns can make the first activation especially decisive when deployment and weapon reach allow substantial immediate damage. Removing enemy units before they act reduces the reply.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Alternating activations can still permit a concentrated alpha strike; they do not automatically remove the problem.

**Design prompt:** Test engagement range against setup distance and potential first-turn losses.

[Supporting discussion, 5:50–12:00](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=kW6isAJlFng&t=350s)

### Delayed damage

Delayed casualty resolution is one way to preserve a force's opportunity to act. The hosts cite Apocalypse assigning damage during the round and resolving it after both forces have acted.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Other alternatives include reducing early reach, limiting the first player's actions or making initiative a paid tactical choice.

**Design prompt:** Compare several remedies for the particular first-turn problem.

[Supporting discussion, 8:40–11:30](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=kW6isAJlFng&t=520s)

### Inactive-player engagement

Long army turns need a reason for the other player to remain engaged. Reactions, planning and alternating whole phases provide different forms of involvement.

**Tradeoff to consider:** A deliberately pure strategic game may rely on thinking during the opponent's turn; that will not suit every desired experience.

**Design prompt:** Measure gaps between meaningful decisions, not merely total play time.

[Supporting discussion, 11:50–15:10](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=kW6isAJlFng&t=710s)

### Handover cost

Alternating activations can reduce the wait to act while increasing repeated reassessment. Every changed board state asks the next player to reconstruct a plan.

**Tradeoff to consider:** The extra decisions can be worth their cost, but should not be confused with automatic speed or engagement.

**Design prompt:** Observe handover time and make planning or upcoming activation order easier to understand.

[Supporting discussion, 17:00–21:20](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=kW6isAJlFng&t=1020s)

### Random activation

Random pulls add uncertainty about who or what acts, including occasional severe streaks. A shared bag, reshuffled tokens or a deck can produce different distributions of opportunities.

**Tradeoff to consider:** A rare event still affects real players when enough games are played.

**Design prompt:** Test extreme sequences and define replacement, exhaustion and turn-ending behavior.

[Supporting discussion, 21:10–25:10](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=kW6isAJlFng&t=1270s)

### Separating randomness

Randomizing the unit and randomizing the player are separate choices. Separate player pools can guarantee alternating players while leaving the identity of the active unit uncertain.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Each solution removes one uncertainty and can create another planning problem.

**Design prompt:** Choose which uncertainty supports the intended fiction and decisions.

[Supporting discussion, 23:30–25:20](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=kW6isAJlFng&t=1410s)

### Simultaneous planning

Separate large decisions from sequential physical resolution when that reduces downtime. X-Wing's players plan on a stable board, then resolve movement and retain smaller reactive choices.

**Tradeoff to consider:** This changes the available information and depends on constraints that make plans executable.

**Design prompt:** Consider planned orders or numbered activation tokens rather than requiring every decision at every handover.

[Supporting discussion, 25:10–32:55](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=kW6isAJlFng&t=1510s)

### Multipurpose cards

An activation resource can encode several related choices. A card's value can set initiative or attack strength while its suit grants an order or bonus.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Additional dimensions should justify their cognitive and handling cost.

**Design prompt:** Explore whether one component can carry connected decisions instead of adding separate procedures.

[Supporting discussion, 31:40–34:00](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=kW6isAJlFng&t=1900s)

### Timing abstraction

Both army turns and alternating activations abstract simultaneous action. Mike finds a motionless army during an opposing turn odd; Glenn finds one unit completing a lifetime of action before its neighbor moves odd.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Neither family is automatically more realistic.

**Design prompt:** Choose the abstraction that supports the experience, then test the resulting decisions and pacing.

[Supporting discussion, 14:40–17:15](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=kW6isAJlFng&t=880s)

**Differences in approach or unresolved questions**

- The hosts experience the timing abstraction of army turns and alternating activations differently; neither is presented as inherently realistic.
- An initial suggestion to include Infinity among pull systems is qualified: its flexible order pool is not the same as a random draw.

**Episode-specific limits**

- The episode explores design families and examples, not full or current rules for the games mentioned.

## 10 greatest powers hidden within normal cards.

**Source:** [10 greatest powers hidden within normal cards.](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=NuOvHuHJ-f4) · **Video ID:** `NuOvHuHJ-f4`

Affordances of standard playing cards as randomizers, resources, hidden information, physical markers, and thematic objects.

### Cards carry multiple signals

A single card can provide a number plus suit, color, parity, and rank, allowing one draw to resolve several linked questions. Malifaux is cited as using card suits as triggers that add different effects to a successful test; dice often need a separate roll or table for such riders.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Cards offer dense information, but players must learn what each signal means and how to map face cards or jokers.

**Design prompt:** Use card features when the game benefits from multi-part outcomes; define rank and suit conventions consistently.

[Supporting discussion, 6:34–7:37](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=NuOvHuHJ-f4&t=394s)

### Cards and accessibility

Standard cards are widely owned, but miniature gamers may be more likely to carry multiple dice than a deck. A shared deck can serve all players, whereas requiring each player to bring a deck changes the practical access burden.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Ubiquity favors standard cards; game-night expectations and shared-deck logistics may favor dice.

**Design prompt:** Specify whether players share one deck or need individual decks, and consider whether the component is likely to be available at a club.

[Supporting discussion, 8:15–9:19](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=NuOvHuHJ-f4&t=495s)

### Card decks have memory

Unlike dice, a deck preserves information about which results have already appeared, enabling card counting and pacing control. Players may track seen cards, discard piles, or cards still in the deck; designers can stack or build decks to control when events appear.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Memory enables suspense and planned pacing, but may reward card-counting skill and slow play if players repeatedly inspect discards.

**Design prompt:** Decide whether discard piles are public and whether card memory is intended to matter; use prepared decks when event timing should be shaped.

[Supporting discussion, 10:33–11:35](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=NuOvHuHJ-f4&t=633s)

### Cards as a hidden resource

Hands let players hold, conceal, play, exchange, or spend results in ways that are awkward with dice. A card can remain face down while its owner manipulates a hand or plays a selected value; played cards can cycle through a discard and return system.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Hands create strategic choice and bluffing, but add rules for draw, hand limits, recycling, and who owns each card.

**Design prompt:** Use cards as resources when choice and secrecy are central, and define draw timing, exhaustion, reshuffling, and ownership.

[Supporting discussion, 12:41–13:46](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=NuOvHuHJ-f4&t=761s)

### Limited and exclusive cards

A shared deck creates scarcity because one player's draw removes that card from others' immediate possibilities. Cards can be one-use resources, recycle after discard, remain exclusive to a player, or be shared across the table.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Scarcity supports timing and contention, but can create uneven access or make players feel denied a needed result.

**Design prompt:** Choose shared or personal decks based on whether competition for specific results should be part of play.

[Supporting discussion, 14:47–15:50](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=NuOvHuHJ-f4&t=887s)

### Hidden choices and bluffing

Face-down cards create genuine hidden commitments and a reveal moment, making bluffing more natural than concealing dice. Players can choose a card in advance, conceal it, and force an opponent to infer its identity before reveal.

**Tradeoff to consider:** This can create social tension and meaningful prediction, but requires careful wording around random selection, shuffling, and information exposure.

**Design prompt:** Use concealed cards when player choice and inference are intended; define whether hands may be viewed, shuffled, or manipulated.

[Supporting discussion, 17:34–18:37](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=NuOvHuHJ-f4&t=1054s)

### Cards as physical board elements

Cards can serve as markers, templates, and scenario spaces as well as randomizers. Their edges can measure or define areas; placed face down, they can mark objectives, deployment areas, or unknown locations that are later revealed.

**Tradeoff to consider:** The standard component is inexpensive and versatile, but card dimensions and placement may limit board scale or precision.

**Design prompt:** Explore card footprints for quick scenario layouts and status markers before commissioning bespoke templates or tokens.

[Supporting discussion, 23:08–24:13](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=NuOvHuHJ-f4&t=1388s)

### Trigger cards for pacing

A specific card buried in a deck can make an event increasingly likely to occur without needing a growing modifier track. Finding a designated card in a known draw sequence creates a trigger with a predictable remaining-deck horizon, unlike repeatedly adding a bonus after failed rolls.

**Tradeoff to consider:** The event is guaranteed if the deck is exhausted, but players may still fail to reach it if the game ends early.

**Design prompt:** Use a trigger card when an event should happen eventually but its exact timing should remain uncertain; set deck size to control expected pacing.

[Supporting discussion, 25:07–26:13](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=NuOvHuHJ-f4&t=1507s)

### Theme and standard versus custom decks

Standard suits can carry cultural associations, while obscure card systems may require a custom deck without providing more useful mechanics. Western and gambling imagery can support western or occult themes; alternative suits or arcana may add flavor but require mappings or less familiar components.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Familiar decks are accessible and legible; custom decks offer stronger thematic hooks at production and learning cost.

**Design prompt:** Use a standard deck unless a custom deck's unique symbolism or face structure provides clear thematic or mechanical value.

[Supporting discussion, 28:37–29:41](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=NuOvHuHJ-f4&t=1717s)

**Episode-specific limits**

- The episode is explicitly an affordance survey and the speakers defer some custom-deck issues to a follow-up.
- Cultural associations are presented as design impressions, not universal player responses.

## How damage REALLY works in your favourite games!

**Source:** [How damage REALLY works in your favourite games!](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=WFrPm84xZPU) · **Video ID:** `WFrPm84xZPU`

Damage tracking, degradation, repair, critical effects, and the role of damage in game pacing.

### Damage track granularity

The number of damage states should match how many meaningful elements a player can manage, not the number of miniatures alone. The speakers note that a unit of many figures can still be one decision-making element, while a small number of vehicles can support detailed component damage.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Component tracking enables local effects but increases state-management burden; simple casualty removal is fast and self-recording.

**Design prompt:** Estimate the number of active elements and player attention before adding per-model or per-component damage tracks.

[Supporting discussion, 3:50–4:51](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=WFrPm84xZPU&t=230s)

### Models as damage records

Removing casualties or changing miniature posture can track damage without a separate bookkeeping system. A unit loses a model and its remaining count shows wounds and reduced capability; a fallen model can signal a changed state.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Physical state is intuitive and low overhead, but does not represent detailed component loss or easy recovery.

**Design prompt:** Use the miniatures themselves as the damage interface where the number of figures and wound structure make that legible.

[Supporting discussion, 5:00–6:04](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=WFrPm84xZPU&t=300s)

### Damage state interface

A game can provide a dedicated damage tracker or let players choose their own counters, but should recognize the usability tradeoff. The examples range from dials, cubes, and tokens to printed damage boxes and stat tables that change by remaining wounds.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Dedicated trackers standardize state but add components; open-ended tracking accommodates player preferences but risks inconsistent or cumbersome recording.

**Design prompt:** Select a damage interface that is readable at the table and consistent with the game's desired granularity.

[Supporting discussion, 8:10–9:14](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=WFrPm84xZPU&t=490s)

### Simulation versus playability

Damage modeling requires choosing how much realism to simulate and how much speed and interesting play to preserve. A realistic injury may remove a fighter immediately, while a skirmish game may instead want several hits and a changed behavior state.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Fine simulation can feel vivid but creates detailed resolution; abstract results preserve pacing but sacrifice physical accuracy.

**Design prompt:** Decide where simulation matters to the game's fiction and avoid modeling detail that does not create useful decisions.

[Supporting discussion, 10:03–11:05](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=WFrPm84xZPU&t=603s)

### Degradation and snowballing

Progressively weakening a damaged unit can remove the player's most interesting options and make the end of a game less dramatic. In the cited spaceship design, damage first reduced performance; a later design instead kept combat capability intact and used damage to increase the chance of a catastrophic end.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Degradation communicates physical damage, but can turn a surviving unit into a disappointing burden; end-accelerating damage preserves agency until destruction.

**Design prompt:** Test whether damage changes create new choices or simply make a unit less capable; consider separating endgame risk from current action capacity.

[Supporting discussion, 14:05–15:08](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=WFrPm84xZPU&t=845s)

### Degraded activations need payoff

If damage leaves a model alive but with little useful action, the rules need a compelling cinematic or tactical reason to keep activating it. A slowed or crawling figure can create a meaningful moment if moving toward safety or an objective; otherwise its activation becomes a repeated time sink.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Lingering injury supports narrative detail, but can consume turns without interesting decisions.

**Design prompt:** For each degradation state, identify what new purpose it serves; remove the model or simplify its state when no useful choice remains.

[Supporting discussion, 17:03–18:06](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=WFrPm84xZPU&t=1023s)

### Repair and healing

Repair is valuable when it creates a real tactical choice, but routine restoration can lengthen play without adding decisions. A limited healing spell competes with other actions, while a broad repair loop can reset damage and undo prior resolution; partial repair with permanent losses can create a desperate final activation.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Healing provides agency and recovery, but full resets may slow the game's progression toward its endpoint.

**Design prompt:** Make repair scarce, partial, or costly in meaningful actions if its timing should matter; reconsider it if it merely reverses damage.

[Supporting discussion, 22:23–23:27](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=WFrPm84xZPU&t=1343s)

### Critical damage tables

Critical hit tables can create memorable effects, but they interrupt play with lookup and often cause further degradation. A special roll sends players to a chart that specifies a component loss or penalty; the speakers disagree on whether the fun of that roll can outweigh its procedural cost.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Tables offer bespoke cinematic consequences but add book consultation and rules states; some players enjoy their unpredictability despite those costs.

**Design prompt:** Use a critical table only when its results are vivid enough to justify the lookup and when players value that kind of narrative swing.

[Supporting discussion, 24:43–25:48](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=WFrPm84xZPU&t=1483s)

### Damage may not be necessary

A game can create pressure and change board state without tracking wounds or removing pieces. The speakers suggest displacement or objective effects as alternatives when the game's purpose is not casualty removal.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Omitting damage removes familiar combat feedback, but can focus play on objectives and movement instead of attrition.

**Design prompt:** Ask whether injury is essential to the intended game loop before building damage and removal systems.

[Supporting discussion, 27:36–28:41](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=WFrPm84xZPU&t=1656s)

**Differences in approach or unresolved questions**

- One speaker dislikes critical-hit tables as a design mechanism while the other enjoys some examples as a player; both accept that they may fit particular games.

**Episode-specific limits**

- Damage examples are drawn from games the speakers discuss and are not claims of universal player preference.
- The transcript ends before a deeper general discussion of granularity promised for another episode.

## The exact correct length for your game, THE TRUTH!

**Source:** [The exact correct length for your game, THE TRUTH!](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=UUgKFJ1ru7k) · **Video ID:** `UUgKFJ1ru7k`

Game duration, round counts, pacing, player energy, and how explicit formats shape expectations.

### Rounds are not a universal unit

There is no correct number of rounds independent of what happens in each round and how long the loop takes. A three-turn game can cover a complete narrative arc if each turn carries enough activity; many short rounds can cover the same play time as fewer long rounds.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Round counts make progress easy to communicate, but become misleading when treated as a universal length standard.

**Design prompt:** Choose round count based on the game's core questions and how long each loop takes, not inherited genre expectations.

[Supporting discussion, 31:07–32:11](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=UUgKFJ1ru7k&t=1867s)

### Advertised duration and sustained fun

A stated play time is useful when actual play roughly matches it and remains engaging throughout. Duration is driven by rules complexity, granularity, setup, and the time needed to resolve subsystems; an inaccurate estimate creates expectation mismatch.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Long games can support depth, while shorter designs may cut detail; either works if the experience remains worthwhile.

**Design prompt:** Estimate real table time and assess engagement across the whole session rather than pursuing a duration as a goal in itself.

[Supporting discussion, 2:33–3:37](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=UUgKFJ1ru7k&t=153s)

### Turns and narrative acts

Round structure can support a deliberate story arc, but it should not force a particular number of turns. The Billion Suns example uses arrival, contest, and departure as three acts; its turns could be subdivided without changing the intended total experience.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Acts give the game a clear beginning, middle, and end; adding turns without purpose can extend resolution after the story has concluded.

**Design prompt:** Map scenario events to setup, contest, and resolution, then let the turn count serve those beats.

[Supporting discussion, 9:27–10:30](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=UUgKFJ1ru7k&t=567s)

### End games decisively

A game should avoid a slow tail after meaningful objectives and decisions have ended. The earlier open-ended version allowed ships to remain after there was little purpose for them; the revised structure ends when the dramatic arc is complete.

**Tradeoff to consider:** An early endpoint can feel abrupt if players need more time to use units, but lingering cleanup can make the finale anticlimactic.

**Design prompt:** Identify when the central contest is resolved and end play near that point rather than requiring empty-board cleanup.

[Supporting discussion, 9:11–10:15](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=UUgKFJ1ru7k&t=551s)

### Give units enough opportunity

A short game can disappoint if pieces never get to perform the role that made them appealing. Players may perceive too few turns as insufficient activation time, though special resources can allow multiple actions and make a shorter round count feel active.

**Tradeoff to consider:** More turns give units room to operate but extend the session; concentrated activations can preserve activity within fewer rounds.

**Design prompt:** Check how often each unit can take its signature action before the game ends, including bonus or double activations.

[Supporting discussion, 11:42–12:47](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=UUgKFJ1ru7k&t=702s)

### Match game length to cognitive intensity

A mentally demanding game has a practical limit on how long players can sustain its intensity. The speakers describe a highly punishing game where early mistakes are hard to repair; extending that decision pressure can exhaust players.

**Tradeoff to consider:** An intense experience can be satisfying, but its duration should not overwhelm the players' attention.

**Design prompt:** Test the length of demanding games with real players and adjust duration or recovery opportunities if the pressure becomes unpleasant.

[Supporting discussion, 14:17–15:21](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=UUgKFJ1ru7k&t=857s)

### Rounds as progress indicators

A fixed round count gives players a time check and helps them judge when to pursue objectives or take risks. Knowing a game has five rounds lets players infer that round three is midgame and plan remaining actions before the endpoint.

**Tradeoff to consider:** A predictable schedule supports planning; unpredictable round duration or changing pace can undermine those expectations.

**Design prompt:** Keep round pacing stable enough that players can use the round number to judge remaining opportunity.

[Supporting discussion, 19:51–20:58](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=UUgKFJ1ru7k&t=1191s)

### Communicate variable duration

A game should not unpredictably expand its session without giving players a way to anticipate or control that change. The speakers compare games whose session length varies due to emergent events with formats where a player knowingly chooses a larger force or longer setup.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Variable arcs can create emergent stories, but make scheduling harder; adjustable game size preserves agency when the longer session is an explicit choice.

**Design prompt:** Tell players how duration changes with force size or optional rules, and avoid hidden levers that multiply play time.

[Supporting discussion, 22:32–23:37](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=UUgKFJ1ru7k&t=1352s)

### Offer honest play formats

A game can support alternate sizes or durations, but should be clear about the format that best delivers its intended experience. Smaller games can work as compact variants, while larger formats add time; players may also pause and resume if the board state can be preserved.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Multiple formats improve flexibility but increase design and teaching demands; one recommended format gives a clearer promise.

**Design prompt:** State the intended player count, force size, and session duration, then identify variants as deliberate options with expected effects.

[Supporting discussion, 29:03–30:09](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=UUgKFJ1ru7k&t=1743s)

**Episode-specific limits**

- Anecdotes about specific products and personal scheduling preferences are not empirical playtime studies.
- Auto-captions include occasional recognition errors; design points are taken from clear portions.

## How to Design an Interesting Combat Phase

**Source:** [How to Design an Interesting Combat Phase](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=3NBBDuyhrVU) · **Video ID:** `3NBBDuyhrVU`

Tactical depth around combat, threat identification, movement links, resolution options, and thematic design.

### Functional versus distinctive combat

A combat system can be functional without being distinctive; memorable resolution should serve the game's subject and tactical choices. The speakers distinguish basic hit-and-damage procedures from systems that make players care about where and when combat happens.

**Tradeoff to consider:** A standard procedure is reliable and familiar; added novelty can become complexity without supporting the game's focus.

**Design prompt:** First make combat work, then add mechanics that express the genre or create a decision the core game needs.

[Supporting discussion, 4:11–5:13](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=3NBBDuyhrVU&t=251s)

### Rock-paper-scissors matchups

Asymmetric strengths and weaknesses create tactical puzzles before and during combat. When one unit is effective against one opponent but vulnerable to another, players must position, screen, and select matchups rather than simply compare size.

**Tradeoff to consider:** A matchup web is quick and legible, but may make movement and deployment more important than the resolution roll itself.

**Design prompt:** Use a small, understandable set of counter-relations and ensure players can identify them before committing units.

[Supporting discussion, 6:10–7:16](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=3NBBDuyhrVU&t=370s)

### Combat as payoff to prior decisions

Combat can be deliberately simple when deployment and movement have already created the consequential question. The game asks where units should meet and which target matters; a single resolution can answer that accumulated setup without a complex combat subsystem.

**Tradeoff to consider:** A concise fight keeps focus on the wider tactical puzzle, but offers less detail to games centered on the clash itself.

**Design prompt:** Trace what question each combat roll answers and avoid adding resolution complexity when the interest is already in setup and positioning.

[Supporting discussion, 7:58–9:01](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=3NBBDuyhrVU&t=478s)

### Threat identification

Combat becomes more interesting when players must identify which enemy poses the greatest threat, including units that score objectives rather than deal damage. Scenario value can make a weak objective specialist a more urgent target than a heavily armed attacker.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Layered threats reward prioritization, but can be hard to read if unit roles and scenario goals are opaque.

**Design prompt:** Make battlefield roles and objective consequences visible so target selection requires judgment rather than guesswork.

[Supporting discussion, 11:41–12:48](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=3NBBDuyhrVU&t=701s)

### Threat ranges shape space

The overlap of weapon ranges and control areas creates invisible lanes and pressure points that make movement choices matter. Threat zones constrain access and make a route risky; players weigh crossing fire lanes against reaching objectives or other valuable positions.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Spatial pressure can make a simple attack roll tense, but overly dense threat coverage may make movement feel impossible.

**Design prompt:** Map threat ranges around objectives and routes, then ensure the board offers meaningful risk choices rather than only blocked paths.

[Supporting discussion, 13:19–14:21](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=3NBBDuyhrVU&t=799s)

### Movement and attack synergy

A few links between movement direction and combat outcomes can deepen both systems at once. Approach lanes, flank or rear attacks, and double engagement can make position change accuracy or effectiveness and give players reasons to maneuver.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Position-sensitive outcomes reward planning, but may add modifiers or create disputes about approach direction.

**Design prompt:** Add directional bonuses only where they reinforce a clear tactical relationship between movement and attack.

[Supporting discussion, 15:14–16:16](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=3NBBDuyhrVU&t=914s)

### Resolution system choices

Combat can resolve through target numbers, stat comparisons, dice pools, opposed rolls, cards, or combinations of these. Each format exposes different information: pools provide result ranges, opposed rolls compare both players, and cards can carry extra signals or choices.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Mechanical variety supports different experiences but brings component, probability, and explanation costs.

**Design prompt:** Choose the randomizer and comparison method that fit the desired uncertainty, granularity, and player interaction.

[Supporting discussion, 17:53–18:57](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=3NBBDuyhrVU&t=1073s)

### Player control over combat outcomes

Resources that let either player modify or preserve results can add agency to otherwise passive resolution. Rerolls, stored results, or spending a resource to alter an opponent's roll create a control layer that can supplement or replace a defender save.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Control counters the feel of one player simply removing pieces, but adds resource tracking and timing decisions.

**Design prompt:** If attacks feel one-sided, test defender-facing control such as a limited reaction or result modification before adding another full defense roll.

[Supporting discussion, 23:00–24:04](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=3NBBDuyhrVU&t=1380s)

### Thematic combat rewards

Rules for cinematic actions should make the intended cool choice genuinely worthwhile. In the shipboard example, tripping and positioning an opponent before a turn of the ship can send them overboard; the action is valuable because it interacts with the environment.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Thematic options create emergent stories, but if they are riskier and weaker than a basic attack, players will ignore them.

**Design prompt:** Compare expected payoff of signature actions with the obvious efficient attack so the game rewards the behavior it promises.

[Supporting discussion, 26:24–27:30](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=3NBBDuyhrVU&t=1584s)

**Episode-specific limits**

- The episode references several game systems and experimental designs; examples show possibilities rather than rules to copy.
- Some names are garbled in captions and were omitted where uncertain.

## Designing Better Deployment Rules

**Source:** [Designing Better Deployment Rules](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=aUfb5j3u1ps) · **Video ID:** `aUfb5j3u1ps`

Alternative deployment structures, open table geometry, scenario design, and player learning.

### Deployment can establish the game's central story

A deployment rule can be chosen to create a specific opening event rather than merely position forces fairly. Gaslands starts cars together to create chaotic first-corner action; the setup began from the desired racing narrative.

**Tradeoff to consider:** A strong opening moment gives the game identity, but may create first-game collisions that some players find frustrating.

**Design prompt:** Start from the scene or decision the game should open with, then design deployment to make that happen.

[Supporting discussion, 2:43–3:49](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=aUfb5j3u1ps&t=163s)

### Center-based deployment is table-flexible

Measuring from the table center can make scenarios less dependent on table edges and easier to adapt to multiple players. Objectives or spawn points placed by radius around a central point can be organized into arcs for three or four players.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Center-based geometry supports varied table shapes and player counts, but may not suit every force scale or narrative.

**Design prompt:** Use a center anchor when edge-based zones restrict layout flexibility; evaluate it against the size and story of the forces.

[Supporting discussion, 7:14–8:18](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=aUfb5j3u1ps&t=434s)

### Spawn points as portable zones

A spawn point with a radius is a portable deployment zone that can be placed wherever a scenario needs it. Unlike fixed table edges or corners, a point plus distance can support central, asymmetric, or multi-player arrivals.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Portable zones allow scenario variety, but their effects depend on placement and distance choices that must be tested.

**Design prompt:** Treat deployment zones as scenario tools that can move with objectives or narrative entry points.

[Supporting discussion, 7:57–9:03](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=aUfb5j3u1ps&t=477s)

### Learn from opening chaos

A dramatic deployment can intentionally permit players to make a costly mistake, but designers should judge how much onboarding pain is acceptable. The racing-grid start can lead first-time players into a head-on collision; extra exceptions might prevent it but would burden every player with setup rules.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Freedom supports experimentation and memorable learning; guardrails can reduce early frustration but dilute or complicate the intended experience.

**Design prompt:** Test first-game failure points and add a safeguard only when its broad benefit outweighs the learning and exception cost.

[Supporting discussion, 12:15–13:20](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=aUfb5j3u1ps&t=735s)

### Fast-forward past empty approach turns

Deployment can begin closer to the action when slow approach turns do not serve the intended experience. Billion Suns places jump points where ships may arrive near each other, effectively skipping the conventional distant setup and moving directly into contested space.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Immediate contact saves setup turns and creates urgency; it removes the traditional approach narrative and can make opening pressure intense.

**Design prompt:** Remove approach distance only when the game has other ways to make the first engagement fair and interesting.

[Supporting discussion, 13:54–14:58](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=aUfb5j3u1ps&t=834s)

### Let deployment distance create different stories

Different deployment distances can support distinct tactical and narrative modes within one system. Close jump points favor immediate fighter craft brawls, while distant points accommodate units with long minimum ranges and ranged attacks.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Flexible spacing creates expressive scenarios, but close deployment can also make a unit's minimum range a liability.

**Design prompt:** Test both close and distant setups with the full range of unit types and show what kinds of encounters each distance creates.

[Supporting discussion, 17:39–18:42](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=aUfb5j3u1ps&t=1059s)

### Build the game around blind deployment

Blind deployment can create uncertainty and storytelling, but the rest of the game must support its risks. Face-down placements or decoy markers can make players infer what is present before the units are revealed; a system not designed for this may produce unfair matchups.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Hidden setups create suspense and fog of war; unsupported randomness can cause one-sided encounters.

**Design prompt:** If using blind deployment, include mechanics that let players manage uncertainty and reduce the cost of unlucky placement.

[Supporting discussion, 16:38–17:40](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=aUfb5j3u1ps&t=998s)

### Deploy suspicion rather than units

Decoy markers can turn the opening approach into a cat-and-mouse game of information management. Players place blips instead of actual units and may reveal identity by moving quickly, so movement speed itself communicates information.

**Tradeoff to consider:** This creates a fog-of-war puzzle, but adds hidden-state recording and potential ambiguity about marker behavior.

**Design prompt:** Use decoys when detection and inference are central, and ensure revealing information follows clear triggers.

[Supporting discussion, 23:00–24:05](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=aUfb5j3u1ps&t=1380s)

### Respect expectation costs

Breaking familiar deployment conventions asks players to spend attention, so the design should offer a clear payoff. Players may expect a standard number of turns or distance between forces; an alternative can feel like lost play unless the action density or story justifies it.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Novel deployment can create a distinctive experience, but challenges genre expectations and can feel unfair or under-length without explanation.

**Design prompt:** Explain what the unusual setup gives players and ensure the scenario delivers enough meaningful activity to justify the change.

[Supporting discussion, 27:48–28:52](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=aUfb5j3u1ps&t=1668s)

### Use scenarios to test deployment

Writing a scenario for an existing game is a practical way to discover why its default deployment rules exist. Changing zones or arrival timing exposes how the baseline supports balance, pacing, or player control.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Scenario experiments reveal design constraints cheaply, but a one-off scenario may not prove a new default is broadly sound.

**Design prompt:** Prototype alternative deployments in scenarios before committing them as general rules; observe which assumptions they break.

[Supporting discussion, 32:07–33:10](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=aUfb5j3u1ps&t=1927s)

**Episode-specific limits**

- The episode focuses on unusual deployment choices and explicitly defers broader scenario-writing principles.
- The speakers discuss their own designs and lessons learned; these are not general balance guarantees.

## Designing Wargame Scenarios

**Source:** [Designing Wargame Scenarios](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=efVHaNo50MA) · **Video ID:** `efVHaNo50MA`

Solo, combinatorial, narrative, and campaign scenarios; scenario adoption and player expectations.

### Solo play depends on core loop

Whether a game supports satisfying solo scenarios depends on the challenge and pleasure already present in its core loop. A solo design may use an AI opponent or present a set of jobs and constraints; games whose tension relies on another person disrupting plans may have little left when alone.

**Tradeoff to consider:** AI can preserve opposition but requires complex behavior rules; objective-driven scenarios can be simpler but must generate enough pressure.

**Design prompt:** Identify what the second player contributes before designing solo play, then reproduce that function or create a different goal-based challenge.

[Supporting discussion, 12:49–13:51](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=efVHaNo50MA&t=769s)

### Solo scenarios need pressure

A solo objective should remain challenging after basic victory is possible, often through time limits or graded results. Campaign rewards for better performance can motivate players to pursue an A result even when a lower grade counts as a win.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Meta rewards increase stakes and replay motivation, but can make a modest success feel insufficient.

**Design prompt:** Use optional degrees of success or time pressure to sustain effort, and ensure the player still receives a meaningful outcome for partial success.

[Supporting discussion, 9:09–10:13](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=efVHaNo50MA&t=549s)

### Not every game needs solo mode

Solo play is not a universal feature; some games depend on interpersonal competition for their core interest. If the opposing human is the objective or the source of unpredictable interference, replacing them with a long AI procedure may not reproduce the fun.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Solo access broadens availability, but forcing it into an unsuitable game can create tedious automation without satisfying play.

**Design prompt:** Treat solo support as a design fit question, and allow a game to remain multiplayer-only when that better serves its purpose.

[Supporting discussion, 13:10–14:14](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=efVHaNo50MA&t=790s)

### Combinatorial scenarios create adaptation

Combining objectives, setups, or secret missions at the table can prevent players from relying on a single prebuilt list for a known matchup. Players bring broad knowledge of their own and opponents' tools, then improvise against scenario elements selected at game time.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Procedural variety creates freshness and reduces pre-game list specialization, but front-loads choices and rules explanation at setup.

**Design prompt:** Use a scenario matrix when adaptation is part of the intended play, and provide enough structure to keep setup manageable.

[Supporting discussion, 16:12–17:19](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=efVHaNo50MA&t=972s)

### Teach procedural scenarios with an entry point
