A procedural scenario system can be hard for newcomers when it offers no stable default way to learn the game. Players must understand objectives, selection tools, and unit roles before they know what to choose; a basic scenario can give them a first foothold.

**Tradeoff to consider:** A default mode lowers onboarding cost, but can become the only mode players ever try.

**Design prompt:** Provide a clear learning scenario or guided first game before asking players to navigate a broad procedural menu.

[Supporting discussion, 20:53–21:56](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=efVHaNo50MA&t=1253s)

### Narrative does not mean noncompetitive

Narrative scenarios can still be balanced and competitive; the label often refers to asymmetry, preparation, or rule complexity. Preset forces, special rules, or unequal deployment can create a story while players still care about winning.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Narrative scenarios allow unusual experiences but demand trust and preparation that pickup games may not have.

**Design prompt:** Describe whether a scenario is asymmetric, competitive, or preparation-heavy rather than assuming narrative means casual or unbalanced.

[Supporting discussion, 26:46–27:50](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=efVHaNo50MA&t=1606s)

### Narrative scenarios spend player effort

Highly specific scenarios can deliver unusual stories when their extra reading, list-building, and coordination pay off clearly. Players may need to bring designated forces, learn special rules, or arrange a future session; preset historical forces can preserve the scenario's premise.

**Tradeoff to consider:** The extra organization can create a distinctive experience but makes the scenario unsuitable for spontaneous pickup play.

**Design prompt:** Make the required preparation explicit and offer a clear payoff in story or mechanics for the effort requested.

[Supporting discussion, 25:30–26:34](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=efVHaNo50MA&t=1530s)

### Campaign scenarios need persistence and varied outcomes

Campaign scenarios should avoid routine meat grinders and record more than a binary win or loss. Persistent injuries and character progression create consequences; secondary objectives can award clues, loot, or individual achievements that keep the campaign interesting.

**Tradeoff to consider:** More outcome tracks support character stories and reduce reliance on one winner, but require additional recordkeeping and scenario design.

**Design prompt:** Give campaign missions objectives and rewards at multiple levels, and consider injury risk across repeated games.

[Supporting discussion, 37:52–38:55](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=efVHaNo50MA&t=2272s)

### Prevent campaign snowballing

Campaign rewards should not make the loser feel that future play is pointless; branch outcomes can preserve bragging rights without compounding power. The speakers suggest keeping later scenarios broadly even while changing story details, clues, or status based on previous results.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Power rewards make victories consequential, but can create avalanches that end a campaign early; narrative rewards preserve continuity with less balance distortion.

**Design prompt:** Audit campaign rewards for compounding advantage and use information or story rewards when preserving competitive viability matters.

[Supporting discussion, 41:25–42:28](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=efVHaNo50MA&t=2485s)

### Why published scenarios go unused

Players may repeat the default scenario because it is familiar, easy to arrange, and supports trust in pickup games. A known baseline reduces rules study and negotiation with strangers; scenario variety may require a different kind of thinking or a trusted organizer.

**Tradeoff to consider:** The default is socially efficient but can leave substantial published content unexplored.

**Design prompt:** Make alternative scenarios easier to teach and arrange, and consider a guided first variant rather than relying on players to self-select unfamiliar content.

[Supporting discussion, 47:59–49:01](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=efVHaNo50MA&t=2879s)

**Episode-specific limits**

- This episode is unusually long and mixes scenario advice with personal preference and anecdotes; claims about why players avoid scenarios are explicitly hypotheses.
- Campaign examples are discussed at a high level; no complete campaign framework is specified.

## How to Write Wargame Scenarios

**Source:** [How to Write Wargame Scenarios](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=xMzPFNzemr4) · **Video ID:** `xMzPFNzemr4`

Scenario structure and prototyping, victory and end conditions, special rules, and scenario quality.

### Scenarios as a safe design prototype

Scenarios are a low-risk way to explore a game's design space before changing its core mechanics. A scenario is a modular layer over the rules engine; changing setup, objectives, or one special rule can test a new experience without rewriting interlocking unit rules.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Scenario experiments are comparatively safe, but may reveal limits that only broader rules changes can solve.

**Design prompt:** Use a scenario to test a new tactical experience on a familiar ruleset before deciding whether a new engine is necessary.

[Supporting discussion, 5:24–6:30](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=xMzPFNzemr4&t=324s)

### Scenario components

A complete scenario needs clear setup, in-game events, an end condition, and victory conditions. Flavor text and force-selection rules may supplement these core instructions; the setup establishes who and what is present, while end and victory rules determine completion and result.

**Tradeoff to consider:** More components permit rich situations, but missing or ambiguous ones leave players to improvise essential procedures.

**Design prompt:** Audit every scenario for setup, special rules, when play ends, and how the winner is determined.

[Supporting discussion, 6:43–7:49](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=xMzPFNzemr4&t=403s)

### Force selection and preparation cost

Preset force requirements can create a vivid narrative challenge but may make a scenario unusable as a pickup game. A scenario may require specific heroes or an unusual model; players who arrive with ordinary prepared forces cannot immediately use it.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Force selection can inspire a hobby project or campaign arc, but risks invalidating time and points invested in a player's chosen force.

**Design prompt:** Reserve force-selection requirements for scenarios with advance notice, and distinguish them from table-ready core scenarios.

[Supporting discussion, 9:08–10:13](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=xMzPFNzemr4&t=548s)

### Accessible versus hobby challenge scenarios

Some scenarios should work with whatever players bring, while others can deliberately ask them to build or select unusual models. A pickup scenario avoids invalidating an existing list; a scheduled challenge may invite players to create a special vehicle or narrative force.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Accessibility enables spontaneous play, while specific forces provide a bespoke hobby and story payoff.

**Design prompt:** Label the preparation expected and give players enough notice when the scenario requires unusual forces or terrain.

[Supporting discussion, 12:06–13:08](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=xMzPFNzemr4&t=726s)

### Victory conditions define the game

Victory conditions determine what players are trying to achieve and can expose weaknesses in the rest of the design. If the required objective is trivial, tedious, or disconnected from the satisfying actions of the game, the mechanics alone cannot make the scenario engaging.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Strong objectives focus the system, but may require redesigning movement, force roles, or action rules to make pursuit interesting.

**Design prompt:** Write and playtest victory conditions early; verify that the optimal path to victory is also fun to execute.

[Supporting discussion, 17:29–18:32](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=xMzPFNzemr4&t=1049s)

### Avoid kill rewards that double-count

Directly rewarding kills can make objectives secondary because removing the opponent also removes their ability to contest them. A player who kills defenders can then capture or destroy the objective unopposed, receiving both the tactical benefit and explicit kill points.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Kill scoring reinforces combat, but can overemphasize elimination even in scenarios meant to test control or strategy.

**Design prompt:** Check whether kills already provide an indirect advantage before assigning them additional victory value.

[Supporting discussion, 20:56–22:00](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=xMzPFNzemr4&t=1256s)

### Scenario special rules and equal impact

Special scenario rules should be understandable at the table and affect both sides as intended. Terrain events or random tables can favor one force because of speed, range, or unit composition even when written as universal effects; nested tables increase cognitive load.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Scenario rules add novelty, but hidden probabilities and uneven interactions can feel arbitrary or require pre-study.

**Design prompt:** Test special rules against different force types, state their timing clearly, and avoid nested random procedures unless they add meaningful payoff.

[Supporting discussion, 26:37–27:42](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=xMzPFNzemr4&t=1597s)

### Clear scenario end conditions

Align the endpoint with the scenario’s dramatic question and consider how known final turns alter behavior. The speakers describe random turn limits reducing contrived last-turn maneuvers, and objective-linked endings such as Billion Suns contracts paying out.

**Tradeoff to consider:** A fixed endpoint aids planning but can encourage last-turn exploitation; a random endpoint adds uncertainty. Continuing after the central contest ends creates cleanup.

**Design prompt:** Specify an explicit end procedure and choose fixed, random or objective-linked timing according to the behavior the scenario needs.

[Supporting discussion, 29:43–30:48](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=xMzPFNzemr4&t=1783s)

### Scenario variety should test distinct questions

A scenario belongs in a game when it tests a distinct part of the system or offers a meaningfully different strategic question. Race, arena combat, and vote collection each highlight a different game capability; duplicating an existing scenario adds little.

**Tradeoff to consider:** A varied set shows system breadth, but a quota of scenarios creates redundant or weak content.

**Design prompt:** For each scenario, name the mechanic or decision it spotlights; omit it if it repeats another scenario without a new payoff.

[Supporting discussion, 39:39–40:40](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=xMzPFNzemr4&t=2379s)

### Scenario discoverability

Players should be able to understand a scenario at the table without winning through prior study of hidden interactions. Transparent special rules and objectives let both players grasp the challenge after one explanation; prior knowledge otherwise creates an information advantage.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Discoverability supports fair pickup play, while complex scenario rules can enable richer bespoke situations for prepared groups.

**Design prompt:** Explain objectives and unusual rules plainly, and reserve dense scenario interactions for explicitly prearranged games.

[Supporting discussion, 41:50–42:53](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=xMzPFNzemr4&t=2510s)

**Episode-specific limits**

- The transcript explicitly promises a second part on narrative, campaign, and solo scenarios; those subjects are not covered here in depth.
- Suggested scenario counts are jokes and design opinions, not a fixed requirement.

## More Solo mode.

**Source:** [More Solo mode.](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=6hUpMP8SklU) · **Video ID:** `6hUpMP8SklU`

Solo modes for playtesting versus player-facing solo experiences, automated opponents, and objective-driven solo scenarios.

### Solo testing is distinct from solo play

A designer's solo testing aid and a satisfying player-facing solo mode serve different purposes. An opponent procedure may reduce the designer’s cognitive load and trigger rules, while still being tedious or weak as a standalone challenge. It can be deterministic or use randomized choices.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Testing automation speeds iteration; publishing it as a mode raises expectations for engaging play.

**Design prompt:** Label tools according to whether they support development or provide an intended player experience.

[Supporting discussion, 28:45–29:50](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=6hUpMP8SklU&t=1725s)

### Automate predictable opponent goals

Solo testing is easier when opposing factions have distinct, predictable goals that the designer can execute without inventing strategy twice. A sponsor's goals can direct the opponent toward a straight or objective, leaving the tester to perform movement and dice procedures.

**Tradeoff to consider:** This reduces cognitive overhead but does not model intelligent or adaptive play.

**Design prompt:** For solo testing, define a simple opponent priority that reliably activates the rules under examination.

[Supporting discussion, 3:57–5:05](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=6hUpMP8SklU&t=237s)

### Automate state, not every decision

A useful automaton can occupy the space another player would affect without simulating their full reasoning. It can spawn pressure, drain resources, or select among a few actions so the human player must react to changing conditions.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Abstract opposition is easy to run but may not challenge the player as a human opponent would.

**Design prompt:** Represent the opponent's game impact with the smallest procedure that creates the intended pressure.

[Supporting discussion, 21:22–22:27](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=6hUpMP8SklU&t=1282s)

### Solo modes should not be instruction drudgery

A long list of mechanical steps can make solo play feel like operating an automaton rather than making decisions. The player executes every opponent rule and tracks every state change, which is useful for diagnosis but often fails to create an engaging solo challenge.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Detailed instructions improve procedural coverage; abstraction keeps the player's attention on meaningful decisions.

**Design prompt:** Reduce routine AI handling for player-facing modes, and reserve detailed step execution for testing or teaching.

[Supporting discussion, 17:04–18:08](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=6hUpMP8SklU&t=1024s)

### Keep the test rules representative

A solo playtest should leave the designer's side operating under the normal rules, even if the opponent receives limited automated advantages. A scripted rival may cheat or choose unpredictably to force interaction, while the tester still uses the game's ordinary procedures.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Asymmetric automation creates a useful test stimulus, but may distort balance if it changes too many opponent rules.

**Design prompt:** Document which opponent behaviors are abstracted and keep the tested player's procedures faithful to the intended game.

[Supporting discussion, 9:37–10:41](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=6hUpMP8SklU&t=577s)

### Use consistent control opponents

Solo testing is more informative when the opponent follows a stable baseline rather than introducing uncontrolled choices. A predictable faction can provide a control while the designer varies the unit or rules under test; repeated extreme dice outcomes can obscure the tested system's behavior.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Consistency aids comparison but may omit skilled responses or strategic surprises.

**Design prompt:** Use simple opponent behavior as a baseline, then add separate tests for aggressive, defensive, or unusual strategies.

[Supporting discussion, 11:41–12:45](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=6hUpMP8SklU&t=701s)

### Solo opponent design depends on game structure

A simple opponent can work when the game has obvious behaviors or resource pressure, but hidden information and social inference are harder to automate. A brawler list can charge directly, while bluffing or concealed information requires modeling what a person believes and when they call a deception.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Simple scripts are easy to run and may test relevant rules; sophisticated strategic AI can be burdensome and still feel unlike a person.

**Design prompt:** Assess whether the game's challenge comes from mechanical pressure or human inference before deciding how much AI to build.

[Supporting discussion, 11:00–12:04](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=6hUpMP8SklU&t=660s)

### Objective-driven solo scenarios

For skirmish games, a solo mission can replace an opponent with a clock, a task, and environmental obstacles. The player retrieves an object under time pressure while terrain and hazards interfere; the scenario itself supplies the pressure normally created by an enemy.

**Tradeoff to consider:** This offers a focused solo challenge without opponent AI, but changes the experience from direct competition to mission execution.

**Design prompt:** When combat-versus-combat automation is impractical, design a solo objective that creates similar urgency and resource tradeoffs.

[Supporting discussion, 27:22–28:25](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=6hUpMP8SklU&t=1642s)

### Solo modes as design practice

Trying to write a solo scenario for a game one knows can reveal how its rules feel to another player. Designers must express opponent behavior, goals, and timing in terms that another person can follow, exposing hidden assumptions.

**Tradeoff to consider:** The exercise can be instructive even if the final mode is not suitable for publication.

**Design prompt:** Use solo-mode experiments as a learning and testing exercise without assuming every game's audience needs a solo variant.

[Supporting discussion, 29:23–30:26](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=6hUpMP8SklU&t=1763s)

**Episode-specific limits**

- The speakers explicitly distinguish personal preferences and experimental prototypes from validated solo modes.
- Several opponent systems discussed were works in progress and were not reported as tested or successful.

## You won't believe these uses for dice!

**Source:** [You won't believe these uses for dice!](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=7_GvjZD3Dh0) · **Video ID:** `7_GvjZD3Dh0`

Dice as state markers, hidden information, bag pulls, stored resources, spatial orientation, direction, and physical components.

### Components and immersion

Additional tokens can make game state visible, but too many objects around miniatures can clutter the table and weaken visual immersion. Dice beside models provide glanceable state; moving all counters to side cards reduces table clutter but forces players to ask or consult records.

**Tradeoff to consider:** On-table markers aid shared awareness, while off-table tracking preserves a cleaner miniature scene.

**Design prompt:** Choose where state lives based on whether visibility or an uncluttered battlefield matters more to the experience.

[Supporting discussion, 3:29–4:35](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=7_GvjZD3Dh0&t=209s)

### Respect die-face limits

If a die is used as a state marker, its face count constrains how much state can be tracked simply. Tracking more than six wounds with d6s requires multiple dice, which can be confused, displaced, or misread; Gaslands caps hazard tokens at six in the example.

**Tradeoff to consider:** A die-face cap simplifies tracking but restricts the range of values; multiple dice expand range at a handling cost.

**Design prompt:** Match track maxima to the dice players already use, or provide a dashboard when several values exceed a single die's range.

[Supporting discussion, 5:40–6:45](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=7_GvjZD3Dh0&t=340s)

### Track game state with ordinary components

Miniature games can delegate state tracking to ordinary objects, but an explicit interface may improve repeatability. Players can choose dice, cards, or notes for a value; a designed marker system can use stacking or position to encode state.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Flexible tracking reduces required components, while standardized tracking makes shared information easier to interpret.

**Design prompt:** Formalize tracking only when it improves clarity or creates a purposeful physical interaction.

[Supporting discussion, 4:00–5:03](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=7_GvjZD3Dh0&t=240s)

### Dice for hidden choices

A die under a cup can preserve a small secret value or choice without custom components. Players can conceal a chosen face for a bid, initiative decision, secret deployment, trap, or ambush and reveal it later.

**Tradeoff to consider:** The method is inexpensive and hard to alter once covered, but supports only limited hidden information and requires trust in handling.

**Design prompt:** Use covered dice for simple concealed values or locations, not elaborate hidden-state systems.

[Supporting discussion, 15:58–17:04](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=7_GvjZD3Dh0&t=958s)

### Dice as themed bluffing tools

A small bluffing or bidding procedure can use dice to evoke gambling or risk-taking themes. Players can secretly place and manipulate dice, then reveal them after a brief round of betting or deception.

**Tradeoff to consider:** The mini-game can add atmosphere and player interaction, but may interrupt a combat game if it takes too long.

**Design prompt:** Keep any dice bluffing sequence brief and tie its payoff directly to the game's setting or core tension.

[Supporting discussion, 14:50–15:55](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=7_GvjZD3Dh0&t=890s)

### Dice as shared battlefield markers

Dice can track global values, objective identities, terrain effects, or aura range where every player needs the same information. A large die can show turn number; a die by an objective can indicate its identity or radius; terrain dice can display a temporary state.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Visible shared markers reduce repeated explanation, but can clutter the board or obscure miniatures.

**Design prompt:** Use a small number of visible dice for state that affects both players and should be checked frequently.

[Supporting discussion, 17:23–18:26](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=7_GvjZD3Dh0&t=1043s)

### Bag pulls create allocation randomness

Colored dice drawn from a bag can randomize which units activate or how many resources they receive. A shared draw selects unit-colored dice; counts can give some units more attacks, leave others inactive, or produce unequal allocations.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Bag pulls vary access and can add uncertainty without new dice types, but may leave a unit with nothing in a given round.

**Design prompt:** Use colored dice in a bag when uneven activation is intentional, and test whether zero allocations create meaningful tension or frustration.

[Supporting discussion, 19:27–20:32](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=7_GvjZD3Dh0&t=1167s)

### Store and spend rolled dice

Dice can be rolled once, then held as a resource pool for later choices rather than discarded after each check. Players may reserve results, spend them to modify later actions, or assign a stored face to a future event; this gives dice memory while preserving visible values.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Stored dice support planning and control, but need rules for when values are rolled, held, spent, or refreshed.

**Design prompt:** Use a reserve pool when resource timing is a core decision and make the lifecycle of each die explicit.

[Supporting discussion, 22:31–23:37](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=7_GvjZD3Dh0&t=1351s)

### Die orientation can encode facing

A die's facing can represent a unit's orientation or defensive posture, turning number selection into a spatial tradeoff. In an illustrative design proposal, the upward face tracks wounds and side faces attack toward their directions; losing a wound can expose a stronger side face.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Orientation makes state tactile and meaningful, but requires standardized dice layouts and clear rules for rotating them.

**Design prompt:** Test die-facing mechanics with standard numbered dice and ensure players can read orientation consistently around the table.

[Supporting discussion, 25:23–26:26](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=7_GvjZD3Dh0&t=1523s)

### Use die position and shape

Dice can communicate direction, proximity, or areas through where and how they land, not just the number showing. A pointed d10 can show scatter direction; dice proximity can select results; a group of dice can outline an area or barrier; physical drops can introduce dexterity.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Physical interactions make the tabletop itself part of resolution, but introduce skill, handling disputes, and possible manipulation.

**Design prompt:** Use spatial dice mechanics when physical placement supports the game's fantasy, and specify what counts if dice scatter or touch models.

[Supporting discussion, 30:01–31:05](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=7_GvjZD3Dh0&t=1801s)

**Episode-specific limits**

- Several physical mechanics are speculative brainstorms rather than tested rules.
- The speakers explicitly caution that dexterity and bluffing mechanics suit some genres better than others.

## Making Dice Pools Work

**Source:** [Making Dice Pools Work](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=_jRw3-WTF8A) · **Video ID:** `_jRw3-WTF8A`

Probability distributions, die choice, player-facing math, pool size, and mechanical/aesthetic reasons to use dice pools.

### Summed dice concentrate totals

Adding independent dice makes middle totals more likely than extreme totals; this statement concerns sums, not all dice-pool operations. The source uses 2d6 and the many combinations producing seven to illustrate the effect.

**Tradeoff to consider:** A concentrated sum gives a more predictable total relative to its range; increasing dice still increases absolute variance. Success counts and highest-die pools require separate analysis.

**Design prompt:** State the aggregation rule and compare the distribution relevant to the decision.

[Supporting discussion, 4:06–5:10](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=_jRw3-WTF8A&t=246s)

### Choose dice distributions deliberately

Different dice and combinations create different probability shapes, so select them for the experience rather than only the range of numbers. A single die is flat; summed dice concentrate around central results; dice such as d6 and d12 allow convenient subdivisions and probability targets.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Familiar combinations are easier for players to estimate, while unfamiliar dice increase design flexibility but obscure odds.

**Design prompt:** Test the full distribution and consider what probability patterns players can intuitively understand.

[Supporting discussion, 3:33–4:40](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=_jRw3-WTF8A&t=213s)

### Avoid opaque player-facing math

A mechanic may be mathematically sound yet ask too much probability estimation from players during decisions. Players usually understand common d6 patterns, but mixed or unfamiliar dice combinations make it difficult to judge likely results and compare options.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Complex distributions can offer tuning control, but can make player decisions feel random because they cannot evaluate the odds.

**Design prompt:** Keep player-facing probability intuitive or clearly communicate likely outcomes; reserve complex calculations for designer-side balancing.

[Supporting discussion, 7:47–8:51](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=_jRw3-WTF8A&t=467s)

### Dice pools reduce arithmetic

A pool of target-number dice can replace adding and comparing several large values with simply counting successes. Players roll multiple dice and look for a known face threshold, avoiding opposed totals and repeated modifier arithmetic.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Counting successes is quick and visible, but pools need enough dice to produce their intended distribution and can require many components.

**Design prompt:** Use pools when their visual counting is easier than the arithmetic they replace.

[Supporting discussion, 10:11–11:14](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=_jRw3-WTF8A&t=611s)

### Tune volatility with pool size

Tune pool size together with target number and result interpretation; small pools can deliberately remain volatile. The speakers recommend larger pools for predictable proportions but also describe Billion Suns using only two d12s against target silhouettes.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Larger pools add handling. For success counting, variance depends on success probability and pool size; die face count alone does not dictate a universal minimum pool size.

**Design prompt:** Calculate the actual success-count or other result distribution rather than adopting the speakers’ rough ten-to-fifteen-dice heuristic as a requirement.

[Supporting discussion, 22:35–23:40](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=_jRw3-WTF8A&t=1355s)

### Use only useful result granularity

Choose a die that provides enough distinct outcomes for the decisions the game needs. A d6 gives few steps for ranking fighters, while a d100 can separate many skill levels; success margin may also supply degrees of outcome.

**Tradeoff to consider:** More granularity enables fine distinction but can make the system harder to interpret; binary success may be sufficient for many tests.

**Design prompt:** Identify whether the game needs a yes/no outcome, a margin, or many discrete ratings before selecting a die.

[Supporting discussion, 15:48–16:54](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=_jRw3-WTF8A&t=948s)

### Player-facing versus internal probability

Designers must track both the actual distribution and the odds players can reasonably predict. A probability tool can calculate hidden balance details, while players may only need to know that more dice or a lower target improves their chances.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Hidden math gives designers precision; opaque odds can make players' strategic choices feel arbitrary.

**Design prompt:** Use probability calculators to validate the system and keep the actionable information legible to players.

[Supporting discussion, 31:53–32:57](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=_jRw3-WTF8A&t=1913s)

### Player experience and dice identity

Dice shape and quantity communicate a game's heritage and tone as well as its mathematics. A bucket of d6s evokes familiar mass-battle systems, a single d20 can evoke role-playing encounters, and an underused d12 may carry less genre baggage.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Familiar dice ease access and signal known procedures; unusual dice can give a game a distinct feel but may require players to acquire them.

**Design prompt:** Consider component availability and the expectations a die choice sets, alongside its probability curve.

[Supporting discussion, 27:55–28:56](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=_jRw3-WTF8A&t=1675s)

### Dice ergonomics and availability

Die choice also has practical consequences: component availability, handling and readability matter alongside probability. The discussion praises familiar d6 handling, notes d20 faces can settle poorly on soft surfaces, and mentions X-Wing’s custom d8 icon pools.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Unusual dice or custom faces can suit a design but may be harder to obtain or physically read.

**Design prompt:** Try the intended dice on the actual play surface and assess ownership and readability.

[Supporting discussion, 29:07–30:14](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=_jRw3-WTF8A&t=1747s)

**Episode-specific limits**

- Probability recommendations are rules of thumb from the speakers, not exact universal thresholds.
- The episode includes personal aesthetic preferences about particular die shapes; these are labeled as such.

## Initiative Systems in Wargames

**Source:** [Initiative Systems in Wargames](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=afUaBHlKfcI) · **Video ID:** `afUaBHlKfcI`

Player and unit initiative, activation order, push-your-luck activation, command systems, and tempo control.

### Static initiative

A fixed alternating order is predictable and reliable, supporting planning without additional subsystems. Players take turns in the same sequence after a possible initial random or purchased first turn.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Predictability lowers cognitive burden, but never varies the rhythm or allows the board state to alter tempo.

**Design prompt:** Use static order when clarity and planning matter more than changing initiative pressure.

[Supporting discussion, 2:12–3:18](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=afUaBHlKfcI&t=132s)

### Initiative categories

Initiative can be random, bought, or earned through decisions made during play. A game may roll for order, pay for priority, or let actions/resources influence who acts first; these can combine with different activation structures.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Each source creates different incentives and player expectations, so 'initiative' should be specified as more than a single roll.

**Design prompt:** Identify whether initiative is a random result, list-building choice, or earned tactical reward before selecting mechanics.

[Supporting discussion, 3:20–4:26](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=afUaBHlKfcI&t=200s)

### I-go-you-go double turns

Randomly deciding the next turn in an army-wide activation system can create a powerful double-turn swing. One player may act, then win initiative again before the opponent acts, giving a whole army two turns consecutively.

**Tradeoff to consider:** The swing adds uncertainty and tactical adaptation, but can make a prior position collapse before the other player responds.

**Design prompt:** Evaluate whole-army double turns separately from alternating activations; test whether the resulting interruption fits the game's intended fairness and drama.

[Supporting discussion, 5:24–6:30](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=afUaBHlKfcI&t=324s)

### Initiative manipulation as a resource

A random initiative roll can become a tactical choice when players spend resources to improve their odds. The Billion Suns example lets a player reduce the die size by spending command tokens, exchanging future resources for a better chance to act first.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Resource bidding gives players agency over randomness, but adds a strategic cost that must compete with other uses.

**Design prompt:** Let players buy initiative only when the resource tradeoff creates a consequential choice, and expose the cost clearly.

[Supporting discussion, 9:08–10:12](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=afUaBHlKfcI&t=548s)

### Unpredictable activation chains

A push-your-luck activation can make players choose between stopping safely and risking further actions. Each additional unit activation becomes harder; failure can end the turn or trigger an opponent's snap activation before control returns.

**Tradeoff to consider:** The subgame creates tension and reaction windows, but can frustrate players who lose remaining actions after one failed test.

**Design prompt:** Use activation risk when the emotional experience should involve sequencing and commitment; make failure costs explicit and test whether they feel fair.

[Supporting discussion, 15:16–16:23](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=afUaBHlKfcI&t=916s)

### Risk sequencing and turnovers

When a failed action ends or interrupts a turn, players must prioritize reliable tasks before risky ones. The Blood Bowl example encourages completing actions that cannot fail first, then choosing when to attempt uncertain actions whose failure causes a turnover.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Risk sequencing creates a rich planning puzzle, but can punish impulsive play and cause frustration when the risky moment arrives too early.

**Design prompt:** If failures terminate a turn, teach players to sequence actions by risk and importance, and ensure that this is the intended challenge.

[Supporting discussion, 16:00–17:04](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=afUaBHlKfcI&t=960s)

### Paid and earned extra activations

Extra activations can be gained through unit abilities, resource spending, or risk-based subgames, each creating a distinct design cost. A model may be purchased with a chain-activation ability, command tokens may buy a second activation, or a roll may risk a turnover for more actions.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Paid abilities affect list construction, resources create in-game timing choices, and push-your-luck creates uncertainty.

**Design prompt:** Choose the acquisition method that best fits whether activation advantage should be a roster feature, tactical expenditure, or gamble.

[Supporting discussion, 18:28–19:35](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=afUaBHlKfcI&t=1108s)

### Unit type and speed sequencing

Initiative can be structured by unit quality, role, or speed rather than using one uniform order. Pilot skill may determine movement or shooting order; categories such as light units and capital ships can act in separate phases to protect vulnerable units from first-turn attacks.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Structured sequencing supports unit identity and reduces some first-turn problems, but can make phase order predictable.

**Design prompt:** Use unit categories to establish a clear battlefield rhythm and prevent the units most vulnerable to acting last from being eliminated before response.

[Supporting discussion, 21:20–22:20](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=afUaBHlKfcI&t=1280s)

### Command reliability

Orders can connect initiative to unit leadership and reliability, making command capacity part of tactical planning. A commander issues orders that units may misunderstand, fail to receive, or execute incorrectly; better command capability improves the chance of following them.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Orders model friction and give leadership value, but create additional tests and unpredictable actions.

**Design prompt:** Use order failure when unreliable command is part of the desired fiction, and decide which actions units can perform without orders.

[Supporting discussion, 23:53–24:56](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=afUaBHlKfcI&t=1433s)

### Activation control and reactions

As units are removed, the surviving player may lose activation choices while the opponent gains uncontested actions; passive reactions can preserve pressure. Each model provides an activation; attrition removes those turns, allowing the larger force to move without response unless auras, snap fire, traps, or control zones still ask questions.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Activation advantage rewards force preservation, but can make late play feel free for one side and empty for the other.

**Design prompt:** Test late-game activation imbalance and consider reaction or board-pressure rules where one player otherwise gets a sequence of unanswered actions.

[Supporting discussion, 33:10–34:15](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=afUaBHlKfcI&t=1990s)

### Speed-based decision pressure

A race to finish simultaneous planning can reduce downtime and make decision speed part of initiative. Players resolve choices together; the first to complete planning gains priority, while the other must finish under pressure.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Speed contests keep both players engaged but can reward quickness over considered strategy.

**Design prompt:** Use speed-based initiative only when time pressure suits the intended player experience, and test that it does not overwhelm deliberative play.

[Supporting discussion, 36:03–37:10](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=afUaBHlKfcI&t=2163s)

**Episode-specific limits**

- The episode surveys many systems, with opinions about which ones are frustrating or satisfying clearly belonging to the speakers.
- Some system examples are described from memory and captions garble a few proper names.

## Types of Randomness

**Source:** [Types of Randomness](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=mfk8wsGVwYc) · **Video ID:** `mfk8wsGVwYc`

Luck and skill in miniature games, uncertainty, output and input randomness, hidden information, and component constraints.

### Skill is necessary to meaningful play

The speakers treat player skill as essential to a game, while asking what role luck should serve. Skill can mean tactical choices, strategic planning, rules knowledge, or—in collaborative play—creating a satisfying shared narrative.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Different games value different skills; a single luck-versus-skill scale can obscure the experience a design intends.

**Design prompt:** Name the skill a game rewards before deciding how much randomness to add.

[Supporting discussion, 4:35–5:41](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=mfk8wsGVwYc&t=275s)

### Luck keeps plans provisional

Randomness can prevent a player's opening plan from determining the entire game and require adaptation to new states. A random event changes the situation, forcing players to formulate a new plan rather than execute a fully precomputed sequence.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Uncertainty creates tactical response and narrative surprise, but can make outcomes harder to control.

**Design prompt:** Use randomness to create changing problems players can answer, rather than to replace decisions with arbitrary outcomes.

[Supporting discussion, 12:29–13:36](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=mfk8wsGVwYc&t=749s)

### Input randomness versus output randomness

Input randomness can create problems players must respond to, while output randomness can invalidate a decision after it is made. A changing battlefield or activation order invites adaptation; a sudden catastrophic roll after a carefully planned attack can remove the payoff without a response window.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Input uncertainty supports reactive skill; output uncertainty adds drama but may deny agency.

**Design prompt:** Give players time and options to respond to random changes, especially when a result could erase a major investment.

[Supporting discussion, 12:26–13:31](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=mfk8wsGVwYc&t=746s)

### Randomness supports comeback stories

Chance can make losses less socially absolute and create memorable stories of unlikely reversals. Players may narrate a narrow win as fate, or remember a last-card or last-roll comeback even when the event was unlikely.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Hope and dramatic reversals sustain engagement, but a rare unearned win may feel unsatisfying to both players.

**Design prompt:** Decide whether dramatic comeback potential is part of the tone, and keep it from making earlier decisions feel irrelevant.

[Supporting discussion, 14:32–15:36](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=mfk8wsGVwYc&t=872s)

### Avoid random results that erase play

If one random result can reverse the entire game, players may stop caring about the decisions that led to it. A final die roll that can flip a chess result makes earlier moves feel irrelevant; if uncertainty affects only part of the result, the contest remains meaningful.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Large swings create drama, but can make strategic effort seem pointless.

**Design prompt:** Check whether high-impact random outcomes preserve the value of prior decisions, and constrain them if they routinely nullify play.

[Supporting discussion, 17:40–18:45](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=mfk8wsGVwYc&t=1060s)

### Randomness can create non-games

Randomness before meaningful choices can prevent the game from becoming playable, even when output uncertainty might otherwise be entertaining. Poor card draws or failed activation checks can leave a player unable to execute a plan before the contest develops.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Early uncertainty models scarcity or risk, but may deny a player a meaningful turn.

**Design prompt:** Audit opening randomness for dead starts and consider smoothing initial resources or guaranteeing basic actions.

[Supporting discussion, 19:28–20:32](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=mfk8wsGVwYc&t=1168s)

### Randomness invites intentionality narratives

Players often attribute agency or bias to random systems, especially when a digital algorithm produces repeated bad results. People organize random outcomes into stories and may perceive a computer as deliberately acting against them more readily than physical dice.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Randomness can enrich narrative interpretation, but perceived unfairness can undermine trust even when probabilities are sound.

**Design prompt:** Make random procedures transparent and explain repeated-outcome behavior when players may suspect hidden bias.

[Supporting discussion, 22:25–23:31](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=mfk8wsGVwYc&t=1345s)

### Perfect predictability can feel flat

Removing randomness increases calculability, but may also remove suspense and the pleasure of uncertainty. A deterministic version of a dice game can make every result predictable; the speakers report that this reduced excitement more than it increased useful skill expression.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Determinism rewards planning, but can become repetitive or overly solved; randomness adds volatility and stories.

**Design prompt:** If reducing luck, verify that the resulting skill decisions remain varied and engaging rather than merely more predictable.

[Supporting discussion, 24:27–25:32](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=mfk8wsGVwYc&t=1467s)

### Hidden information as an alternative to chance

Hidden information can create uncertainty without random results, but miniature games have difficulty tracking it honestly and cleanly. Face-down ranks or secret unit states can make outcomes unknown to opponents; models on a visible table reveal too much unless players use extra records or components.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Hidden information preserves strategic uncertainty, but demands bookkeeping and bespoke tools that may fit board games better.

**Design prompt:** Use hidden state only when its tracking method is simple enough to preserve the physical clarity of a miniature battlefield.

[Supporting discussion, 31:11–32:16](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=mfk8wsGVwYc&t=1871s)

**Episode-specific limits**

- The speakers explicitly say they do not fully resolve why miniature games favor chance; findings represent their hypotheses and examples.
- The episode title concerns randomness broadly, while much discussion frames it as luck versus skill.

## Luck VS Skill

**Source:** [Luck VS Skill](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=pgOB1QBVNXY) · **Video ID:** `pgOB1QBVNXY`

Luck and skill as independent dimensions, input/output randomness, mitigation, and how randomness fits narrative play.

### Luck and skill are independent

More randomness does not automatically mean less skill; luck can change the consistency of results without changing the relative ranking of stronger players. Dice chess retains the same best-to-worst player ranking as chess but requires more games to establish who is stronger; poker can involve both skill and luck.

**Tradeoff to consider:** A luckier game can still reward skill, but single-game outcomes become less reliable measures of player ability.

**Design prompt:** Distinguish skill expression from outcome variance when deciding how much luck the game should contain.

[Supporting discussion, 2:55–4:00](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=pgOB1QBVNXY&t=175s)

### Luck can complement different skill levels

A game can offer low cognitive overhead while still rewarding skill, making it playable when players do not want a demanding strategic session. The speakers distinguish easy-to-start, luck-forward play from games that require sustained planning, without treating one as inherently inferior.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Accessible play supports relaxed sessions, but may frustrate players who expect their planning to dominate outcomes.

**Design prompt:** Describe the kind of skill and attention the game rewards instead of using 'casual' as shorthand for low skill.

[Supporting discussion, 5:09–6:12](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=pgOB1QBVNXY&t=309s)

### Luck can frustrate inexperienced players

High randomness may be easy to engage with but can become frustrating when players care about reaching an objective. A simple roll can amuse by itself, but repeated bad outcomes obstruct an invested player's goal; the speakers suggest this can be harder for children to tolerate.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Chance offers immediate excitement, but can punish emotional investment when the result blocks progress.

**Design prompt:** Evaluate how much agency players have after an unfavorable roll, especially for audiences likely to invest in a clear goal.

[Supporting discussion, 7:56–9:03](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=pgOB1QBVNXY&t=476s)

### Randomness can support narrative discovery

Luck is useful when the point is to discover what happens, but conflicts with a game presented as a test of tactical execution. A narrative game may treat alternate outcomes as equally interesting story branches; a competitive game asks players to control a route to victory.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Story uncertainty creates surprise, while competitive players may see the same uncertainty as undermining a plan.

**Design prompt:** Decide whether the game asks players to find out what happens or to demonstrate superior play, and align randomness accordingly.

[Supporting discussion, 12:07–13:13](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=pgOB1QBVNXY&t=727s)

### Input randomness creates adaptive problems

Randomly generated resources or conditions can preserve skill when players then decide how to use them. A randomly assigned spell or battle-board dice result gives players a new situation; their choices about timing and application determine the payoff.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Input randomness makes scenarios less predictable but still leaves meaningful control over execution.

**Design prompt:** Use random inputs when players should adapt to changing tools or circumstances, and make the follow-up choices consequential.

[Supporting discussion, 15:35–16:41](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=pgOB1QBVNXY&t=935s)

### Output randomness should leave room to respond

Random results after a player commits can erase effort unless the player has ways to adapt or mitigate the next result. An attack roll may fail after setup, but a player can still manage future attempts through resources, rerolls, or choices that reduce risk.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Output randomness can model uncertainty and create suspense, but can feel punitive when the outcome offers no response.

**Design prompt:** Pair high-impact output rolls with mitigation options or meaningful decisions before commitment.

[Supporting discussion, 29:16–30:20](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=pgOB1QBVNXY&t=1756s)

### Set expectations for narrative uncertainty

Unexpected results can be welcome when players approach the game as a shared discovery of the story. The speakers contrast wondering which army will win with judging force selection and tactical execution, and discuss players enjoying or disliking troops refusing orders.

**Tradeoff to consider:** The same loss of control may support one group’s story and frustrate another group’s investment.

**Design prompt:** Communicate how strongly narrative surprise versus tactical control shapes the experience.

[Supporting discussion, 12:49–13:55](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=pgOB1QBVNXY&t=769s)

### Prepare for known output risk

When players know a random test is coming and can make allowances for it, failure may reflect poor risk management rather than arbitrary luck. Players can choose a safer movement option, preserve rerolls, or avoid relying on a narrow result; failing after ignoring those safeguards is a consequence of their choice.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Preparation gives agency, but too much mitigation can remove the excitement of the roll.

**Design prompt:** Make output risks visible and give players at least some opportunity to reduce exposure before rolling.

[Supporting discussion, 30:34–31:37](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=pgOB1QBVNXY&t=1834s)

### Resources can make randomness interactive

A resource tied to random movement can let players choose how much uncertainty to accept now and later. Gaslands' skid dice create uncertain movement, while hazard tokens and rerolls support later decisions about managing that uncertainty.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Random resolution remains dramatic, but persistent resources provide a planning layer and some control.

**Design prompt:** Connect random outcomes to a resource that enables subsequent decisions rather than treating each roll as an isolated result.

[Supporting discussion, 28:34–29:41](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=pgOB1QBVNXY&t=1714s)

### Input and output randomness can chain

In multi-stage systems, one random event can become an input to later decisions, so the categories are not always cleanly separable. A roll may determine movement, which changes available positions and then affects later attacks; the same event functions as output and as a new state for the next choice.

**Tradeoff to consider:** The labels help analyze agency, but overclassifying chained events can obscure how the system actually plays.

**Design prompt:** Trace how each random result changes later options instead of evaluating it as an isolated roll.

[Supporting discussion, 30:42–31:48](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=pgOB1QBVNXY&t=1842s)

**Episode-specific limits**

- Some claims about audience frustration are personal observations rather than controlled studies.
- Examples such as Warhammer, Magic, and Gaslands illustrate differing design goals, not universal conclusions about randomness.

## 10 most powerful uses for cards.

**Source:** [10 most powerful uses for cards.](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=8VFPFNmSAho) · **Video ID:** `8VFPFNmSAho`

Practical card uses, prototyping, custom decks, player access, and production tradeoffs.

### Cards and probability distributions

Cards can produce probability curves, but players may not intuitively understand them as readily as familiar dice combinations. Drawing multiple cards and summing ranks produces a distribution; face-card values can also weight results toward certain numbers.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Cards support unusual distributions, but translating a familiar dice curve may require explanation and limit rank choices.

**Design prompt:** Use card sums or face-card weighting only when the resulting odds can be understood or taught at the table.

[Supporting discussion, 3:45–4:49](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=8VFPFNmSAho&t=225s)

### Card volume and miniature scale

Cards are less practical than dice when many units must resolve attacks simultaneously. A player may roll many dice together, but flipping a separate card for each of many figures slows mass resolution.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Cards offer richer per-draw information; dice handle large batches more efficiently.

**Design prompt:** Choose cards for individual decisions or events and dice for frequent high-volume resolution.

[Supporting discussion, 4:43–5:47](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=8VFPFNmSAho&t=283s)

### Cards as events, setup, and reference

A deck can generate random events and accelerate scenario setup, while individual cards can provide quick-reference information. Cards may define weather, encounters, setup, or bonuses; unit and spell cards keep key information visible without book searches.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Cards combine varied information, but add component handling and may require a deck for each type of procedure.

**Design prompt:** Use cards where varied events or frequent lookups benefit from a compact visible reference.

[Supporting discussion, 5:13–6:20](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=8VFPFNmSAho&t=313s)

### Cards as measuring templates and dashboards

Card dimensions can perform physical measurement and list-building functions as well as hold information. A card frame can measure a ship's reach; pilot and upgrade cards can assemble a tactile roster and dashboard.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Physical cards reduce separate templates and make lists tangible, but require consistent dimensions and enough table space.

**Design prompt:** Consider card footprints as reusable templates or dashboard components when their shape can replace another accessory.

[Supporting discussion, 7:53–9:00](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=8VFPFNmSAho&t=473s)

### Prototype cards cheaply

Paper cards can be prototyped quickly with ordinary layout tools or sleeves, making iteration practical before manufacturing. Designers can print multiple card faces, fold double-sided sheets, cut them out, and insert them into standard sleeves.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Home prototypes are cheap and editable, but take manual cutting and may not match final component quality.

**Design prompt:** Use sleeve-sized paper prototypes early, and update shared layouts so balance edits propagate across the deck.

[Supporting discussion, 10:26–11:28](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=8VFPFNmSAho&t=626s)

### Cards for programming and secret orders

Cards can encode hidden orders or planned movement that players reveal simultaneously. A card can specify a stance, order, or movement choice; players commit face down and reveal after both have planned.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Hidden commitment creates prediction and planning tension, but requires clear timing and rules for when cards can be changed.

**Design prompt:** Use cards for secret plans when simultaneous reveal supports the intended tactical challenge.

[Supporting discussion, 14:45–15:48](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=8VFPFNmSAho&t=885s)

### Custom card access and logistics

Custom decks raise the access barrier and create production and stock obligations beyond ordinary reference cards. A player can handwrite a stat card, but a deck used as a randomizer needs identical backs, a complete set, and reliable acquisition.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Custom decks enable tailored mechanics; needing a proprietary deck may block play when it is unavailable.

**Design prompt:** Differentiate optional reference cards from required randomizer decks, and specify a standard-card substitute where practical.

[Supporting discussion, 15:44–16:46](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=8VFPFNmSAho&t=944s)

### Build custom decks from standard cards

A standard deck can approximate custom-deck mechanics by removing some cards or assigning effects to suits and ranks. Players can define a reduced subset of a regular deck and map suits or low/high ranks to the game's actions.

**Tradeoff to consider:** This avoids bespoke printing and supports deck construction, but reduces the number of available signals and may be less exact.

**Design prompt:** Use a standard deck as a low-cost prototype or published substitute before requiring custom components.

[Supporting discussion, 17:32–18:33](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=8VFPFNmSAho&t=1052s)

### Custom decks and pay-to-win risk

If stronger cards or upgrades are tied to product releases, card availability can become a competitive fairness issue. More purchased products may grant a larger pool of build options; essential card packs can reduce the need to buy unrelated miniatures but still create availability constraints.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Product expansion supports variety and revenue, but can tie competitive power to purchase access.

**Design prompt:** Provide essential card access independently of premium products and consider whether purchases increase power or only broaden choices.

[Supporting discussion, 21:16–22:20](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=8VFPFNmSAho&t=1276s)

### Cards as hobby objects

Cards can extend miniature-game customization through marking, collecting, and thematically matching decks. Players can write on cards, alter them to represent a force, or select an existing themed deck that fits the game's fiction.

**Tradeoff to consider:** This adds a low-cost hobby dimension and personal ownership, but destructive or legacy uses require clear limits and replacement options.

**Design prompt:** If customization is intended, give players inexpensive ways to mark or sleeve cards and explain whether changes persist between games.

[Supporting discussion, 25:57–27:00](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=8VFPFNmSAho&t=1557s)

**Episode-specific limits**

- Some proposed card-throwing and physical direction methods are brainstorms, not validated components.
- The speakers acknowledge cards are not a good replacement for dice in every mass-resolution context.

## Movement system Tier list: Templates VS Measures, Awesome battle to the death!

**Source:** [Movement system Tier list: Templates VS Measures, Awesome battle to the death!](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=F1x6me9gNYE) · **Video ID:** `F1x6me9gNYE`

Movement templates, measuring tools, momentum, usability, and grid alternatives.

### Templates encode momentum

Templates are useful when movement should commit to a finite path or minimum distance rather than allow arbitrary partial movement. A vehicle must use a defined maneuver; it cannot claim to move only a tiny fraction to avoid an imminent collision, preserving the sense of speed and momentum.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Commitment creates meaningful movement constraints, but reduces flexibility to stop or adjust mid-move.

**Design prompt:** Choose templates when minimum movement and momentum are core to the fiction or tactical puzzle.

[Supporting discussion, 2:16–3:19](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=F1x6me9gNYE&t=136s)

### Templates make spatial puzzles tactile

Fitting a movement template into available space can make positioning an immediate physical puzzle. Players can see whether a maneuver physically fits between obstacles and where it places the model without repeatedly measuring several relationships.

**Tradeoff to consider:** This tactile feedback is satisfying for vehicles and discrete maneuvers, while free movement often requires more abstract measuring and comparison.

**Design prompt:** Use templates when the path itself is part of the decision and the model physically matches the template's scale.

[Supporting discussion, 4:16–5:20](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=F1x6me9gNYE&t=256s)

### Templates need not require full movement

Templates can serve as measuring guides while still allowing partial movement, showing that template and tape systems are not strict opposites. In the cited game, infantry may move any distance along a straight template, while fast flyers use the full maneuver templates.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Hybrid use preserves the convenience of a guide but can blur the rules distinction between movement modes.

**Design prompt:** Use templates as user-interface tools even where movement remains flexible, and define which units must complete the full path.

[Supporting discussion, 6:02–7:08](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=F1x6me9gNYE&t=362s)

### Templates can simplify complex rules

A template can make a maneuver easier to teach and execute than prose about turning, wheeling, and partial movement. The shape directly constrains a unit's movement path and can replace detailed measurement rules, especially for vehicles or formed units.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Templates reduce written and table explanation but introduce a physical component and may limit maneuvers to the supplied shapes.

**Design prompt:** Prototype a template when a movement rule is difficult to describe in words or creates repeated measurement disputes.

[Supporting discussion, 8:09–9:14](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=F1x6me9gNYE&t=489s)

### Match tool to unit scale and behavior

A maneuver tool should fit the size and expected movement of the piece using it. Small starfighters and cars visually fit their templates, while large ships or infantry blocks can feel clumsy or too large for the same mechanism.

**Tradeoff to consider:** A shared template system is consistent, but can feel awkward for pieces with very different footprints or movement behavior.

**Design prompt:** Test template scale and physical fit with the largest and smallest units; use another movement mode where the tool feels unwieldy.

[Supporting discussion, 10:28–11:33](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=F1x6me9gNYE&t=628s)

### Templates can carry rules

Printed templates can function as quick-reference tools by showing legal moves, labels, and special outcomes directly at the table. Gaslands templates show gears and slide directions so players can identify available moves without consulting a rulebook.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Embedded information speeds play, but can lock future expansions to existing markings or require changes to component sets.

**Design prompt:** Put frequently consulted movement information on the template, and plan how later abilities will work with older components.

[Supporting discussion, 17:45–18:48](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=F1x6me9gNYE&t=1065s)

### Components and access

Templates can lower the need for tape measures but create a component requirement that some players may not have. Players can print and mount simple templates, but those without printers may need purchased versions; a tape measure is widely available but feels more like technical measurement.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Printable templates are approachable and clear, while required accessories can block setup; tape measures avoid a dedicated component but may deter some audiences.

**Design prompt:** Provide printable or purchasable templates and a tape-measure fallback where possible; consider what equipment newcomers already own.

[Supporting discussion, 21:31–22:35](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=F1x6me9gNYE&t=1291s)

### Templates versus continuous movement

Movement tools also signal the game's feel: templates suggest committed maneuvers, while measuring permits continuous positional adjustment. A template path conveys a discrete maneuver; measuring lets players shuffle units, change direction incrementally, and place figures precisely around terrain.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Continuous movement supports flexible positioning and open-table play, but increases measurement and placement decisions; templates constrain those choices.

**Design prompt:** Choose the tool that matches whether units execute maneuvers or continually adjust position, and make that promise visible in play.

[Supporting discussion, 12:02–13:04](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=F1x6me9gNYE&t=722s)

### Grids as a different tradeoff

Grids can make distances crisp and reduce arguments, but require a prepared playing surface and change the miniature-tabletop feel. Discrete cells make movement and adjacency easy to count, while flexible terrain and arbitrary placement are replaced by a fixed map structure.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Grids improve precision and can simplify area effects; they may reduce terrain flexibility and add a map or mat requirement.

**Design prompt:** Consider grids when clear discrete positions are more valuable than freeform terrain placement, and account for the extra setup component.

[Supporting discussion, 29:16–30:22](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=F1x6me9gNYE&t=1756s)

**Episode-specific limits**

- The discussion includes personal aesthetic preferences about templates and grids; these are not universal usability findings.
- A hybrid example is drawn from an authored game and not presented as a tested general solution.

## Going from PvP to PvE; Solo Mode!

**Source:** [Going from PvP to PvE; Solo Mode!](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=IhC7eQbsj24) · **Video ID:** `IhC7eQbsj24`

Converting multiplayer miniatures games to solo or co-op play through scenario design, automation, and bespoke systems.

### Playing both sides is a valid baseline

A player can solo many games by controlling both forces, but a published solo mode should add value beyond that basic option. The player already knows the rules and can make both sides' decisions; a separate mode may streamline or alter that process.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Self-play requires no extra system, but removes opponent uncertainty and can be unsatisfying in hidden-information games.

**Design prompt:** Treat playing both sides as a fallback and ask what added experience a dedicated solo mode provides.

[Supporting discussion, 5:48–6:52](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=IhC7eQbsj24&t=348s)

### Hidden information challenges solo play

Games built around secrecy and asymmetric knowledge are difficult to solo by simply switching perspectives. The solo player knows both hidden hands or unit identities, eliminating the uncertainty that creates the core interaction.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Playing both sides can still teach rules or create a toy-soldier narrative, but it cannot reproduce the intended information contest.

**Design prompt:** When secrecy is central, use a separate automation or scenario structure that preserves unknown information.

[Supporting discussion, 6:10–7:16](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=IhC7eQbsj24&t=370s)

### Scenarios replace the opponent with a task

A solo objective and time limit can create a playable challenge more easily than an AI that imitates a human opponent. The player must complete a job with limited resources while obstacles or enemies interfere; the opponent's full strategic reasoning is unnecessary.

**Tradeoff to consider:** The challenge is easier to script and tune, but changes the experience from competitive play to mission solving.

**Design prompt:** Use objective-driven scenarios when a game has clear tasks, and reserve full AI for cases where opposing decisions are essential.

[Supporting discussion, 15:15–16:21](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=IhC7eQbsj24&t=915s)

### Solo difficulty should test mastery

The speakers often make solo opposition stronger to compensate for limited automation, while recognizing that players want different difficulties. An automated opponent lacks human strategic intelligence, so stronger or earlier threats can compensate; the source describes powerful ships appearing early in a Billion Suns solo campaign.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Strong opposition makes replay valuable, but may frustrate players who primarily want a narrative or miniature showcase.

**Design prompt:** Tune difficulty intentionally and consider giving players a choice between a gentle story experience and a demanding tactical challenge.

[Supporting discussion, 11:10–12:12](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=IhC7eQbsj24&t=670s)

### Solo modes benefit from difficulty options

Different players want different levels of challenge, so a scenario can offer simple difficulty adjustments. Extra forces, stronger threats, or modifiers to encounter generation can change how hard the objective is to achieve.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Difficulty choices widen the audience, but require testing that each setting still creates coherent play.

**Design prompt:** Offer a small number of transparent adjustments for players seeking either a relaxed narrative or a tough mastery test.

[Supporting discussion, 14:14–15:16](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=IhC7eQbsj24&t=854s)

### Objective-driven systems are easiest to adapt

Solo scenarios are simpler to design when the original game already contains independent objectives or scoring opportunities. A player can pursue contracts, profit, or points under a time limit; where the base game has no task, the scenario must invent one.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Built-in objectives support a natural solo mode, while combat-only games need added missions or automation.

**Design prompt:** Audit the game's objectives first and reuse them for score-attack or time-attack challenges where possible.

[Supporting discussion, 15:41–16:45](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=IhC7eQbsj24&t=941s)

### Use clocks and speed bumps

A basic solo mission can combine an objective, a time limit, and obstacles that require thought to overcome. The player has a fixed number of turns to reach a goal, while guards, monsters, or terrain consume time and create graded success levels.

**Tradeoff to consider:** This is easy to adapt across systems, but requires calibrating challenge difficulty against movement and combat speed.

**Design prompt:** Estimate how long an objective normally takes, add a meaningful obstacle, and set turn limits that create partial success as well as failure.

[Supporting discussion, 18:05–19:13](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=IhC7eQbsj24&t=1085s)

### Solo battle games may need a different frame

A symmetrical battle game may not translate directly into solo play; a siege, defense, or mission can provide a more natural structure. One force can remain static while the player maneuvers, or the battle can become a behind-enemy-lines mission with distinct goals.

**Tradeoff to consider:** A new scenario frame creates a workable solo challenge but may shift the game away from its original large-battle experience.

**Design prompt:** Decide whether solo play should preserve the original battle or deliberately adapt it into a different kind of mission.

[Supporting discussion, 20:16–21:24](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=IhC7eQbsj24&t=1216s)

### Cards and tables can generate evolving obstacles

Random encounters can reveal new objectives and complications over time, supporting an emergent solo story. Trigger points, cards, or tables can introduce guards, hazards, clues, or new threats as the player moves through the board.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Emergent events add replayability and surprise, but require additional tables, triggers, and timing rules.

**Design prompt:** Add random complications where they create decisions, and keep the event procedure simple enough to run without a second player.

[Supporting discussion, 22:51–23:54](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=IhC7eQbsj24&t=1371s)

### Threat markers create hidden escalation

A visible map of partially hidden threats can let solo players plan while preserving uncertainty about what each threat contains. Markers have concealed identities and become more dangerous as time passes or they gather; revealed enemies then follow their own behavior rules.

**Tradeoff to consider:** This combines planning, suspense, and escalation, but adds multiple state systems that can make a full solo game harder to learn.

**Design prompt:** Use layered threat markers when the scenario needs both visible pressure and uncertain encounters, while limiting the number of simultaneous rules tracks.

[Supporting discussion, 29:40–30:43](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=IhC7eQbsj24&t=1780s)

### Keep opponent setup fast

A solo mode should let players invest in their own force while making the enemy side quick to assemble and run. Provide fixed enemy lists or simple generation rules so players do not have to build and pilot two complex forces.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Fast setup reduces friction, while detailed enemy customization may deepen variety at the cost of preparation time.

**Design prompt:** Make the enemy roster and behavior easy to deploy, and reserve complexity for the player-facing decisions.

[Supporting discussion, 31:01–32:06](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=IhC7eQbsj24&t=1861s)

### Enemy behavior should fulfill genre expectations

The automated side is often the narrative star, so it should act like the monster, guard, or antagonist players came to face. A solo scenario may have simple player heroes but must make Dracula or another headline threat behave in a recognizable, satisfying way.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Thematic behavior may be less strategically optimal, but it delivers the promised story and spectacle.

**Design prompt:** Design enemy behavior around recognizable genre actions and cinematic moments, not only efficient tactics.

[Supporting discussion, 34:04–35:06](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=IhC7eQbsj24&t=2044s)

**Episode-specific limits**

- Several solo systems and scenarios discussed are prototypes or personal examples; their success is not evaluated systematically.
- The episode transitions to a promised AI discussion that is not included in this transcript segment.

## The most powerful AI in gaming!

**Source:** [The most powerful AI in gaming!](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=dv_ZykdY6Mo) · **Video ID:** `dv_ZykdY6Mo`

Solo tabletop opponent behavior, target selection, procedural burden, and reactions.

### Predictable target selection enables kiting

A solo opponent that always targets the nearest model can be manipulated. Players place decoys or stand just outside charge range to redirect the AI away from a threat.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Simple rules are fast but create exploitable behavior.

**Design prompt:** Test target priorities against deliberate baiting.

[Supporting discussion, 23:02–24:04](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=dv_ZykdY6Mo&t=1382s)

### Randomness is a backstop, not a full policy

Random choices can prevent predictability but may cause implausible indecision. Random target changes can make an opponent switch between goals or ignore an obvious win.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Randomness adds variation while weakening competent behavior.

**Design prompt:** Use random selection only after meaningful criteria fail.

[Supporting discussion, 4:58–5:59](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=dv_ZykdY6Mo&t=298s)

### Flowcharts and tables trade precision for speed

Tables are quick to run while flowcharts can encode more contextual behavior. A die lookup gives direct instructions; conditional steps can examine nearby models and objectives.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Tables can make dumb results; flowcharts require more player processing.

**Design prompt:** Match procedure complexity to how much time the human should spend piloting the opponent.

[Supporting discussion, 9:30–10:37](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=dv_ZykdY6Mo&t=570s)

### Simplify AI units

Automated forces should have fewer interacting options than player-controlled units. The player has no interest in choosing among an automated model’s equivalent actions, so simple stats and direct instructions reduce handling.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Simplification removes some of the source game’s tactical richness.

**Design prompt:** Design automated models for fast operation instead of copying all player-facing complexity.

[Supporting discussion, 15:19–16:24](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=dv_ZykdY6Mo&t=919s)

### Prioritize target logic

Identifying what matters is harder than telling the AI how to move toward it. The episode proposes layered target criteria: nearby threats, objective proximity, vulnerability, then random tie-break.

**Tradeoff to consider:** More contextual logic resists abuse but is harder to operate and balance.

**Design prompt:** Use a short ordered priority list tied to objective impact.

[Supporting discussion, 21:47–22:52](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=dv_ZykdY6Mo&t=1307s)

### Never ask the player to choose for the AI

Unresolved choices put the solo player in a conflict between fairness and self-interest. Add tie-break criteria or a random fallback so every situation has an instruction.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Specific rules take extra writing but make turns faster and avoid self-adjudication.

**Design prompt:** Audit every branch for a deterministic or randomized resolution.

[Supporting discussion, 23:51–24:55](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=dv_ZykdY6Mo&t=1431s)

### Reactions create spatial pressure

AI reactions can interrupt player actions and make movement order matter. Proximity or being targeted triggers an enemy response, creating zones and sequencing puzzles.
