**Tradeoff to consider:** Reactions raise threat and complexity; too many interruptions can slow play.

**Design prompt:** Use a small number of clear reaction triggers to pressure choices.

[Supporting discussion, 30:57–32:01](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=dv_ZykdY6Mo&t=1857s)

**Episode-specific limits**

- Examples focus on the designers experience and do not establish a universal algorithm.

## The GREATEST power move in games: Activation Advantage

**Source:** [The GREATEST power move in games: Activation Advantage](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=m6b3SgJSPaM) · **Video ID:** `m6b3SgJSPaM`

Alternating activations, unequal force sizes, countermeasures, and tactical choice.

### Activation advantage compounds with attrition

Unequal force sizes and casualties can leave one player with activations after the other has run out. In a one-activation-per-model structure, the numerous force retains choices and objective actions beyond the opponent’s final response.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Activation count adds strategic flexibility beyond combat efficiency.

**Design prompt:** Test unequal force sizes and late-game attrition; measure the value of uncontested activations as well as combat efficiency.

[Supporting discussion, 3:44–4:49](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=m6b3SgJSPaM&t=224s)

### Group activations aid coordination

A player may coordinate several units before the opponent responds. Group activations let nearby models act together.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Groups support combined plans but do not remove interruption risk.

**Design prompt:** Use groups when coordination matters, then test disruption.

[Supporting discussion, 8:10–9:16](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=m6b3SgJSPaM&t=490s)

### Delayed removal softens alpha strikes

Turn-end casualty removal can preserve the opposing player's opportunity to act. A destroyed model remains until the end of the turn.

**Tradeoff to consider:** This limits immediate snowballing but makes damage state less immediate.

**Design prompt:** Consider delayed removal where activation order otherwise prevents any response.

[Supporting discussion, 13:34–14:39](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=m6b3SgJSPaM&t=814s)

### Hybrid phases restore coordination

Alternating by action type can retain responses while allowing plans across the force. Players alternate movement, then shooting, rather than completing one unit's whole activation.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Phases aid coordination but may reduce unit-level interruption.

**Design prompt:** Choose between unit-level interruption and force-level sequencing.

[Supporting discussion, 7:30–8:32](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=m6b3SgJSPaM&t=450s)

### Turnover rules exchange certainty for volatility

A failed activation can hand initiative to the other player. In cited systems, units test to activate; failure ends the current run and passes the opportunity.

**Tradeoff to consider:** It reduces predictable activation advantage but adds chance and possible frustration.

**Design prompt:** Treat it as a stylistic choice and evaluate swinginess.

[Supporting discussion, 15:14–16:18](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=m6b3SgJSPaM&t=914s)

### Objectives can create meaningful activation choices

Activation order matters more when players choose between combat and scoring tasks. Different objectives create competing jobs; hidden objectives can obscure which action matters.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Hidden goals enable bluffing but are difficult to balance and communicate.

**Design prompt:** Build several credible priorities only if players receive enough signals to decide.

[Supporting discussion, 23:31–24:36](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=m6b3SgJSPaM&t=1411s)

### Alternating activation depth is scenario-dependent

The speakers debate whether alternating activation creates real choice or merely obvious serial tasks. Changing board states demand reprioritization, but a glaring threat may make each next move obvious.

**Tradeoff to consider:** The system can feel urgent or repetitive depending on scenario decisions.

**Design prompt:** Test choices against objectives and information, not activation cadence alone.

[Supporting discussion, 17:41–18:45](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=m6b3SgJSPaM&t=1061s)

### Pass tokens equalize timing opportunities

Giving the smaller force passes can reduce activation-count advantage without changing the core turn structure. The speakers propose counting each side’s activations and giving the smaller side a stack of passes, allowing key units to wait while the larger force commits.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Passes preserve timing choice but do not make unequal forces identical or replace scenario balance.

**Design prompt:** Prototype a pass allowance equal to the activation gap and test early objective moves as well as delayed shooting.

[Supporting discussion, 12:35–13:41](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=m6b3SgJSPaM&t=755s)

**Differences in approach or unresolved questions**

- The speakers differ in their personal taste for uncertain activation or turnover systems.

**Episode-specific limits**

- The discussion is primarily design analysis and opinion; examples do not establish universal balance outcomes.

## Why every game with shooting in it is WRONG!

**Source:** [Why every game with shooting in it is WRONG!](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=oXg_k-hnbiI) · **Video ID:** `oXg_k-hnbiI`

Shooting balance, scale abstraction, player experience, range geometry, and nonlethal effects.

### Table scale compresses weapon ranges

Tabletop range bands often distort the real differences between weapon reach. Designers divide table width into movement and shooting units, compressing long-range weapons and stretching pistols.

**Tradeoff to consider:** This keeps combat on the board but strains realism and immersion.

**Design prompt:** Treat range as a deliberate game-scale abstraction and explain the chosen bands.

[Supporting discussion, 2:41–3:42](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=oXg_k-hnbiI&t=161s)

### Shooting can dominate list construction

A player can exploit repeated, low-risk shooting when its output outpaces alternatives. The cited gunline performed well by firing more often and fielding more guns while opponents crossed open ground.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Strong shooting can invalidate other play styles and cause early removal before interaction.

**Design prompt:** Test all-shooting forces and first-turn damage against sparse terrain and melee lists.

[Supporting discussion, 6:02–7:06](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=oXg_k-hnbiI&t=362s)

### Shooting can reduce tactical interaction

If shooting only asks players to prioritize targets, it may produce dull high-reward turns. With broad sightlines and few directional constraints, one side fires while the other removes models.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Lethality creates realism but can leave one player with little agency.

**Design prompt:** Check whether shooting turns create choices for both players, including the recipient of attacks.

[Supporting discussion, 8:00–9:04](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=oXg_k-hnbiI&t=480s)

### Choose a clear role for shooting

The speakers suggest either limiting shooting or centering the whole game on it. Other proposals are unusual range shapes, lower lethality, or shooting that projects pressure rather than damage.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Each path changes genre expectations and requires its own supporting rules.

**Design prompt:** Do not add shooting as a default; choose the experience it should create.

[Supporting discussion, 11:21–12:26](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=oXg_k-hnbiI&t=681s)

### Make range geometry positional

Minimum ranges and blind spots can reward fast units for getting inside a weapon's effective zone. A weapon with a minimum range cannot attack targets too close, creating nested zones of fire.

**Tradeoff to consider:** This creates maneuver choices but may need fictional explanation and careful weapon differentiation.

**Design prompt:** Use minimum ranges and overlapping coverage to create movement decisions, then test whether fast units can exploit the intended gaps.

[Supporting discussion, 20:01–21:04](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=oXg_k-hnbiI&t=1201s)

### Use shooting for suppression or displacement

Shooting can affect movement and action choices without relying only on casualties. The cited examples force units into cover, consume actions, create dangerous zones or push models back.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Nonfatal effects preserve interaction but add state tracking or board constraints.

**Design prompt:** Consider payloads that change decisions when lethal fire makes play one-sided.

[Supporting discussion, 23:58–25:02](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=oXg_k-hnbiI&t=1438s)

### Terrain and arcs make positioning matter

Shooting relies on table layout and facing when line of fire is part of the intended puzzle. The speakers favor scenarios with defined cover and open-space proportions, and restricted firing corridors or arcs.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Arc rules add measurement but make movement and facing consequential; they cannot fix a game that rewards static gunlines.

**Design prompt:** Specify terrain expectations and use arcs only alongside objectives that require engagement.

[Supporting discussion, 27:36–28:40](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=oXg_k-hnbiI&t=1656s)

**Episode-specific limits**

- The speakers state strong personal design opinions and acknowledge successful shooting-focused games.

## What is the Perfect Length for your game?

**Source:** [What is the Perfect Length for your game?](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=2PQYf3ZLPS4) · **Video ID:** `2PQYf3ZLPS4`

Game duration, pacing arc, repeatability, setup cost, audience, and player expectation.

### Pacing arc over fixed duration

Judge length by whether the game completes its intended dramatic curve, not a universal minute target. A satisfying arc gives setup, rising uncertainty and resolution; a short game can feel complete if these beats fit.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Adding turns without reversals can prolong play without adding development; compressing too far can omit the contested middle.

**Design prompt:** Identify the question the match asks and ensure the ending resolves it after meaningful swings.

[Supporting discussion, 6:00–7:06](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=2PQYf3ZLPS4&t=360s)

### Tighten setup and cleanup

Players asking for a longer game may want more of its central play rather than more total elapsed time. Setup, rules loading, opening and cleanup reduce the share of time spent in the contested middle.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Short transitions increase replayability but take design effort and may compromise hobby scale.

**Design prompt:** Measure time spent in preparation, active decisions and resolution separately.

[Supporting discussion, 15:26–16:30](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=2PQYf3ZLPS4&t=926s)

### Match duration to use context

The setting where players will play should shape intended duration. A cafe or pub game needs quick setup and compact play; a dedicated session can support longer play.

**Tradeoff to consider:** A short format improves access but may undersell miniature painting and terrain investment.

**Design prompt:** State the expected session context and account for carrying, setup and reset time.

[Supporting discussion, 19:32–20:40](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=2PQYf3ZLPS4&t=1172s)

### Tell players what kind of session it is

Duration expectations include perceived fullness, not only clock time. The speakers contrast a filler slot with a full evening and note that box size and hobby labor shape expectations.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Breaking common expectations can be refreshing but needs clear communication and a satisfying delivered experience.

**Design prompt:** Position the game honestly and make its play arc justify that promise.

[Supporting discussion, 17:33–18:42](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=2PQYf3ZLPS4&t=1053s)

### Meaningful game time

The active, decision-rich portion should dominate the session. One speaker proposes that the “good bit” should occupy at least 70% and include commitment, uncertainty and resolution; this is a personal heuristic.

**Tradeoff to consider:** The ratio is not universal, but exposes excessive overhead.

**Design prompt:** Use the suggested ratio as a diagnostic, not a rule.

[Supporting discussion, 22:09–23:14](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=2PQYf3ZLPS4&t=1329s)

### Asymmetric scenarios can invite rematches

For strongly asymmetric roles, switching sides can complete the comparison and extend a short session. Each player experiences both roles, as in the cited Netrunner and boarding-game examples.

**Tradeoff to consider:** A paired match takes longer but gives context a one-sided result lacks.

**Design prompt:** Consider paired games when roles differ sharply and comparison matters.

[Supporting discussion, 3:26–4:30](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=2PQYf3ZLPS4&t=206s)

**Episode-specific limits**

- The speakers offer design opinions and examples, not a universal target duration.

## Do bag pulls in games ever work?

**Source:** [Do bag pulls in games ever work?](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=hFZTOVvEqJ0) · **Video ID:** `hFZTOVvEqJ0`

Randomized activation order, bag-pull components, fairness, activation limits, and tactile information.

### Randomized activation order

A bag can randomize which side acts next. Players draw tokens, sometimes mixed with combat markers that interrupt activations.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Order uncertainty adds tension but can produce long inactive stretches.

**Design prompt:** Design for bad sequences, not only typical draws.

[Supporting discussion, 3:31–4:36](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=hFZTOVvEqJ0&t=211s)

### Test rare streaks

Unlikely sequences still merit design attention. Repeated draws for one side and round-ending markers can prevent another side acting.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Safeguards can reduce unfairness but add exceptions.

**Design prompt:** Check extreme streaks and their effect on player experience.

[Supporting discussion, 8:01–9:05](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=hFZTOVvEqJ0&t=481s)

### Make the waiting player active

Activation randomness is less passive when both players have meaningful choices during resolution. One example resolves engaged combat together and allows players to choose hidden attack and defense allocations.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Reactive choices help, but do not restore lost movement agency.

**Design prompt:** Audit the inactive player's decisions, not just turn frequency.

[Supporting discussion, 10:13–11:20](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=hFZTOVvEqJ0&t=613s)

### Justify the component

A bag should create value that cards or dice do not. Unmarked tokens randomize order; different shapes or sizes can enable tactile recognition.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Custom pieces cost more; familiar cards are easier to obtain.

**Design prompt:** State why physical drawing matters before requiring bespoke components.

[Supporting discussion, 14:30–15:34](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=hFZTOVvEqJ0&t=870s)

### Combine hidden and visible timing

Partial information can give players control over when a phase resolves. A concealed battle token may be held until a favorable board configuration, while other triggers are visible.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Timing agency creates tension and surprise, with fairness risks.

**Design prompt:** Decide which resolution triggers are public and which can be withheld.

[Supporting discussion, 19:20–20:29](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=hFZTOVvEqJ0&t=1160s)

### Scale draws to force size

A fixed activation count affects elite and numerous forces differently. The episode questions whether draw totals should track force size, points or command groupings.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Scaling can preserve participation but changes bag size and probabilities.

**Design prompt:** Test elite and horde lists under the same draw rules.

[Supporting discussion, 25:09–26:16](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=hFZTOVvEqJ0&t=1509s)

**Differences in approach or unresolved questions**

- The speakers find some bag-pull mechanics interesting but question whether the bag itself is superior to cards or dice.

**Episode-specific limits**

- The episode evaluates examples and possibilities; it does not settle on a universally preferred system.

## Narrative

**Source:** [Narrative](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=FqDy4m-xmTk) · **Video ID:** `FqDy4m-xmTk`

Preset scenarios and forces, campaign narrative, player agency, and balancing story against hobby preferences.

### Preset scenarios reduce invention burden

A crafted scenario can give players a concrete starting point for story and play. The cited game specifies setting, forces, characters and a linked next scenario.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Prescription creates focus but reduces freedom to use arbitrary collections and stories.

**Design prompt:** Offer ready-to-play scenarios for players who want authored direction.

[Supporting discussion, 2:02–3:07](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=FqDy4m-xmTk&t=122s)

### Narrative does not excuse imbalance

A story scenario can still be balanced when its setup and forces are controlled. Preset lists and positions give designers a bounded state to test, as with the cited Advanced Wars maps.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Balance supports competitive play, while intentional asymmetry may serve story stakes.

**Design prompt:** State whether a scenario prioritizes fairness, drama, or a deliberate mix.

[Supporting discussion, 7:40–8:44](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=FqDy4m-xmTk&t=460s)

### Tutorial progression risks repeat friction

Prescribed learning sequences can burden returning players when they must restart with newcomers. A new participant may need to replay early tutorial missions before reaching later content.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Gradual teaching reduces initial complexity but can slow groups with mixed experience.

**Design prompt:** Provide a way to onboard at the appropriate rules level.

[Supporting discussion, 9:49–10:54](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=FqDy4m-xmTk&t=589s)

### Preset campaigns need cast continuity

A contained story benefits from a cast and roles that remain coherent across encounters. The episode describes an adventure with local factions and repeated protagonist-versus-cronies setups that can become implausible when another hero force appears.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Fixed protagonists support continuity but can constrain who participates and what miniatures are needed.

**Design prompt:** Define who can take each role and how additional players enter the fiction.

[Supporting discussion, 13:02–14:04](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=FqDy4m-xmTk&t=782s)

### Rotate or supply antagonist roles

A narrative campaign can distribute roles by giving players temporary enemy forces or alternate warbands. Players might field heroes in one scenario and supplied minions in another, allowing minion-versus-minion combinations.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Role rotation supports story matchups but some players dislike buying, painting or playing antagonists.

**Design prompt:** Make role expectations and model requirements explicit before a campaign begins.

[Supporting discussion, 14:26–15:29](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=FqDy4m-xmTk&t=866s)

### Separate authored story from emergent story

Players differ in how much narrative direction they want from a game. A game can provide procedures that generate events, or author specific characters, scenes and outcomes for players to enact.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Emergent play protects agency but asks players to invent; authored stories reduce that work but may feel imposed.

**Design prompt:** Treat narrative prescription as a spectrum and signal where the game sits.

[Supporting discussion, 28:49–29:56](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=FqDy4m-xmTk&t=1729s)

### Solo or cooperative play supports stronger prescription

The speakers suggest that fixed narrative scenarios can be easier to accept in solo or cooperative formats. Players share the authored challenge instead of one player being cast as the disposable antagonist against another.

**Tradeoff to consider:** This can reduce versus-role conflict but changes the social and competitive experience.

**Design prompt:** Consider cooperative delivery when a scenario requires strongly unequal narrative roles.

[Supporting discussion, 30:04–31:09](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=FqDy4m-xmTk&t=1804s)

**Episode-specific limits**

- The speakers explore a design space and their preferences rather than prescribe one narrative format.

## Movement

**Source:** [Movement](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=_Lp3ZyXg1A8) · **Video ID:** `_Lp3ZyXg1A8`

Movement modes, physicality, and rules complexity in miniature games.

### Movement asymmetry

Distinct movement rules can make unit classes feel tactically unlike one another; avoid forcing every piece into a universal lowest-common-denominator procedure. Give each movement mode a compact rule and assign it as a unit trait; then evaluate how modes meet at engagement and terrain boundaries.

**Tradeoff to consider:** The interactions can be rich, but add rules load and create severe matchups if one mode can exploit another without counterplay.

**Design prompt:** For a game with meaningfully different bodies or vehicles, prototype two or three legible movement modes and test their interactions directly.

[Supporting discussion, 10:42–11:53](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=_Lp3ZyXg1A8&t=642s)

### Movement agency

Movement restrictions are high impact because they govern the first way players interact with the board; randomness or prohibitions can leave a player with no meaningful action. Keep movement outcomes understandable and preserve some usable choice even when a test fails or a restriction applies.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Constraint creates tactical texture, but too much restriction turns a turn into waiting and can lock a player out of the game.

**Design prompt:** Audit each movement failure state: confirm the player still has a comprehensible decision, alternative action, or recovery path.

[Supporting discussion, 7:42–9:00](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=_Lp3ZyXg1A8&t=462s)

### Movement modes and scope

Movement complexity depends on the environment around it: a template-based space game can stay simple when terrain, cover, and close combat are absent. Rules that would be needed in a terrain-rich skirmish can disappear when the design fixes or excludes terrain interactions.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Removing environmental cases trims the rules, but narrows the kinds of scenes and play the game supports.

**Design prompt:** Treat terrain and engagement as explicit parts of the movement scope; do not borrow a simple movement engine without its simplifying assumptions.

[Supporting discussion, 11:34–12:51](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=_Lp3ZyXg1A8&t=694s)

### Tactical geometry

Precise placement can be enjoyable when it asks players to choose among imperfect compromises; it becomes tedious when players merely calculate a single correct spot. Overlapping threat zones or a deliberate risk trade-off make position a decision rather than a geometry exercise with a uniquely optimal answer.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Dense positional puzzles reward careful players but can demand prolonged measuring and calculation.

**Design prompt:** When exact positioning matters, create competing risks or acceptable alternatives; otherwise abstract or relax measurements where intent is clear.

[Supporting discussion, 16:15–17:24](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=_Lp3ZyXg1A8&t=975s)

### Physical execution and declared intent

The speakers value play by declared intent when the intended legal position is clear, because small placement errors can obstruct the tactical decision. Agree an achievable intended position and allow correction of an obvious placement mistake.

**Tradeoff to consider:** This depends on mutual trust and agreement about what was physically achievable.

**Design prompt:** Consider stating whether players may clarify intended positioning and correct small execution errors.

[Supporting discussion, 18:21–19:26](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=_Lp3ZyXg1A8&t=1101s)

### Mechanic-to-fiction fit

Movement rules communicate weight and cinematic identity; an easy-to-use procedure can still feel wrong when it contradicts how the object should move. Restricted wheeling or side-slipping can make a formation or heavy machine read as weighty, while free pivoting can make a tank feel like a cursor icon.

**Tradeoff to consider:** More physical movement better conveys identity but costs time, explanation, and precision.

**Design prompt:** Test movement by asking both whether it plays cleanly and whether its table behavior evokes the intended object.

[Supporting discussion, 21:03–22:15](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=_Lp3ZyXg1A8&t=1263s)

### Movement complexity budget

Multiple movement systems can create satisfying asymmetric interactions, but consume complexity and rules budget. Assess the combined learning and interaction costs when adding another movement mode.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Interesting movement interactions can also create friction and unfamiliar procedures.

**Design prompt:** Test whether each additional movement mode earns the complexity it introduces.

[Supporting discussion, 26:49–28:03](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=_Lp3ZyXg1A8&t=1609s)

**Episode-specific limits**

- Movement examples and evaluations are the speakers' play experiences and design opinions, not universal performance evidence.
- One referenced current-edition erratum may have been described from memory; do not rely on it as a rules citation.

## How many rules are too many rules?

**Source:** [How many rules are too many rules?](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=BCh6jTozeEI) · **Video ID:** `BCh6jTozeEI`

Rulebook length, game scope, component-supported complexity, and design focus.

### Scope controls word count

A tightly bounded game can omit procedures for units, terrain, and stories it never supports; broader games need more rules because they promise more flexibility. Define the exact play experience and trim rules outside its outline; broad toolkits retain optional systems for varied player-created situations.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Focused rules are easier to learn, while broad applicability requires more coverage and text.

**Design prompt:** Set scope before drafting and challenge each rule against the situations the game actually promises to handle.

[Supporting discussion, 6:54–8:13](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=BCh6jTozeEI&t=414s)

### Components carry rules

Some games communicate complex systems compactly because cards and other components carry information that would otherwise sit in the rulebook. Put recurring local effects on the component where players need them, supported by concise shared procedures.

**Tradeoff to consider:** This shortens reference text but transfers complexity to component layout and can scatter rules across the table.

**Design prompt:** When a procedure repeats with varied effects, test whether a component can state the variation clearly without duplicating core rules.

[Supporting discussion, 3:34–4:56](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=BCh6jTozeEI&t=214s)

### Bounded environment

Fixed terrain behavior can remove many special cases for climbing, gaps, falling, and movement through scenery. A deliberately abstract obstacle rule makes terrain interaction predictable rather than modeling every physical feature.

**Tradeoff to consider:** This saves substantial rules space but gives up cinematic interactions that may be central to some genres.

**Design prompt:** Choose between standardized terrain and detailed interaction based on the game's promised scenes, not an assumed requirement of miniature games.

[Supporting discussion, 7:49–9:02](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=BCh6jTozeEI&t=469s)

### Innovation budget

The speakers advise limiting novel mechanics because several unfamiliar ideas in one section can overwhelm players even when each is good on its own. Treat novelty as a finite learning budget and compare each innovation's contribution to the target experience.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Fewer innovations improve accessibility but can make the design less distinctive; the speakers' suggested count is opinion, not a validated threshold.

**Design prompt:** Identify the core innovations and simplify supporting procedures around them; do not treat the cited 'three' as a universal cap.

[Supporting discussion, 21:56–23:16](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=BCh6jTozeEI&t=1316s)

### Rule necessity

No phase or familiar subsystem is mandatory if it does not help deliver the game's intended experience. Remove inherited conventions such as terrain handling, pivoting, or a separate psychology phase when they do not support the design goal.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Omission improves focus but can surprise players who expect genre conventions or needs explanation in the pitch.

**Design prompt:** Review the outline for borrowed phases and require each to justify its player-facing purpose.

[Supporting discussion, 25:43–27:11](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=BCh6jTozeEI&t=1543s)

### Learning footprint

A small indie game may benefit from being easy to add to a player's existing hobby rather than demanding commitment as their primary game. Short, transmissible rules and low entry cost reduce the effort required to teach and try the game.

**Tradeoff to consider:** A compact game can be approachable but may offer less long-term breadth than a large expandable system.

**Design prompt:** Assess onboarding and teachability as product strengths, especially when the design is not meant to replace a player's main game.

[Supporting discussion, 23:22–24:34](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=BCh6jTozeEI&t=1402s)

### Player generosity

More pages are not automatically more generous; removing unnecessary reading can respect players' time better than supplying every conceivable rule. Include explanation and content when it enables play the game needs, not merely to make the book feel substantial.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Conciseness may leave some readers wanting extra examples or options, while padding imposes a cost on everyone.

**Design prompt:** Use player effort, clarity, and usefulness as editorial tests for additions rather than page count.

[Supporting discussion, 25:15–26:40](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=BCh6jTozeEI&t=1515s)

**Episode-specific limits**

- Page-count targets and claims about market positioning are personal design opinions, not comparative sales evidence.
- The episode contrasts selected games and components; examples do not establish that short rulebooks are always preferable.

## Making Draws Fun!

**Source:** [Making Draws Fun!](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=6oyOMTQnVtM) · **Video ID:** `6oyOMTQnVtM`

Meaning, narrative closure, and tiebreakers for drawn miniature games.

### Draws advance through context

A drawn match can feel consequential when it advances a league or campaign state; otherwise players may feel that nothing changed. Attach match outcomes to a larger progression or ranking so that equal scores still produce shared advancement or a strategic consequence.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Meta-game stakes add meaning but depend on a campaign or league structure that standalone games lack.

**Design prompt:** If draws are common, test their effect on the larger competition and make that effect explicit to players.

[Supporting discussion, 7:42–9:03](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=6oyOMTQnVtM&t=462s)

### Avoid arbitrary tiebreakers

A tiebreak condition that appears only at the end can cheapen the preceding game; if an event matters, include it in scoring from the start. Promote the proposed tie criterion to a visible secondary victory point condition, or allow the draw to stand.

**Tradeoff to consider:** An extra scoring axis can complicate play, but a hidden last-minute criterion feels disconnected and arbitrary.

**Design prompt:** Disclose all scoring factors before play and ensure a tiebreaker reflects actions players could knowingly pursue.

[Supporting discussion, 16:39–17:48](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=6oyOMTQnVtM&t=999s)

### Scenario gives a draw meaning

Scenario framing can make an equal score narratively rich by explaining what each side achieved or failed to achieve. Define the engagement's stakes and interpret outcomes as specific events, such as holding an airfield or destroying a carrier group.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Narrative interpretation adds closure but requires scenario writing and may be less satisfying to players seeking only a ranked result.

**Design prompt:** Write outcome text for win, loss, and draw that connects objective results to the scenario's situation.

[Supporting discussion, 19:21–20:45](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=6oyOMTQnVtM&t=1161s)

### Asymmetry reframes near-draws

An asymmetrical scenario can make a draw an achievement for the disadvantaged side when the challenge is communicated numerically and narratively. One side begins with a lead; the opponent's progress toward parity becomes a visible measure of success.

**Tradeoff to consider:** The structure provides a clear challenge but can feel discouraging if the initial gap or expected goal is not understood.

**Design prompt:** State the asymmetrical objective plainly and test whether intermediate progress feels meaningful to both players.

[Supporting discussion, 21:14–22:24](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=6oyOMTQnVtM&t=1274s)

### Tie spectacle must fit the game

A short finale mini-game can resolve a tie when it expresses the same game's character and creates an entertaining shared moment. The example uses a quick dice-tower stacking contest framed as a spectacle; its speed and chaos fit that particular game.

**Tradeoff to consider:** A physical skill test can be fun for spectators but may feel unrelated or unfair in a different game's competitive context.

**Design prompt:** Use a tie-ending mini-game only when its skill, tone, and duration fit the main game and the table enjoys the spectacle.

[Supporting discussion, 16:57–18:03](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=6oyOMTQnVtM&t=1017s)

### Narrative closure beyond scoring

Narrative games can give players closure without mathematical resolution, but the scenario still needs a coherent account of what happened. A clear story context interprets events and outcomes, including cases where no side is declared the winner.

**Tradeoff to consider:** This supports roleplay and reenactment but depends on scenario quality and players' interest in story over ranking.

**Design prompt:** For narrative play, define the consequences and meaning of major outcomes rather than relying on a score alone.

[Supporting discussion, 23:39–24:48](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=6oyOMTQnVtM&t=1419s)

**Episode-specific limits**

- Player responses to draws are described as differing preferences; no empirical survey or universal preference is supplied.
- The tie-mini-game example is specific to Space Gits and is not a general competition recommendation.

## Finding your voice.

**Source:** [Finding your voice.](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=qqDrQQ6Bg40) · **Video ID:** `qqDrQQ6Bg40`

Rulebook tone, in-world narration, clarity, and terminology.

### Keep rules facts distinct from character commentary

An in-world narrator can add flavor, but putting binding procedures in a character's voice may make players unsure whether the text is authoritative. Separate technical rules from clearly marked commentary, as in the Saga examples discussed.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Character voice increases immersion but can obscure rules meaning when the narrator is opinionated or unreliable.

**Design prompt:** Use sidebars for character interpretation and keep the procedure itself direct and unambiguous.

[Supporting discussion, 6:15–7:24](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=qqDrQQ6Bg40&t=375s)

### Voice can prepare players emotionally

A consistent tone can teach players how to feel about an unusual mechanic, such as rapid character deaths, before they encounter it repeatedly. The speaker describes using comic punk language to normalize frequent, unpredictable losses in Puke Apocalypse.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Tone can soften a harsh mechanic but may alienate players who do not share the intended attitude.

**Design prompt:** Use voice deliberately to frame emotional expectations around mechanics that could otherwise frustrate or surprise.

[Supporting discussion, 9:21–10:37](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=qqDrQQ6Bg40&t=561s)

### Write with direct authority

The speaker describes moving from passive, ornate and legalistic writing toward plain English and a confident authorial voice. Choose simpler words and sentence structures so readers, including international readers, can understand the intended procedure.

**Tradeoff to consider:** A firm voice is clear but can feel severe if the game's tone calls for warmth or playfulness.

**Design prompt:** Use direct verbs for procedures and reserve softer or modal language for genuine options and recommendations.

[Supporting discussion, 25:14–26:29](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=qqDrQQ6Bg40&t=1514s)

### Use named states only when they help

A short named condition can connect multiple sentences and avoid dense legal prose, but unused or one-off jargon becomes needless vocabulary. The 'on fire' example gives a repeated state a label that lets later rules refer back to it; the speakers warn that labels without follow-up hooks should be removed.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Named states improve cross-reference and brevity but create glossary burden.

**Design prompt:** Name a state when it recurs or meaningfully organizes text; otherwise explain the effect where it occurs.

[Supporting discussion, 37:07–38:13](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=qqDrQQ6Bg40&t=2227s)

### Split complex rules into steps

Breaking a conditional procedure into smaller linked sentences can be easier to follow than packing every exception into one contract-like sentence. A named state serves as a bridge from the trigger sentence to the later effect sentence.

**Tradeoff to consider:** More sentences may repeat terms, but reduce the need to reread a dense clause.

**Design prompt:** For multi-step effects, state the trigger first, define the state or outcome, then explain what happens next.

[Supporting discussion, 35:59–37:09](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=qqDrQQ6Bg40&t=2159s)

### Vary voice by rules function

A game can use a strong persona selectively at moments where it supports play while keeping precision-focused procedures straightforward. The pirate example uses an in-character voice for deception but returns to plain language for measurement and core rules.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Selective voice preserves clarity but requires careful editing to keep tone shifts intentional.

**Design prompt:** Map where a persona adds useful attitude and where players need neutral procedural language.

[Supporting discussion, 32:21–33:21](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=qqDrQQ6Bg40&t=1941s)

**Differences in approach or unresolved questions**

- On named concepts, one speaker prefers a label only when reused, while the other allows a name to bridge two sentences even without wider reuse; both agree that obsolete hooks and unused jargon should be edited out.

**Episode-specific limits**

- Voice and terminology guidance is based on the speakers' writing experience and examples, not a reader-comprehension study.
- Examples refer to specific games and may not generalize to every genre or audience.

## Additional Voices.

**Source:** [Additional Voices.](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=FGzcB6Bhrgg) · **Video ID:** `FGzcB6Bhrgg`

FAQs, completeness, rulebook structure, and balancing precision against cognitive load.

### Separate core instructions from edge cases

An FAQ can hold obscure interactions that most players do not need during their first read, keeping the main rules focused on how to play. The speakers describe publishing an FAQ alongside release to reassure players about rare sequences without treating it as an erratum.

**Tradeoff to consider:** A shorter core text is easier to learn, but players may not find an edge-case answer until they consult another document.

**Design prompt:** Keep frequent procedures in the rules and place rare clarifications in a clearly indexed FAQ or reference section.

[Supporting discussion, 1:59–3:10](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=FGzcB6Bhrgg&t=119s)

### Completeness can become exhausting

Trying to make rules watertight for every imaginable interaction can exhaust readers and make the book less effective at teaching the game. A long sequence of low-probability clauses consumes attention that players need for the procedures they will actually use.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Some ambiguity remains, but exhaustive text can make common rules harder to retain and apply.

**Design prompt:** Prioritize clarity for expected play; document unusual interactions separately when they arise or when players need reassurance.

[Supporting discussion, 3:08–4:24](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=FGzcB6Bhrgg&t=188s)

### A functioning oddity may not need repair

A rare interaction can be strange without preventing play; fixing every odd result may cost more than accepting it. The speakers compare occasional awkward outcomes to a videogame glitch that still resolves and allows the session to continue.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Accepting quirks preserves a lean rules system but may break immersion or upset players who expect simulation fidelity.

**Design prompt:** Distinguish between an interaction that stalls play and one that produces an odd but playable result before adding a correction.

[Supporting discussion, 5:28–6:34](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=FGzcB6Bhrgg&t=328s)

### Use authoritative, plain language

Rules text can be decisive and human without becoming a legal code; direct statements can communicate the procedure more clearly than passive or overqualified wording. The speaker describes moving from passive phrasing toward confident statements of what happens in the game.

**Tradeoff to consider:** A strong declarative voice improves clarity but requires care where the rule is conditional or optional.

**Design prompt:** Write procedures as direct statements, then identify genuine exceptions explicitly rather than hiding them in vague modal wording.

[Supporting discussion, 12:07–13:12](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=FGzcB6Bhrgg&t=727s)

### Use section references without overlegalizing

Numbering and cross-references can help players navigate a complex ruleset, but a legal-document structure is only useful if it improves play and lookup. The speakers discuss section-numbered systems as one possible approach to precision, then contrast them with more accessible writing.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Formal structure supports reference and consistency but can make a book feel intimidating.

**Design prompt:** Choose navigation aids based on the book's size and lookup needs; do not confuse a legal appearance with clarity.

[Supporting discussion, 7:44–8:59](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=FGzcB6Bhrgg&t=464s)

### Treat the rulebook as instruction, not promotion

Once a player is reading the rules, the text's job is to explain the game's facts rather than sell its excitement. The speakers distinguish marketing voice from the authoritative voice of a procedure book.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Flavor and enthusiasm can make a game inviting, but exaggerated claims may obscure how actions actually work.

**Design prompt:** Keep promotional language in pitches and use clear, reliable statements in rules, with flavor separated where it does not interfere.

[Supporting discussion, 11:29–12:24](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=FGzcB6Bhrgg&t=689s)

**Episode-specific limits**

- The episode discusses an FAQ practice and writing philosophy from the speakers' own games; it is not a universal publication standard.
- The speakers acknowledge that different audiences may prefer exhaustive rules and legal-style precision.

## Affordances and what you shouldn't let your players do.

**Source:** [Affordances and what you shouldn't let your players do.](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=ao1l5G6-WOE) · **Video ID:** `ao1l5G6-WOE`

How physical affordances and permissive rules create opportunities players may exploit.

### Remove expected affordances deliberately

A game can withhold an action players would expect from its fiction when that restriction directs them toward its intended puzzle. Portal prevents the portal gun from directly destroying turrets; players must instead use its spatial puzzle tools.

**Tradeoff to consider:** The restriction can initially seem odd, but becomes acceptable when players understand the experience it enables.

**Design prompt:** Identify familiar actions that bypass the intended puzzle and test whether withholding them makes the core play clearer.

[Supporting discussion, 6:00–7:13](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=ao1l5G6-WOE&t=360s)

### Remove shortcuts that bypass the fun

A direct solution can keep players from engaging with a richer intended system even when the shortcut is less enjoyable. Early Boards and Black Flags allowed ordinary killing, which bypassed ship movement and lanes of destruction; removing direct wounds made positional attacks central.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Removing an expected attack outcome narrows player freedom and may exclude people seeking a different combat experience.

**Design prompt:** Observe whether players repeatedly bypass the central system, then consider removing the shortcut instead of merely weakening it.

[Supporting discussion, 9:17–10:23](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=ao1l5G6-WOE&t=557s)

### Priority can favor intended behavior

Giving the preferred action procedural priority can encourage it without removing alternatives entirely. The Doctor Who RPG example allows violence but resolves speaking first, favoring persuasion and confusion in keeping with its protagonist.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Retaining alternatives does not guarantee players choose the intended route; elsewhere in the episode weak stabbing still attracts players.

**Design prompt:** Test action priority as one way to reinforce the behavior the fiction promises.

[Supporting discussion, 15:30–16:47](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=ao1l5G6-WOE&t=930s)

### Protect the promised chance to act

The speakers describe limiting ranged destruction in Hobgoblin to help every model get a chance to participate, rather than allowing a prized model to disappear before acting. They discuss capped shooting effects and the absence of direct-damage magic as ways to prevent a gunline from defining play.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Players who specifically enjoy destructive gunlines may find that the game does not serve their preference.

**Design prompt:** Check whether opening attacks can contradict a promise that every unit has a chance to contribute.

[Supporting discussion, 18:37–19:39](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=ao1l5G6-WOE&t=1117s)

### Check fixes against design principles

A plausible fix can still fail if it contradicts another design goal; replacing shooting damage with slowing or pushing effects clashed with Hobgoblin’s brutally fast aim. Return to the stated design principles when evaluating alternative effects, rather than treating a locally neat solution as sufficient.

**Tradeoff to consider:** A broad fantasy game has established unit expectations that make removing or redefining a familiar action difficult.

**Design prompt:** For each proposed subsystem fix, check its effect on the whole game’s intended pace and supported tropes.

[Supporting discussion, 22:32–23:33](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=ao1l5G6-WOE&t=1352s)

### Restrict partial movement to create commitment

Gaslands is used to show how removing the freedom to stop partway through a movement template creates a distinctive positional challenge. The chosen template fixes the move instead of merely offering a path along which the player can stop at will.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Newcomers may assume conventional partial movement and need the restriction explained.

**Design prompt:** Look for ordinary movement freedoms whose removal would create useful commitment in the intended game.

[Supporting discussion, 27:33–28:50](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=ao1l5G6-WOE&t=1653s)

**Episode-specific limits**

- The examples are primarily thought experiments and anecdotes, not evidence about how often players exploit such rules.
- The transcript overlaps thematically with the proscription/permission episode but focuses on affordances created by rules and physical handling.

## Non-miniature removal.

**Source:** [Non-miniature removal.](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=RByi1Jyvy10) · **Video ID:** `RByi1Jyvy10`

Alternatives to permanent piece removal and its effects on tension, agency, and game length.

### Removal as an assumption

Permanent removal is common but should be questioned, especially in small-crew games where each removed model erases several choices. Compare the decisions, tools, and interactions lost when a miniature leaves play against the friction its removal creates.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Removal can focus the endgame, but may reduce tactical options and fun for the player losing pieces.

**Design prompt:** For each removal rule, identify the experience it adds and compare it with a temporary or nonlethal consequence.

[Supporting discussion, 1:19–5:26](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=RByi1Jyvy10&t=79s)

### Downed states create rescue choices

A model that is knocked down rather than removed can generate a new tactical priority and resource decision. A prone ally becomes a temporary objective: players choose whether to spend actions helping them up or pursue existing objectives.

**Tradeoff to consider:** This adds interaction and keeps pieces relevant, but requires rules for recovery and can extend play.

**Design prompt:** Prototype a recoverable state when preserving crew presence is valuable, and measure whether rescue choices compete meaningfully with other actions.

[Supporting discussion, 19:17–23:07](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=RByi1Jyvy10&t=1157s)

### Model count shapes attention

Mass-battle and small-crew games may benefit from different removal assumptions because their attention and agency are distributed differently. Large formations can begin visually broad and later focus as models are removed; skirmish pieces may each carry many distinct options from the start.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Removal can sharpen focus in mass battles but may hollow out a small crew's interconnected tactics.

**Design prompt:** Evaluate removal by game scale and by the number of active roles each piece supports, not by genre convention alone.

[Supporting discussion, 4:20–8:07](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=RByi1Jyvy10&t=260s)

### Removal and objective play

In objective games, eliminating opposing models may function as a second route to victory by denying objective participation. Compare scoring timing and kill incentives: endgame-only scoring can reward disabling the opponent before objectives resolve; scoring each turn may secure progress earlier.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Combat keeps stakes high, but may overshadow the stated objective if it primarily removes the opponent's ability to interact.

**Design prompt:** Test whether combat is a meaningful objective in itself or an efficient way to suppress objective play, then align scoring timing with the intended priority.

[Supporting discussion, 26:45–30:23](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=RByi1Jyvy10&t=1605s)

### Permanent removal can express fiction

Different classes of characters may warrant different injury or removal systems when their narrative durability differs. The discussion suggests expendable troops and narratively central heroes need not share the same fate procedure.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Separate treatment can better match fiction but adds complexity and risks making exceptions feel arbitrary.

**Design prompt:** Use differentiated durability only when the fiction and gameplay roles clearly support it, and state the distinction consistently.

[Supporting discussion, 23:51–27:40](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=RByi1Jyvy10&t=1431s)

### Non-removal needs an ending

Keeping every piece available avoids early table-outs and stabilizes match duration, but requires another credible end condition. A fixed round count can guarantee the game lasts a planned duration; without removal, however, designers must supply a non-arbitrary way to conclude.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Predictable length helps scheduling, while artificial endings can feel detached from events.

**Design prompt:** When removing attrition, design and test the ending condition at the same time rather than treating it as a later patch.

[Supporting discussion, 28:01–30:56](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=RByi1Jyvy10&t=1681s)

**Episode-specific limits**

- The speakers state a personal preference and are not arguing that all miniature games should eliminate removal.
- Examples include individual games and anecdotes; no comparative playtest data is reported.

## How to produce minis as an independent designer.

**Source:** [How to produce minis as an independent designer.](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=bxWL7tqnSWY) · **Video ID:** `bxWL7tqnSWY`

Routes, licensing relationships, and sourcing physical miniatures for small independent games.

### Partnership routes

An independent designer can reach out to miniature creators or manufacturers to build a mutually beneficial product relationship instead of commissioning an entire range alone. The episode describes photography permission developing into a retail and Kickstarter partnership, including specific sculpts.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Partnership shares audiences and skills but requires agreement on brand, rights, availability, and promotion.

**Design prompt:** Approach potential collaborators early with a concrete, reciprocal proposal and clarify where each party's work will be sold and shown.

[Supporting discussion, 1:30–2:38](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=bxWL7tqnSWY&t=90s)

### License continuity

A short-term vendor subscription can leave a product exposed if the creator disappears; perpetual commercial licenses can preserve the ability to keep selling prints. The speakers contrast recurring Patreon vendor access with lifetime Kickstarter commercial licenses and describe a creator becoming unreachable during development.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Long-term licenses require upfront commitment and may not fit every platform or creator arrangement.

**Design prompt:** Confirm the duration, sales channels, and post-campaign rights before making licensed miniatures part of a long development cycle.

[Supporting discussion, 8:48–10:10](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=bxWL7tqnSWY&t=528s)

### Collaborative fit

A good creative relationship depends on open communication and benefits in both directions, rather than merely obtaining permission to use someone else's work. The speakers describe mutual promotion, shared brand visibility, and possible future commissions as outcomes of compatible goals.

**Tradeoff to consider:** This can broaden reach but depends on the collaborator remaining engaged and the audiences aligning.

**Design prompt:** Evaluate communication and reciprocal value as part of partner selection, alongside sculpt quality and legal terms.

[Supporting discussion, 7:15–8:26](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=bxWL7tqnSWY&t=435s)

### Let available models inspire design

Existing miniatures can be a productive source of faction concepts and mechanics; available sculpt details can prompt rules rather than merely illustrate finished rules. The speaker describes selecting a flexible tech-pirate range, then writing units to match visible model features and kit-bashed figures.

**Tradeoff to consider:** This reduces sculpting cost and sparks ideas, while availability can constrain faction identity and roster choices.

**Design prompt:** During early design, inventory candidate model ranges and treat their silhouettes and accessories as prompts for playable roles.

[Supporting discussion, 16:45–17:53](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=bxWL7tqnSWY&t=1005s)

### Offer a default miniature path

Even miniature-agnostic games can benefit from a clear starter option for players who do not want to source or kitbash models themselves. A suggested 'step zero' roster gives players a baseline that they can adopt or modify.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Providing a default helps onboarding but adds sourcing or licensing work and should not imply that alternatives are unsupported.

**Design prompt:** Pair agnostic rules with an optional, easy-to-find model recommendation that demonstrates how roles map to figures.

[Supporting discussion, 23:23–24:27](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=bxWL7tqnSWY&t=1403s)

### Build factions around supply

Faction composition is often adjustable, so designers can adapt rosters to existing models and available licenses instead of treating every concept as fixed. The speaker recommends hunting for compatible ranges and shifting faction elements to fit available STL and physical lines.

**Tradeoff to consider:** This can make production achievable but may alter the original creative concept or leave gaps if a range changes.

**Design prompt:** Separate a faction's essential gameplay identity from replaceable visual details before selecting miniatures.

[Supporting discussion, 22:45–23:59](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=bxWL7tqnSWY&t=1365s)

**Episode-specific limits**

- Commercial license prices and marketplace practices are personal experience from the recording date; verify current terms before relying on them.
- The speakers explicitly lack experience commissioning custom miniatures, so that production route is not evaluated.

## How to produce books as an independent designer.

**Source:** [How to produce books as an independent designer.](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=98Co_mdwcPw) · **Video ID:** `98Co_mdwcPw`

Print production options, physical proofing, scale, and publication choices for independent game books.

### Choose production by scale and intent

Local short runs, print-on-demand, and offset printing serve different quantities and product goals; no route is universally best. The speakers compare small local zines, on-demand distribution, and larger offset runs with different costs and finishes.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Small runs limit inventory risk but raise unit cost; larger runs unlock finishes but require capital and confidence in demand.

**Design prompt:** Match production method to expected quantity, cash flow, product identity, and desired finish before requesting quotes.

[Supporting discussion, 22:32–23:51](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=98Co_mdwcPw&t=1352s)

### Use comparable books to request quotes

A novice can communicate desired print quality by pointing manufacturers to existing books and asking them to clarify missing specifications. The speaker gathered multiple quotes and used known books as references for size, quality, and paper weight.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Reference samples make expectations concrete but do not replace reviewing a formal specification or proof.

**Design prompt:** Send candidate printers a physical benchmark and request itemized specifications rather than guessing technical terms.

[Supporting discussion, 5:41–6:57](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=98Co_mdwcPw&t=341s)

### Small runs reduce uncertainty

Local small print runs offer a low-risk way to gain experience making and selling physical books before larger commitments. Order a modest batch, see the physical result, and repeat at a manageable scale.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Local short runs have higher unit costs, but reduce cash exposure and production uncertainty.

**Design prompt:** Consider a small local run before committing to a large order when production experience is limited.

[Supporting discussion, 25:40–26:52](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=98Co_mdwcPw&t=1540s)

### Test demand before a large run

Several inexpensive zines at a convention can reveal which ideas attract buyers before investing in artwork or a larger print run. Compare sales of small batches of different ideas, then use the response to guide investment.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Small runs cost more per copy, but let the designer learn about demand at lower risk.

**Design prompt:** Use a small convention offering to test demand before funding a larger production.

[Supporting discussion, 27:26–28:46](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=98Co_mdwcPw&t=1646s)

### Production expertise has value

A printer offering knowledgeable technical support can help a first-time publisher identify file problems and work through revisions. The speaker describes repeated technical feedback about color profiles and ink coverage from the chosen printer.

**Tradeoff to consider:** This support was helpful, but still required the author to learn unfamiliar production concepts and fix the files.

**Design prompt:** Ask potential printers what technical checks and feedback they provide when comparing quotes.

[Supporting discussion, 7:57–8:58](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=98Co_mdwcPw&t=477s)

### Brand and format align

A book's print quality and finish should fit its audience and brand; a polished hardback is not automatically the right product for every game. A deliberately home-printable zine format is presented as an identity choice, while a premium volume may justify its price through craftsmanship or included components.

**Tradeoff to consider:** A premium object can increase appeal but raises cost; inexpensive formats can feel authentic and accessible but limit presentation.

**Design prompt:** Choose physical format as part of the product concept and price it according to what readers value.

[Supporting discussion, 15:42–16:45](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=98Co_mdwcPw&t=942s)

**Episode-specific limits**

- Supplier prices, minimum quantities, tax issues, and print-on-demand availability are dated personal experience and require current checking.
- The episode uses examples from specific printers and formats; outcomes may differ with geography and specifications.

## A Billion Suns: Second Edition | Design Goals

**Source:** [A Billion Suns: Second Edition | Design Goals](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=694bwtpMcq8) · **Video ID:** `694bwtpMcq8`

Design principles for revising a complex space miniatures game and making its special experience more accessible.

### Diagnose barrier to play

A game may be well regarded yet difficult to get to the table because players need many models, tokens, and terrain before the first session. List setup and collection requirements as part of onboarding, not only the rules themselves.

**Tradeoff to consider:** More content can extend replay value after setup but make initial participation burdensome.

**Design prompt:** Document the minimum playable kit and reduce or stage the requirements for a first game.

[Supporting discussion, 3:05–4:20](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=694bwtpMcq8&t=185s)

### Revision can cut with confidence

Experience from an earlier edition can help a designer remove mechanics that were added to show creativity but do not support the game's identity. The designer describes second-game ambition producing unnecessary difficulty, then uses later experience to edit more decisively.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Cutting reduces cognitive load but must preserve the interactions that make the game distinctive.

**Design prompt:** For each inherited rule, ask whether it serves a core experience or merely demonstrates novelty.

[Supporting discussion, 6:37–7:51](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=694bwtpMcq8&t=397s)

### Let larger systems create moments

A memorable cinematic event may emerge from linked systems; encoding every desired moment as a bespoke rule can add clutter. The speaker contrasts an explicit jump-shock moment with the emergent story of multiple tables, objectives, ships, and blockade runners interacting.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Emergence supports replayable stories but requires the underlying systems to reliably create those moments.

**Design prompt:** Prototype interactions that might generate the desired scene before adding a dedicated rule for it.

[Supporting discussion, 16:06–17:18](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=694bwtpMcq8&t=966s)

### Give the core game room to work

A streamlined core can support varied experiences when its linked decisions already create tactical interest; peripheral mechanics may obscure that flow. The revision emphasizes deployment, jumping, launching fighters, and order-token decisions while removing micro-rules that compete for attention.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Streamlining clarifies the strategic puzzle but may remove flavor some players value.

**Design prompt:** Identify the core decision sequence and remove side procedures that do not strengthen its choices or theme.

[Supporting discussion, 17:14–18:19](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=694bwtpMcq8&t=1034s)

### Keep special mechanics visible

The designer names the distinctive features that should remain central, including scale, multi-table play, and a particular dice system. A short set of design principles becomes a filter for additions and cuts.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Strong focus protects identity but makes tradeoffs explicit when a mechanic serves other goals.

**Design prompt:** Write and revise a concise set of current design principles, then use them when evaluating changes.

[Supporting discussion, 23:39–24:56](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=694bwtpMcq8&t=1419s)

### Expand carefully in a later edition

A second edition can offer additional game modes when the first edition already establishes the game's core identity and the new material does not bury it. The designer proposes retaining an existing mode while adding a simpler fleet battle option, framed as expansion after the original premise is known.

**Tradeoff to consider:** A toolkit can serve more players but risks making the lead experience harder to find.

**Design prompt:** For expansions, label the core experience clearly and place optional modes so newcomers can start without learning the whole toolkit.

[Supporting discussion, 12:53–13:58](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=694bwtpMcq8&t=773s)

**Episode-specific limits**

- The second-edition plans are described during development and are the designer's intentions, not evidence of final success.
- Examples and evaluations are specific to A Billion Suns and the speakers' experience.

## What is your game's Core Loop?

**Source:** [What is your game's Core Loop?](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=vtqS8bLGJ3c) · **Video ID:** `vtqS8bLGJ3c`

Repeated decisions, feedback, skill and meaningful differentiation

### Core loop

Identify the repeated activity that makes a player want to begin another turn. The hosts contrast a familiar target-prioritize, move, attack, remove sequence with loops involving prediction, resource allocation or physical dexterity.

**Tradeoff to consider:** A familiar loop can still serve a deliberate hobby, narrative or streamlined mass-battle experience.

**Design prompt:** Describe what the player repeatedly decides, does and learns.

[Supporting discussion, 0:45–4:40](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=vtqS8bLGJ3c&t=45s)

### Strategic context

A round can create a shared decision context for its smaller activations. They discuss planning movement dials, allocating a control hand or using a battle board before resolving individual actions.

**Tradeoff to consider:** These are design interpretations of named games, not complete rules descriptions.

**Design prompt:** Consider whether the round changes the resources or information guiding the next decisions.

[Supporting discussion, 4:20–10:00](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=vtqS8bLGJ3c&t=260s)

### Cross-round consequences

Small loops can connect decisions across rounds. The hosts use Saga's fatigue markers as an example of a resource gained in one round and spent later.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Persistence can support continuity but should remain understandable.

**Design prompt:** Ask whether consequences feed the next decision rather than resetting into repetition.

[Supporting discussion, 8:50–10:25](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=vtqS8bLGJ3c&t=530s)

### Borrowing loops

An engaging core activity can come from another genre. They discuss a dexterity-style stacking loop in Space Gits and worker-placement-like allocation in Saga.

**Tradeoff to consider:** The borrowed activity changes what skill the player is demonstrating.

**Design prompt:** Borrow an interaction because it fits the desired experience, not merely to add another subsystem.

[Supporting discussion, 7:00–9:10](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=vtqS8bLGJ3c&t=420s)

### Feedback and mastery

A strong feedback loop lets the player connect a decision with its result and recognize improvement. Gaslands provides an immediate answer to whether a chosen movement template fits a space.

**Tradeoff to consider:** A winning result at the end of a game may not reveal whether an earlier tactical choice was correct.

**Design prompt:** Look for repeated opportunities to decide, see the result and adjust within a session.

[Supporting discussion, 10:10–16:00](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=vtqS8bLGJ3c&t=610s)

### Differentiation

If the central loop is familiar, the game needs a deliberate reason to choose it. The hosts identify smoother play, satisfying large armies, narrative and hobby context as possible reasons.

**Tradeoff to consider:** They do not claim every game needs a novel resolution or activation system.

**Design prompt:** State the intended improvement rather than adding novelty without purpose.

[Supporting discussion, 17:10–23:10](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=vtqS8bLGJ3c&t=1030s)

### Testing the loop

Test whether restarting the loop is appealing before relying on presentation or extra content. The prototype should contain the toy-like interaction that makes another round worth playing.

**Tradeoff to consider:** The hosts' criticism of particular games and initiative roll-offs is opinion, not a universal ban.

**Design prompt:** Ask what the player anticipates discovering next turn.

[Supporting discussion, 22:30–27:55](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=vtqS8bLGJ3c&t=1350s)

**Episode-specific limits**

- Strong preferences and polemical criticism of named games are design opinions; retain the reasoning without turning them into universal verdicts.

## Proper preparation for publishing your game.

**Source:** [Proper preparation for publishing your game.](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=LioJXWT6vd4) · **Video ID:** `LioJXWT6vd4`

Manuscript readiness, manufacturing files, proofing, and production pitfalls.

### Separate publication stages

Treat manuscript readiness, layout, manufacturing, and surrounding components as distinct problems that each need preparation. The conversation breaks publication into text editing, layout, physical production, and component design rather than treating a finished draft as a finished product.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Stage-by-stage work takes time but reveals different classes of problems before they become expensive.

**Design prompt:** Create separate readiness checks for text, layout, print files, and every physical component before production.

[Supporting discussion, 1:39–2:44](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=LioJXWT6vd4&t=99s)

### Get an outside manuscript read

An independent reader can find inconsistent terminology and unclear meaning that the author no longer notices. Ask someone to attempt a read and record where different words obscure whether the same game concept is intended.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Review takes coordination and may uncover substantial revisions, but can prevent confusion from reaching print.

**Design prompt:** Use a fresh reader to check terms and meaning before layout, then standardize the vocabulary they flag.

[Supporting discussion, 4:14–5:28](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=LioJXWT6vd4&t=254s)

### Learn print specifications from trusted sources

Physical book preparation contains technical conventions that authors may not know to ask about; tutorials and experienced printers can fill those gaps. The speakers cite learning about page numbering, endpapers, cover wraps, bleed, and file preparation through targeted instruction.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Self-education saves money but requires time and can miss errors; specialist support costs more but reduces uncertainty.

**Design prompt:** Identify unfamiliar print terms early and consult current printer documentation or specialist guidance before finalizing the layout.

[Supporting discussion, 3:08–4:12](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=LioJXWT6vd4&t=188s)

### Bleed and file setup

Insufficient bleed can force artwork revisions late in the process, especially for a wraparound hardback cover. Extend artwork beyond the trim edge according to the printer's required bleed so cutting variation does not expose blank edges.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Extra bleed space constrains usable artwork placement but protects finished edges.

**Design prompt:** Get exact trim and bleed specifications from the chosen printer before commissioning or completing cover art.

[Supporting discussion, 5:41–6:52](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=LioJXWT6vd4&t=341s)

### Proof and test assembled parts

Manufacturing and assembly can expose fit problems that appear correct in a flat design file, so test components physically and repeatedly. The episode gives examples of mirrored double-sided parts and slots that need taper because friction differs by material.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Prototypes consume time and sample materials, but catch issues before mass production.

**Design prompt:** Print and assemble representative samples using the intended material and tolerances before approving production.

[Supporting discussion, 24:28–25:33](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=LioJXWT6vd4&t=1468s)

### Match support to experience

A first-time publisher can reduce risk by selecting a manufacturer who offers technical guidance and by listening to the production specialist. The speakers describe printers identifying ink saturation and other file issues, then advising on corrections and proofs.

**Tradeoff to consider:** More supportive service may cost more, but a low quote can become expensive if the designer must catch every hidden technical issue alone.

**Design prompt:** Evaluate vendor support and proof quality alongside price, and budget for specialist help where the team lacks production experience.

[Supporting discussion, 20:23–21:33](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=LioJXWT6vd4&t=1223s)

**Episode-specific limits**

- The discussion is based on the speakers' own production experiences; technical requirements vary by printer, material, and current workflow.
- Specific vendor recommendations and quoted costs should be treated as dated personal experience.

## How to go From Notebook To Tabletop

**Source:** [How to go From Notebook To Tabletop](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=IgDdV11MlGE) · **Video ID:** `IgDdV11MlGE`

Idea selection, prototyping, iteration and early feedback

### Idea selection

Capture the exciting question before attempting to finish the whole game. Mike records a title, date, question and rough bullets. Glenn keeps a brief idea list and revisits it after the initial excitement fades.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Immediate physical prototyping and deliberate incubation suit different working styles.

**Design prompt:** Record why an idea is interesting, then choose an incubation or rapid prototype process that works for you.

[Supporting discussion, 1:50–8:50](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=IgDdV11MlGE&t=110s)

### Salvaging work

A failed game can still yield a reusable mechanic or useful design knowledge. Glenn reduces abandoned prototypes to short mechanical ideas for later projects.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Salvage does not require finishing a game whose central experience fails.

**Design prompt:** Archive useful components and lessons before moving on.

[Supporting discussion, 4:40–6:30](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=IgDdV11MlGE&t=280s)

### Prototyping

Writing and physically playing a crude prototype exposes connections that only seemed to work in your head. Cross-references force phase rules to connect; moving components reveals impossible states and missing procedures.

**Tradeoff to consider:** A coherent imagined system can conceal missing links.

**Design prompt:** Write enough connected rules to run a complete sequence and put the pieces on the table.

[Supporting discussion, 8:40–15:30](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=IgDdV11MlGE&t=520s)

### Temporary mechanics

A prototype can use familiar supporting mechanics while testing the part that makes it interesting. Mike temporarily borrows a known shooting resolution system when the experimental feature lies elsewhere.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Scaffolding still has to interact with the new idea; borrowing is a prototype technique, not proof of a finished design.

**Design prompt:** Make the experimental question playable without first reinventing every subsystem.

[Supporting discussion, 11:00–13:40](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=IgDdV11MlGE&t=660s)

### Prototype tools

Use tools that let you change the design comfortably and quickly. Paper, erasable pens, card sleeves, digital layouts, printouts and reference cards serve different practical needs.

**Tradeoff to consider:** There is no uniquely correct software or physical process.

**Design prompt:** Choose a process that makes rules and components easy to revise.

[Supporting discussion, 15:40–20:30](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=IgDdV11MlGE&t=940s)

### Early testing

Show a functioning prototype to a trusted person early enough to expose obvious choices and missing fun. After a few solo runs, another person can identify a supposedly clever system with only one meaningful option.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Know enough about the intended experience to judge whether feedback is relevant.

**Design prompt:** Ask whether the central experience could be fun, rather than demanding polish from an early draft.

[Supporting discussion, 20:30–25:50](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=IgDdV11MlGE&t=1230s)

### Protecting the concept

Distinguish the experience you care about from the particular mechanic currently expressing it. Glenn stresses testing the heart of the game; Mike notes that a supposedly central idea may become expendable when something more enjoyable emerges.

**Tradeoff to consider:** The hosts differ in how firmly they protect the early mechanical spine.

**Design prompt:** State which experience matters while keeping implementation open to revision.

[Supporting discussion, 24:40–27:00](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=IgDdV11MlGE&t=1480s)

### Iteration and versions

Keep prototypes cheap and disposable, and use versions to make large changes safe and understandable. Major version changes can mark replacement of an entire movement or casting system while preserving an earlier working game.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Polished artwork and expensive production can create attachment to weak components.
