**Design prompt:** Archive old versions, identify the version testers used, and postpone production polish.

[Supporting discussion, 27:10–35:00](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=IgDdV11MlGE&t=1630s)

**Differences in approach or unresolved questions**

- Glenn deliberately lets ideas cool; Mike often needs an immediate physical prototype before setting an idea aside.
- Glenn emphasizes the central mechanical spine; Mike is willing to abandon an initially central mechanic if a better experience emerges.

**Episode-specific limits**

- Personal design processes, not a mandatory sequence or prescribed number of prototypes.

## How to go From Pitch to Product

**Source:** [How to go From Pitch to Product](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=EaSDsNv21Xg) · **Video ID:** `EaSDsNv21Xg`

Choosing a publishing route and preparing a pitch

### Product scope

Choose a first product whose production demands fit the skills and resources you actually have. Glenn selected a relatively manageable game rather than the largest idea on his list.

**Tradeoff to consider:** A larger project can work with the right team and experience; small is not an absolute rule.

**Design prompt:** Assess components, art, logistics and the desired outcome before committing to a product.

[Supporting discussion, 1:25–6:05](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=EaSDsNv21Xg&t=85s)

### Defining success

Define success for the project before judging its funding total. A modest campaign can meet its creator's aim by covering production costs and creating stock for later sales.

**Tradeoff to consider:** A hobby milestone, a business launch and a living wage require different outcomes.

**Design prompt:** Write down what the project must accomplish for you.

[Supporting discussion, 2:50–5:20](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=EaSDsNv21Xg&t=170s)

### Crowdfunding

Crowdfunding can connect discovery of a game with an immediate commitment to support it. Glenn describes exposure and a clear purchasing action as important to his small publishing operation.

**Tradeoff to consider:** This is his experience at the time; the episode does not establish current platform reach, fees or likely profitability.

**Design prompt:** Compare a route's audience and commitment mechanism with its production responsibilities.

[Supporting discussion, 7:50–11:40](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=EaSDsNv21Xg&t=470s)

### Publishing route

A publishing route is also a choice about which jobs you want to do. Mike preferred a publisher to take on physical production, distribution and marketing so he could focus on designing games alongside his day job.

**Tradeoff to consider:** His route reflects personal priorities rather than proof that publishing partners suit every designer.

**Design prompt:** List the work you want to retain and the work you need a partner to handle.

[Supporting discussion, 11:20–14:00](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=EaSDsNv21Xg&t=680s)

### Pitch preparation

Explain the game's appeal and show that a real, coherent manuscript exists. Mike used a short energetic abstract, a chapter outline and a playable rules sample.

**Tradeoff to consider:** His approximate 200-word abstract describes his own approach, not a universal publisher requirement.

**Design prompt:** Prepare an experience-led pitch supported by concrete rules and the recipient's requested materials.

[Supporting discussion, 14:00–17:20](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=EaSDsNv21Xg&t=840s)

### Publisher fit

Research the publisher's actual product line and submission process. Mike narrowed a publisher list to companies that handled compatible standalone games and asked whether submissions were accepted.

**Tradeoff to consider:** A rejection can reflect fit, timing or the publisher's financial choices.

**Design prompt:** Make a targeted, respectful approach and expect repeated rejection.

[Supporting discussion, 17:30–22:30](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=EaSDsNv21Xg&t=1050s)

**Episode-specific limits**

- Historical personal publishing experience. Named services, prices, fundraising amounts and commercial expectations are not current benchmarks.

## How to Playtest Tabletop Games

**Source:** [How to Playtest Tabletop Games](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=WxmsMPyCmWk) · **Video ID:** `WxmsMPyCmWk`

Observing play, asking questions and interpreting feedback

### Testing purpose

Playtesting tests the connected experience, including interesting decisions, feelings and whether the game functions at all. Physical play reveals incompatible rules and sequences that imagined or solo play may conceal.

**Tradeoff to consider:** A mechanically interesting draft is not automatically enjoyable.

**Design prompt:** Record the question each session is meant to answer.

[Supporting discussion, 1:50–4:05](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=WxmsMPyCmWk&t=110s)

### Observer role

Observe players attempting to use the rules instead of immediately guiding them. When a tester asks how a rule works, Mike asks how they think it works and watches them consult the text.

**Tradeoff to consider:** A spoken explanation can hide rules that the designer never actually wrote.

**Design prompt:** Sometimes remain outside the game so you can watch and take notes.

[Supporting discussion, 5:50–8:20](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=WxmsMPyCmWk&t=350s)

### Emotional feedback

Watch the precise moment that excitement, confusion, disappointment or disengagement occurs. A player asks to do something the fiction suggests, encounters an unexpected rule or starts checking their phone.

**Tradeoff to consider:** An unmet expectation is a signal to investigate, not an automatic requirement to add a subsystem.

**Design prompt:** Ask what the player expected and whether the mismatch serves the intended experience.

[Supporting discussion, 8:10–13:30](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=WxmsMPyCmWk&t=490s)

### Interview technique

Ask about the weakest or most disappointing part rather than relying on a general enjoyment rating. Politeness can produce positive answers that do not predict whether someone will choose to play again.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Harsh wording is not the objective; specific and honest observations are.

**Design prompt:** Ask which moment was worst, confusing or contrary to expectations.

[Supporting discussion, 8:50–10:50](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=WxmsMPyCmWk&t=530s)

### Test groups

Use both experienced repeat testers and fresh players. Experienced players help isolate changes and remember rejected approaches; newcomers reveal whether the current edition teaches itself.

**Tradeoff to consider:** The first encounter happens only once per tester.

**Design prompt:** Continue recruiting newcomers as the rules change.

[Supporting discussion, 14:50–18:30](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=WxmsMPyCmWk&t=890s)

### Cognitive load

Repeatedly forgotten rules may need removal, rewriting or a different presentation. Glenn suggests reconsidering a rule that must be explained more than three times in one session.

**Tradeoff to consider:** That number is a personal diagnostic heuristic, not an empirical threshold.

**Design prompt:** Log reminders and forgotten rules alongside balance outcomes.

[Supporting discussion, 16:10–17:20](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=WxmsMPyCmWk&t=970s)

### Feedback ownership

Accept the reported problem without automatically adopting the suggested fix. A tester's bad experience identifies a design problem; the designer still chooses among possible remedies.

**Tradeoff to consider:** The hosts also welcome useful ideas from testers rather than literally rejecting every suggestion.

**Design prompt:** Keep problem, proposed solution and final design decision as separate notes.

[Supporting discussion, 19:40–23:00](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=WxmsMPyCmWk&t=1180s)

### Sustainable testing

Respect testers, test other people's games and recognize that a deadline eventually ends iteration. Reliable testers provide repeated attention and learn the design; testing others' work teaches useful feedback habits.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Continued testing can improve a design indefinitely, but can also prevent release or expose that a project should be shelved.

**Design prompt:** Make sessions purposeful and keep a release or shelving decision distinct from polishing forever.

[Supporting discussion, 3:50–27:40](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=WxmsMPyCmWk&t=230s)

**Episode-specific limits**

- The episode's suggested game counts and reminder threshold are the hosts' practical heuristics, not universal readiness criteria.

## Playtesting for Tabletop Miniatures Games

**Source:** [Playtesting for Tabletop Miniatures Games](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=SVZafm_WqkM) · **Video ID:** `SVZafm_WqkM`

Mental, solo, observed and blind tests; audience and broken sessions

### Testing methods

Mental analysis, solo testing, observed group testing and blind testing answer different questions. A logical contradiction may be fixed without a full game; solo play checks basic functioning; external players expose interaction and interpretation.

**Tradeoff to consider:** No single method replaces the others.

**Design prompt:** Choose the lightest test that answers the current question.

[Supporting discussion, 2:50–9:50](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=SVZafm_WqkM&t=170s)

### Solo testing

Run enough solo tests to avoid wasting other people's time on immediately collapsing rules. Play all sides, commentate decisions and note missing procedures or unfair assumptions.

**Tradeoff to consider:** The solo tester can unconsciously favor a side and cannot reproduce all social effects.

**Design prompt:** Use solo runs for coherence and coverage, then involve other players.

[Supporting discussion, 5:00–9:10](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=SVZafm_WqkM&t=300s)

### Blind rules testing

A rulebook must work when the designer is absent. Players may struggle, invent a ruling, stop playing or reject a game in ways they would avoid in front of its creator.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Their account of confusion can be vague after the event.

**Design prompt:** Ask testers to use the supplied rules without your assistance.

[Supporting discussion, 9:10–12:40](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=SVZafm_WqkM&t=550s)

### Recording tests

A recording can reveal the exact assumption that sends play away from the intended rules. Watching an impasse and the players' improvised solution preserves details they may later forget.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Recordings and detailed reports require willing testers; follow-up questions can still improve written reports.

**Design prompt:** Capture the point of confusion and its consequences, not only the final rating.

[Supporting discussion, 12:00–16:15](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=SVZafm_WqkM&t=720s)

### Recruiting testers

Clubs, conventions, stores and communities can provide fresh testers and sustained relationships. A way to contact the designer and discuss specific reports supports follow-up; committed testers may later become advocates.

**Tradeoff to consider:** The named channels and paid-service prices are historical examples.

**Design prompt:** Give remote testers a clear way to report their version and ask questions.

[Supporting discussion, 16:00–21:00](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=SVZafm_WqkM&t=960s)

### Audience fit

Be honest about the experience and accept that some players will dislike it. A very high player count can imply a party game, while a harsh elimination game may attract a different audience.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Trying to please everyone can dilute the experience that some players would love.

**Design prompt:** Test whether presentation and player expectations match the actual game.

[Supporting discussion, 22:00–26:30](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=SVZafm_WqkM&t=1320s)

### Elimination

A token activity after elimination is not necessarily better than an honestly harsh game. Glenn describes choosing avoidable elimination in his cooperative survival game rather than a weak consolation role.

**Tradeoff to consider:** This is a contextual alternative to Mike's usual concern about elimination and disengagement.

**Design prompt:** Compare whether the proposed consolation activity is genuinely worth the player's time.

[Supporting discussion, 23:30–24:30](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=SVZafm_WqkM&t=1410s)

### Stopping a failed session

A collapsed test can become a useful design discussion instead of an obligation to finish a broken game. Stop moving models, identify what failed and discuss the system with trusted testers.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Pushing through additional hours can consume goodwill without answering a new question.

**Design prompt:** Record why play stopped and turn that into the next design task.

[Supporting discussion, 26:30–28:10](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=SVZafm_WqkM&t=1590s)

**Differences in approach or unresolved questions**

- Mike generally avoids player elimination; Glenn's survival-game example accepts it when it fits the promised experience and the alternative role is unrewarding.

**Episode-specific limits**

- Personal anecdotes and historical recruitment/service examples are not present-day market claims.

## Why make it a Minis game? For the Skillz!

**Source:** [Why make it a Minis game? For the Skillz!](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=Fix8CfDYsd4) · **Video ID:** `Fix8CfDYsd4`

Choosing miniatures as a medium by considering spatial affordances and the player skills a game tests.

### Choose the medium deliberately

A promising game idea may fit cards, a board, or live action better than miniatures; decide whether the central play depends on tabletop space and physical figures. Miniature games support measured spatial relationships and can incorporate diverse physical objects as terrain or markers.

**Tradeoff to consider:** The medium enables physical situations but introduces handling, measurement, and hobby work.

**Design prompt:** Before committing, describe what miniatures and three-dimensional space contribute to the main decisions.

[Supporting discussion, 2:14–3:24](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=Fix8CfDYsd4&t=134s)

### Preserve meaningful spatial relations

If the key interaction no longer depends on distance, facing, line of sight, or relative position, miniatures may be an unnecessary representation. Repeatedly abstracting range, firing arcs, and flanking can remove the physical relationship that gives figures gameplay meaning.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Abstraction can streamline play, but may leave a miniature shell around a card or token game.

**Design prompt:** Check whether changing a figure's position changes a decision; if not, test a medium that represents the real decision more directly.

[Supporting discussion, 17:37–18:53](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=Fix8CfDYsd4&t=1057s)

### Separate skill types

Designers can distinguish physical skills, transferable spatial skills, and game-specific strategic skills, then decide which ones the player should exercise. The snooker example separates cue technique, angle judgment, and strategic placement; adaptations abstract some skills while retaining others.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Transferring a physical skill can deepen embodiment but risks testing dexterity rather than the intended strategic judgment.

**Design prompt:** Name the player skills the game is meant to reward and ensure the physical procedures actually test those skills.

[Supporting discussion, 8:59–10:19](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=Fix8CfDYsd4&t=539s)

### Put tension in the central activity

If the interesting experience is an abstracted choice, consider making that choice itself tactile or tense rather than forcing miniatures to carry all the physical meaning. The speakers suggest using a direct dexterity moment for a tense monster encounter instead of resolving everything through abstract dice around figures.

**Tradeoff to consider:** A physical challenge can increase immediacy but shifts the game toward dexterity and may not fit all players.

**Design prompt:** Locate the game's emotional or tactical focus, then decide whether the player should directly perform a small part of it.

[Supporting discussion, 19:42–20:56](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=Fix8CfDYsd4&t=1182s)

### Respect the cinematic scale

A scene can be visually miniature-friendly only if its important action is physically expressed at tabletop scale; some concepts demand a different scale or abstraction. The discussion contrasts an internalized wizard duel with a physically dynamic psychic fight involving movement, obstacles, visibility and range.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Scale choices can clarify action but may sacrifice detailed miniatures or hobby presentation.

**Design prompt:** Use the scale and objects that make the action legible and interesting, even if that means counters, maps, or a different game format.

[Supporting discussion, 23:50–25:02](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=Fix8CfDYsd4&t=1430s)

### Test the counterfactual medium

A strong test of medium fit is to imagine removing the miniatures and ask whether the game loses its central tension or merely an attractive shell. The speakers discuss designs where spatial relationships remain essential versus games whose meaningful choices happen in an abstracted system elsewhere.

**Tradeoff to consider:** This can prevent wasted production effort but may discount the social and hobby value players find in figures.

**Design prompt:** Prototype a paper, card, or token version to compare whether spatial play changes the experience materially.

[Supporting discussion, 15:53–17:05](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=Fix8CfDYsd4&t=953s)

**Episode-specific limits**

- The speakers frame their medium criteria as guidance and taste, not a formal test that decides every design.
- Some examples are film, videogame, and personal hobby analogies rather than tested miniature-game comparisons.

## Why make it a Minis game? To end!

**Source:** [Why make it a Minis game? To end!](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=_QDyNaRPXHU) · **Video ID:** `_QDyNaRPXHU`

Pacing arcs in games where resources and pieces decline rather than grow.

### Laddering and zeroing

Game endings can become more engaging through increasing capability (laddering) or through narrowing forces toward a decisive target (zeroing). The episode distinguishes an engine that grows stronger from a war game where options disappear as pieces are removed but decisions become more consequential.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Laddering gives players a sense of building power; zeroing can create focus but may feel like loss of agency if choices collapse too quickly.

**Design prompt:** Identify whether the game should build capacity or concentrate attention over time, then align its progression and ending with that arc.

[Supporting discussion, 25:28–26:40](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=_QDyNaRPXHU&t=1528s)

### Fewer choices can mean higher stakes

The endgame need not offer more options than the start to feel interesting; a smaller set of choices can matter more when the outcome is near. The chess example is used to contrast broad opening choices with the focused significance of endgame moves.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Concentration intensifies decisions but can remove too many tools if the game reaches the end state abruptly.

**Design prompt:** Evaluate decision significance and consequence, not just action count, when assessing whether the game arc remains engaging.

[Supporting discussion, 6:03–7:16](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=_QDyNaRPXHU&t=363s)

### Loss can focus the tactical question

A diminishing force can create a compelling arc when players begin with broad or obscure possibilities and progressively zero in on a concrete objective. As the target becomes clearer and forces dwindle, each remaining decision gains importance and the game narrows toward closure.

**Tradeoff to consider:** A dramatic narrowing can support tension but may frustrate players if early losses eliminate their ability to affect the result.

**Design prompt:** Track whether attrition sharpens the central question or simply removes a player's meaningful participation.

[Supporting discussion, 6:35–7:57](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=_QDyNaRPXHU&t=395s)

### Make final actions feel earned

A final decisive act can feel powerful through its context and timing, even when the player has less remaining capability than earlier. The speakers compare built-up power with the significance of being the last figure to fall or deliver a decisive outcome.

**Tradeoff to consider:** A dramatic final moment can make a loss memorable, but narrative framing cannot compensate for a game that was unenjoyable before that moment.

**Design prompt:** Design late-game objectives or events that give remaining choices clear consequences, without assuming players will enjoy simply losing models.

[Supporting discussion, 26:11–27:27](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=_QDyNaRPXHU&t=1571s)

### Physical persistence can support a different arc

A game where pieces stay in place can build complexity through evolving relationships rather than through movement or attrition. The speakers use Go as a prompt for thinking about stationary objects whose placement continues to matter as the board develops.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Persistent pieces preserve presence, but designers must create changing interactions and a credible end condition.

**Design prompt:** Explore whether a static or growing spatial pattern better supports the intended tension than standard attrition.

[Supporting discussion, 28:13–29:28](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=_QDyNaRPXHU&t=1693s)

### Ask whether the medium improves the game

A concept that can use miniatures is not necessarily better as a miniature game; test whether open physical space contributes more than a discrete board or cards would. The discussion asks whether terrain and unconstrained placement create meaningful relationships or merely make the design harder to operate.

**Tradeoff to consider:** A miniature format supports varied objects and spatial nuance but imposes extra measuring and handling.

**Design prompt:** Compare a tabletop prototype with a grid or card version and retain miniatures only if the spatial affordance improves play.

[Supporting discussion, 29:06–30:20](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=_QDyNaRPXHU&t=1746s)

**Episode-specific limits**

- 'Laddering' and 'zeroing' are the speakers' terminology in this discussion; no formal theory or measured validation is supplied.
- The examples discuss genre and personal design philosophy rather than controlled comparisons.

## Why make it a Minis game? To grow!

**Source:** [Why make it a Minis game? To grow!](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=4dhCZBpspGs) · **Video ID:** `4dhCZBpspGs`

Skill development, list-building, and hobby loops as possible reasons to use a miniature-game format.

### Practice and mastery loop

Miniature games often appeal to players who want to improve over repeated sessions, and their complexity can support a long learning arc. Players learn through games, roster choices, and reflection on tactical errors across multiple sessions.

**Tradeoff to consider:** A deep mastery loop rewards commitment but is less suitable for one-shot or casual play.

**Design prompt:** Decide whether the target audience wants repeatable mastery or quick, occasional sessions before choosing rules depth.

[Supporting discussion, 16:44–17:58](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=4dhCZBpspGs&t=1004s)

### List-building as a learning layer

Roster construction can express knowledge gained outside the match and materially affect outcomes, extending player skill beyond piloting. A player uses experience from prior games to choose units or equipment before play, while many boxed games keep setup fixed.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Pre-game strategy adds breadth but creates a homework burden and can reward collection or optimization over table skill.

**Design prompt:** If list building is central, make its rules and learning resources clear; otherwise provide ready-made forces to focus learning on play.

[Supporting discussion, 7:29–8:50](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=4dhCZBpspGs&t=449s)

### Gamify the hobby loop

When a game already asks players to collect, assemble, and paint miniatures, designers can make those activities part of a broader sense of progression. The speakers suggest connecting the hobby preparation loop to roster building and player ownership rather than leaving it disconnected from play.

**Tradeoff to consider:** This can make the hobby more rewarding but may increase barriers for players who do not want to build or paint.

**Design prompt:** Decide whether roster revision and model preparation between games are part of the intended experience, then support that loop deliberately.

[Supporting discussion, 18:49–20:01](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=4dhCZBpspGs&t=1129s)

### Match complexity to audience

A high-complexity game can suit experienced players seeking subtle interactions, while a social one-shot game may be better served by a simpler boxed format. The discussion connects measurement and line-of-sight overhead with the different expectations of repeated hobby play versus casual group play.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Complexity can deepen mastery but also imposes study and table setup costs.

**Design prompt:** Choose rules weight based on how often players will return and what kind of learning commitment they want to make.

[Supporting discussion, 17:47–19:04](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=4dhCZBpspGs&t=1067s)

### List building is optional

List construction is a common feature but not a defining requirement; designers can supply fixed miniatures and a board when that better serves the experience. The format can be adjusted to offer configurable forces, fixed kits, or grid-based play depending on the design goal.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Fixed forces reduce preparation and balance space but limit customization and emergent starting variety.

**Design prompt:** Treat list building as a design choice with costs, not an automatic genre requirement.

[Supporting discussion, 22:12–23:25](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=4dhCZBpspGs&t=1332s)

### Account for game sprawl

A miniature game can extend beyond the table into study, collection, and preparation; the designer should decide whether that is part of the intended value. Rules for measurement and line of sight, roster configuration, and model acquisition may create a sustained hobby relationship or unwanted overhead.

**Tradeoff to consider:** A broader hobby loop builds investment but can make the game hard to start and maintain.

**Design prompt:** Map what players must do between sessions and remove or support the steps that do not serve the desired experience.

[Supporting discussion, 20:33–21:50](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=4dhCZBpspGs&t=1233s)

**Episode-specific limits**

- The episode offers design reasoning and examples, not evidence that mastery or list-building games outperform simpler formats.
- Learning curves and hobby expectations vary by audience and product model.

## Why make it a Minis game? For the looks!

**Source:** [Why make it a Minis game? For the looks!](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=_xP5uIsYg8Q) · **Video ID:** `_xP5uIsYg8Q`

Visual composition, player ownership, and co-creation as reasons to use miniatures.

### Visual play as a core pleasure

Some players value the ability to compose, inspect, and reframe an evolving three-dimensional scene as part of the game experience. The table becomes a slowly changing physical picture; players can move their viewpoint and notice new compositions during play.

**Tradeoff to consider:** This rewards models, terrain, and presentation but requires physical objects and may not matter to players focused on competition.

**Design prompt:** If visual discovery is central, design scenarios and terrain to create changing, readable compositions rather than treating appearance as packaging only.

[Supporting discussion, 4:21–5:39](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=_xP5uIsYg8Q&t=261s)

### Player ownership through customization

Open-ended physical objects let players make a shared game world their own through painting, modeling, and storytelling. Players can use the same rules with different figures, terrain, and invented identities, producing distinct table scenes.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Customization creates attachment and self-expression but can add cost, time, and uneven visual readiness.

**Design prompt:** Support compatible proxies and personal modeling choices when player authorship is part of the intended appeal.

[Supporting discussion, 14:26–15:37](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=_xP5uIsYg8Q&t=866s)

### The game as a performed art

The physical game product can function as a tool for a shared creative performance rather than as the final artwork itself. Rules and components enable players to enact decisions and create a one-time story together at the table.

**Tradeoff to consider:** The result can be meaningful and unique but is ephemeral and depends on player participation.

**Design prompt:** Design components and rules to enable memorable shared moments, not only to look polished when displayed.

[Supporting discussion, 10:09–11:39](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=_xP5uIsYg8Q&t=609s)

### Let components express identity

A miniature game's flexibility can let players express identity through chosen models and tactics, while other game forms may center direct social expression instead. The speakers contrast figures as proxies for the player with social deduction where conversation itself is the meaningful interaction.

**Tradeoff to consider:** A strong representational focus may deepen immersion for some and distance others who prefer direct social play.

**Design prompt:** Identify where players should invest attention and expression, then let the medium reinforce that focus.

[Supporting discussion, 15:35–17:01](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=_xP5uIsYg8Q&t=935s)

### Customize only when it serves the experience

Hobby work can be a valued part of the experience, but it need not be made compulsory if the game's purpose is simply an interesting interaction between players. One speaker values painting and modeling as an intrinsic hobby; the other prioritizes delivering a focused experience with minimal barriers.

**Tradeoff to consider:** A hobby-rich product supports ownership but raises entry effort; a minimal kit is easier to start but offers less personal making.

**Design prompt:** Make customization a supported option and be clear whether it is central or optional to enjoying the game.

[Supporting discussion, 20:52–22:13](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=_xP5uIsYg8Q&t=1252s)

### Choose visuals that fit scale

The visual metaphor can justify counters or maps over miniatures when the subject's meaningful scale is too large for figures to represent convincingly. The discussion of fleet warfare finds that broad maps and concealed counters better express the scale and bluffing interaction than tiny ship models.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Abstraction can improve the visual logic and decision focus but gives up detailed miniature scenery.

**Design prompt:** Let the intended cinematic view determine whether the game needs figures, tokens, or map-based markers.

[Supporting discussion, 24:58–26:19](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=_xP5uIsYg8Q&t=1498s)

**Differences in approach or unresolved questions**

- Mike treats creating and customizing the miniature scene as an intrinsic part of the appeal; Glenn emphasizes interesting human interaction with as few barriers and components as possible. Glenn also argues that shared creative performance characterizes games broadly, rather than uniquely defining miniatures.

**Episode-specific limits**

- The episode presents competing personal views about visual storytelling and hobby investment; these are not claims about all players.
- Examples are illustrative comparisons and not empirical audience research.

## The first section of the book on how to design better miniatures games!

**Source:** [The first section of the book on how to design better miniatures games!](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=7DTonRcCfXY) · **Video ID:** `7DTonRcCfXY`

The opening book section frames miniature games around physical space, openness of components, sources of fun, and deliberate design scope.

### Define the medium

A useful genre definition should identify the design constraint that shapes play, rather than rely on market labels such as skirmish or war game. The hosts settle on non-discrete miniature games: positions can vary continuously and the physical relationship between models and terrain matters in three dimensions.

**Tradeoff to consider:** A sharper boundary helps designers reason and communicate, but excludes adjacent formats and should not imply that excluded games are inferior.

**Design prompt:** When describing a game, state what its table-space requires designers to support; use that definition to focus design questions and explain where innovation may cross the boundary.

[Supporting discussion, 5:52–6:06](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=7DTonRcCfXY&t=352s)

### Support physical ambiguity

Open miniature play obliges designers to account for arbitrary objects and placements that board games can abstract away. Players may bring unusual terrain or move models by fractional distances; rules need to let physical objects acquire a clear game meaning through agreed properties and interactions.

**Tradeoff to consider:** This freedom creates emergent play and personalization, while increasing adjudication and clarity demands for line of sight, blocking, and terrain.

**Design prompt:** Write robust general procedures for classifying objects and resolving spatial relations, then make table expectations explicit so openness does not turn into disputes.

[Supporting discussion, 11:17–11:23](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=7DTonRcCfXY&t=677s)

### Physical form carries rules meaning

Terrain geometry is part of the rules interface because shape, openings, height, and placement alter what models can do. The transcript contrasts abstract board spaces with a hollow building whose windows and physical structure affect visibility and movement.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Using tangible geometry deepens spatial play, but exact physical measurements and model/terrain variation can burden setup and adjudication.

**Design prompt:** Specify which physical features matter and provide consistent conventions for them; test with varied terrain pieces rather than assuming a single idealized board.

[Supporting discussion, 7:21–7:29](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=7DTonRcCfXY&t=441s)

### Fun has multiple sources

A game can serve several kinds of enjoyment at once; treating one style of play as illegitimate can unnecessarily narrow its audience. The hosts map intrinsic/extrinsic and in-world/out-of-world enjoyment, including fiction, system mastery, hobby craft, social recognition, and competition.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Serving multiple motivations can enrich the experience, but the framework is a design lens rather than a requirement that every game maximize every category.

**Design prompt:** Name the intended sources of fun, inspect which are supported by rules and which arise through hobby or community, and make exclusions deliberate.

[Supporting discussion, 26:12–26:18](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=7DTonRcCfXY&t=1572s)

### Avoid ranking player motivations

Competitive, narrative, social, and hobby motivations should not be ranked as inherently good or bad design goals. The hosts reject a moralized reading of the fun quadrants and note that the same person can value different dimensions at different times.

**Tradeoff to consider:** A broad framework encourages inclusion, though a focused game may validly prioritize some motivations and omit others.

**Design prompt:** Describe the intended play experience without dismissing other player preferences; use audience fit, not moral judgment, to assess a design choice.

[Supporting discussion, 30:48–30:53](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=7DTonRcCfXY&t=1848s)

### Dramatic focus as a pruning test

Rules should earn their place by supporting the fantasy or experience the game promises. The hosts preview dramatic focus as a framework for asking why a mechanic exists and when it should be removed, without detailing its full method here.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Pruning can sharpen the experience but risks removing rules that support another audience or a secondary source of fun.

**Design prompt:** For each subsystem, state the player-facing experience it enables and consider removal when that connection is weak; consult the fuller framework before using it as a strict test.

[Supporting discussion, 34:28–34:33](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=7DTonRcCfXY&t=2068s)

**Differences in approach or unresolved questions**

- The hosts describe their definition of non-discrete miniature games as a useful framing, not a universal taxonomy; they acknowledge that other kinds of games can sit outside it while remaining worthwhile.

**Episode-specific limits**

- This is an introductory discussion tied to a book section; the episode previews but does not fully explain the dramatic-focus framework.
- The transcript contains ASR errors around technical terminology; ‘non-discrete’ is clear from context, but isolated malformed terms are not treated as separate concepts.

## The book on how to design better miniatures games!

**Source:** [The book on how to design better miniatures games!](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=9vwVLEKlweA) · **Video ID:** `9vwVLEKlweA`

The hosts explain the book's intended audience and structure, research standards, scope decisions, and collaborative revision process.

### Teach in a deliberate sequence

A design reference becomes more usable when concepts build from foundations toward detailed systems in a reasoned order. The hosts describe organizing material from overview concepts through the mechanical raw materials to particular systems; restructuring exposed prerequisites such as terrain and line of sight.

**Tradeoff to consider:** A logical progression helps new designers, but some topics remain useful as standalone references and not every reader needs a linear course.

**Design prompt:** Map prerequisite relationships while outlining; place foundational concepts before systems that rely on them, and retain clear chapter entry points for targeted consultation.

[Supporting discussion, 2:06–2:14](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=9vwVLEKlweA&t=126s)

### Design chapters for lookup

A reference book can support both sequential learning and just-in-time problem solving when chapters have self-contained questions and examples. The hosts say chapters examine a mechanic or design area independently, so a reader stuck on card mechanics can consult that chapter without following one sustained argument.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Standalone chapters invite selective reading, while cross-cutting foundational material may need repetition or explicit links to preserve context.

**Design prompt:** Give each chapter a clear problem, useful entry context, and examples; identify dependencies when a chapter assumes earlier concepts.

[Supporting discussion, 5:02–5:11](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=9vwVLEKlweA&t=302s)

### Research examples against primary rules

Specific game examples are more trustworthy when checked against the actual rules rather than recalled loosely. The hosts distinguish casual mentions in videos from researched case studies written with the source rules available, covering how a mechanic works and its strengths and weaknesses.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Careful case studies improve fairness and accuracy but take time; the book cannot provide one for every mechanic.

**Design prompt:** For claims about a named game's procedure, consult its rules and explain both function and consequences; label broad recollection as such and limit the number of deep cases.

[Supporting discussion, 7:16–7:22](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=9vwVLEKlweA&t=436s)

### Balance canonical and unusual examples

A design reference can show both familiar implementations and less visible experiments to expand a reader's option set. The hosts deliberately pair major games with independent or unusual designs, using examples to identify emerging approaches rather than only repeat established conventions.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Novel examples can inspire but may not be proven or broadly applicable; familiar cases are easier to contextualize but can reinforce defaults.

**Design prompt:** Choose examples for contrasting design approaches and state their context; treat a novel case as an option to investigate rather than a validated best practice.

[Supporting discussion, 8:54–9:01](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=9vwVLEKlweA&t=534s)

### Scope through constraint

A limited word count can force a project to clarify its central contribution and remove adjacent material that dilutes it. The authors cut process-of-making material so the book could focus on the distinctive mechanical and philosophical concerns of miniature games.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Narrow scope makes the through-line coherent but leaves valuable neighboring topics outside the work.

**Design prompt:** When a draft exceeds its budget, identify the promised subject and cut material that does not advance it; signpost where omitted questions belong if useful.

[Supporting discussion, 22:44–22:50](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=9vwVLEKlweA&t=1364s)

### Reorder after drafting

Writing reveals dependencies that an outline may miss, so structure should remain revisable as arguments become concrete. The hosts describe moving chapters after drafting and revising the order to ensure readers learn necessary concepts before dependent ones.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Reordering improves comprehension but may require substantial rewriting and coordination.

**Design prompt:** Review chapter order after a full draft by asking what a reader must already understand at each point; revise sequence before polishing prose.

[Supporting discussion, 23:18–23:26](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=9vwVLEKlweA&t=1398s)

### Use iterative critique to find the claim

Difficult design topics may need repeated rewrites before their central explanation becomes clear. The coauthors exchange drafts, challenge missed points and voice, and report rewriting some chapters from scratch multiple times.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Iteration can clarify novel or abstract subjects but costs time and depends on constructive collaboration.

**Design prompt:** For a hard-to-explain topic, invite a collaborator to identify the actual claim and reader gap; rewrite structure where polishing cannot fix the problem.

[Supporting discussion, 25:14–25:20](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=9vwVLEKlweA&t=1514s)

**Episode-specific limits**

- The discussion describes the authors' book workflow; it is illustrative practice, not evidence that every book benefits from the same chapter format.
- The transcript contains promotional material and jokes, excluded from findings.

## Hidden Movement! Finally!

**Source:** [Hidden Movement! Finally!](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=zzgdqZGY8UQ) · **Video ID:** `zzgdqZGY8UQ`

The hosts compare hidden movement and hidden information across board and miniature games, with examples of discrete, double-blind, probabilistic, and solo implementations.

### Hidden movement is harder in analog space

A miniature game's open physical table makes exact location secrecy difficult to administer without extra recording or mediation. Unlike a board game's numbered spaces, a free-position model has no simple coordinate to write down; historical solutions use maps, referees, rooms, or plotting procedures.

**Tradeoff to consider:** These tools can support secrecy but add components, labor, or setup friction, including the costly addition of a nonplaying umpire.

**Design prompt:** Before adding hidden movement, account for how players will record location, verify fair movement, and resolve disputes at the table.

[Supporting discussion, 5:08–5:17](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=zzgdqZGY8UQ&t=308s)

### Discretize the search space

Hidden movement can be made practical by limiting possible locations even if continuous movement is otherwise central. All Quiet on the Martian Front uses blip tokens for a finite set of possible locations; Pacific Command uses stacks of poker chips that may contain task forces or blanks.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Discrete possibilities simplify concealment and search, but sacrifice some analog freedom and may require a design rationale tied to the setting.

**Design prompt:** Choose a meaningful set of candidate positions—such as buildings or sea-area stacks—and make reveals and movement legible within that system.

[Supporting discussion, 17:16–17:26](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=zzgdqZGY8UQ&t=1036s)

### Double blind needs uncertainty about detection

Double blind is more than both players hiding their own movement; the hidden player also does not learn which searches found them. In Pacific Command, reconnaissance samples chips from a stack, shuffles them back, and conceals which subset was examined, so neither side knows what was seen.

**Tradeoff to consider:** This creates richer fog of war, but requires components and an explicit procedure for preserving uncertainty.

**Design prompt:** Define who knows a unit's location, who knows it was searched, and what information a failed search reveals; use blinding or shuffling procedures where the second knowledge state matters.

[Supporting discussion, 11:13–11:22](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=zzgdqZGY8UQ&t=673s)

### Make negative reconnaissance ambiguous

A failed search can remain informative when the search procedure itself is imperfect rather than definitive. In the naval example, a reconnaissance result may miss a force that was present, so players should treat negative results cautiously.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Uncertain failure supports the theme of vast search areas, but can frustrate players who expect a search to settle the question.

**Design prompt:** If a setting depends on imperfect detection, define how searches can miss and communicate that a negative result is evidence with limits, not proof of absence.

[Supporting discussion, 12:13–12:25](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=zzgdqZGY8UQ&t=733s)

### Use decoys that have strategic value

Bluffs are compelling when a decoy action can still produce value for the player who invests in it. In Pacific Command, blank stacks can move, consume activation resources, and later merge or exchange chips with real forces, enabling shell games rather than disposable fake markers.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Useful decoys deepen deception but add state and rules overhead; purely empty blips can make bluffing a wasted action.

**Design prompt:** Give decoys a secondary tactical role or meaningful possible identities so choosing to bluff remains a real decision even when the opponent ignores it.

[Supporting discussion, 13:32–13:39](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=zzgdqZGY8UQ&t=812s)

### Bound uncertainty around physical units

A unit can be represented by a known possibility area and revealed only when interaction requires a precise physical placement. The hosts describe a marker as the center of a possible location radius; once line of sight forces disclosure, the owner places the actual miniature, and it may return to uncertainty when out of sight.

**Tradeoff to consider:** This preserves physical movement while adding positional ambiguity, but can permit surprising interpretations such as a vehicle having crossed terrain while hidden.

**Design prompt:** Set clear reveal triggers and movement constraints for a possibility area, then test whether the resulting surprise feels like intended stealth or an unfair retroactive claim.

[Supporting discussion, 21:36–21:44](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=zzgdqZGY8UQ&t=1296s)

### Test information asymmetry as a player-experience risk

A player who must decide without access to information available to the opponent may feel unable to exercise competence. The hosts describe one player making probability-driven guesses while the other uses full tactics, which can make a later wrong decision feel unavoidable rather than skill-based.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Asymmetry can strongly express stealth or a game's premise, but may undermine agency when it appears as a small ability in an otherwise symmetric tactical game.

**Design prompt:** Make severe information asymmetry conspicuous in the game's promise and test whether the uninformed player still has meaningful choices; avoid treating hidden information as harmless flavor.

[Supporting discussion, 18:38–18:44](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=zzgdqZGY8UQ&t=1118s)

### Prefer symmetric fog when appropriate

Hidden information is easier to accept when both players face the same uncertainty about positions and detection. The hosts contrast asymmetric stealth with Pacific Command's shell game, where both sides lack knowledge of the other's ships and whether searches have occurred.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Symmetry reduces unequal competence, though it may not fit a scenario built around a single stealth attacker.

**Design prompt:** When the fiction permits it, mirror uncertainty and search rules for both sides; when it does not, add compensating agency or explicit expectation-setting.

[Supporting discussion, 20:54–21:01](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=zzgdqZGY8UQ&t=1254s)

### Use blips for identity and intent

Concealing what a marker represents can create meaningful response choices beyond concealing its exact location. The hosts propose making every blip something useful, such as an engineer, sapper, or whole unit, so each concealed identity demands a different response.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Multiple plausible identities support bluff and counterplay, but require each possibility to matter and be readable at reveal.

**Design prompt:** Design a small set of distinct hidden identities with different responses, and ensure every identity—including decoys—has a credible purpose.

[Supporting discussion, 24:26–24:31](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=zzgdqZGY8UQ&t=1466s)

### Hidden information can attach to lists and equipment

Information secrecy can extend to unit identity, leaders, or equipment even when models remain visible. The discussion notes hidden champions, magic items, and disguised units; whether lists are openly shared changes how strongly models must communicate their role visually.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Concealed lists can increase discovery and reward modeling clarity, but make it harder for opponents to know what a miniature represents.

**Design prompt:** State which roster details are public and when concealed details are revealed; align visual conventions with the information opponents are expected to infer.

[Supporting discussion, 26:01–26:09](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=zzgdqZGY8UQ&t=1561s)

### Solo play permits true hidden movement

A solo opponent can keep secrets because the game itself need not share the player's knowledge. The hosts contrast token-based enemy movement in Perilous Tales with AI guards that only detect a ninja when defined conditions are triggered.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Solo procedures can provide true secrecy without a human opponent, but demand reliable AI or token handling to preserve it.

**Design prompt:** For solo designs, define what the system knows and what triggers detection; use concealed tokens for probabilistic threats or behavioral rules for spatial stealth.

[Supporting discussion, 28:11–28:18](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=zzgdqZGY8UQ&t=1691s)

**Differences in approach or unresolved questions**

- The hosts are skeptical that fully continuous hidden movement can be practical or enjoyable in a miniature game without a computer or heavy administration, but explicitly leave the question open and favor exploring discrete and partially hidden alternatives.

**Episode-specific limits**

- Specific game names are sometimes mangled by ASR; Pacific Command, All Quiet on the Martian Front, Perilous Tales, and Infinity are inferred from clear context and could benefit from parent validation.
- The hosts' judgments about player tolerance for asymmetric competence are design cautions, not universal psychological findings; no study details are supplied here.

## Why Campaigns?

**Source:** [Why Campaigns?](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=rihuqsIcn30) · **Video ID:** `rihuqsIcn30`

The hosts examine why designers include campaign systems despite uncertain uptake, additional length, and the difficulty of testing a full campaign.

### Separate campaign aspiration from actual use

Some players value the idea and identity of campaign play even when they rarely complete campaigns. The hosts describe campaign systems as an aspirational lifestyle signal and a visible promise of long-term development.

**Tradeoff to consider:** The promise can attract players, but may add pages and implied obligation without actual use.

**Design prompt:** Distinguish audience desire for campaign possibility from demonstrated play; treat presence as a design choice with costs, not automatic generosity.

[Supporting discussion, 1:13–1:21](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=rihuqsIcn30&t=73s)

### Avoid padding under the name of generosity

More rules text does not itself provide more value; explanatory prose should clarify meaningful choices. The hosts invoke a uselessly verbose definition of climbing as an example of adding words simply to fill space, then question whether campaign chapters serve the game or the writer's wish to provide more.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Concise rules save reader effort, but necessary explanation and optional depth still have value.

**Design prompt:** Ask whether each campaign page enables play or communicates a desired experience; cut material that only increases page count or creates guilt about unused modes.

[Supporting discussion, 3:18–3:26](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=rihuqsIcn30&t=198s)

### Campaigns can be a core need or an optional extension

Some games are complete as one-off experiences; others need campaign structure to deliver their central promise. The hosts contrast a self-contained game with a campaign that can extend character or force stories, and note that a campaign may reveal how repeated play should feel even if players do not use its procedures.

**Tradeoff to consider:** An optional campaign adds imagined depth, while integrating it into the core engine increases design commitment and can burden players who want standalone games.

**Design prompt:** Decide whether progression is essential to the game's premise; if it is optional, keep the one-off game satisfying and label the campaign's role clearly.

[Supporting discussion, 6:12–6:17](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=rihuqsIcn30&t=372s)

### Build campaign play into the engine when it drives the premise

A campaign's strongest contribution may emerge when its progression reinforces the same idea as the battle rules. The host praises a campaign that mirrors an escalation-focused game and says discovering that relationship earlier could have allowed the campaign to shape the engine.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Integration can create coherence, but makes campaign decisions affect core balance and increases testing scope.

**Design prompt:** If escalation, persistent characters, or changing resources define the game, prototype how campaign progression and match rules reinforce one another before late-stage writing.

[Supporting discussion, 7:38–7:45](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=rihuqsIcn30&t=458s)

### Campaign framing can color one-off play

A campaign section can teach players to imagine the wider stakes of individual games even when they play isolated matches. The hosts observe that territory fame or worsening vehicle reliability can shape how players picture a battle without applying the full campaign sequence.

**Tradeoff to consider:** This ambient framing may enrich play, but risks implying persistent effects that one-off players do not actually track.

**Design prompt:** Use campaign fiction to explain what repeated battles mean, while separating evocative framing from mandatory bookkeeping.

[Supporting discussion, 8:53–8:59](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=rihuqsIcn30&t=533s)

### End-to-end campaign testing is unusually expensive

A campaign's balance and fun cannot be established by testing isolated matches alone. The hosts note that campaign tests require playing through long sequences; abstracting early games and jumping to a later point can miss interactions that arise across the full arc.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Full runs give stronger evidence but consume time and coordination; partial tests are cheaper but leave progression risks unresolved.

**Design prompt:** Plan campaign testing as a separate project with complete runs where feasible, and label conclusions from abbreviated tests as provisional.

[Supporting discussion, 12:16–12:24](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=rihuqsIcn30&t=736s)

### Campaigns carry a large release risk

A poorly tested campaign can become an unusually large source of player frustration inside an otherwise sound game. Because a full playthrough takes many matches, campaign defects may survive much more testing than battle procedures and only become apparent after extended use.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Including a campaign promises depth and perceived value, but exposes players to a long-form system with less reliable validation.

**Design prompt:** Test the campaign's whole arc and make its optional status or evidence level clear; consider omitting it if it cannot be tested adequately and is not core.

[Supporting discussion, 10:49–10:55](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=rihuqsIcn30&t=649s)

### Do not equate length with value

A campaign's apparent depth can make a product seem richer even when a shorter complete game would serve players better. The hosts compare campaign pages to long episode counts: visible duration signals value, while a concise work may already deliver its intended experience.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Perceived breadth can help communicate potential, but should not substitute for satisfying play or proven quality.

**Design prompt:** Evaluate campaign inclusion against the game's actual needs and audience, not a presumed expectation that a rulebook must be thick.

[Supporting discussion, 13:25–13:30](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=rihuqsIcn30&t=805s)

**Differences in approach or unresolved questions**

- The hosts remain divided between rational concerns about unnecessary and under-tested campaigns and their own enthusiasm for campaigns' narrative promise; they explicitly invite examples and contrary views.

**Episode-specific limits**

- The episode is exploratory and does not settle when any particular game needs a campaign.
- Campaign reception and play frequency are asserted from the hosts' experience, without survey evidence.

## Putting plot armour on your games.

**Source:** [Putting plot armour on your games.](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=8Vti7Ck0uT8) · **Video ID:** `8Vti7Ck0uT8`

The hosts explore how designers can support dramatic moments in solo, narrative, and competitive miniature games without forcing a fixed story.

### Solo design permits authored hero protection

A solo game can protect a protagonist from early failure because no opposing human needs a fair competitive chance. The designer controls the AI and can make a hero survive an opening encounter so a longer dramatic arc can unfold.

**Tradeoff to consider:** This enables deliberate pacing but reduces uncertainty if the player recognizes the protection as arbitrary.

**Design prompt:** Use protected outcomes selectively in solo scenarios when the dramatic promise depends on a hero reaching a later beat; preserve challenge in other decisions.

[Supporting discussion, 1:03–1:08](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=8Vti7Ck0uT8&t=63s)

### Head-to-head games need shared agency

In opposed play, a designer cannot simply declare one side the hero without accounting for the other player's agency and incentives. If one player controls the protagonists and the other the enemies, an efficient opponent can end the story early unless rules or goals shape both sides' choices.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Strong narrative direction can constrain competitive freedom; unrestricted play may fail to produce the intended story.

**Design prompt:** Identify who is expected to care about each narrative beat and align incentives or rules so both players still have worthwhile decisions.

[Supporting discussion, 4:37–4:43](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=8Vti7Ck0uT8&t=277s)

### Use limited resources as plot armour

A bounded defensive resource can protect a character through a key early moment without making them invulnerable. The hosts describe The Lord of the Rings heroes as having limited protective resources, contrasting that protection with being freely indestructible; resources run out and their use matters.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Resource-based survival supports a climax and player choice, but still changes combat balance and needs clear limits.

**Design prompt:** When a character must plausibly reach a later scene, test a finite survival resource whose use matters; avoid open-ended immunity that removes tactical risk.

[Supporting discussion, 10:02–10:09](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=8Vti7Ck0uT8&t=602s)

### Different vulnerabilities create character-specific stories

Plot support can be asymmetric within a character rather than a blanket increase to durability. Boromir's example pairs strength in close combat with exceptional vulnerability to arrows, making a particular narrative fate more likely through ordinary play.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Character-specific vulnerability can produce recognizable stories, but may become predictable or feel unfair if the threat is not signaled.

**Design prompt:** Represent a character's dramatic strengths and dangers as differentiated rules, and ensure opponents can understand and tactically engage with them.

[Supporting discussion, 10:23–10:32](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=8Vti7Ck0uT8&t=623s)

### Reward desired moments and protect catastrophic ones

Designers can shape emergent narrative through a mix of incentives for desired actions and safeguards against story-ending outcomes. The hosts describe rewarding cinematic behavior and protecting moments that should recur, while letting players compete freely inside those boundaries.

**Tradeoff to consider:** This can make narrative beats more common without scripting them, but too many rewards or safeguards can make play feel authored.

**Design prompt:** List the moments you want to happen more often and the outcomes you need to prevent; use the lightest rule intervention that changes their likelihood.

[Supporting discussion, 12:08–12:15](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=8Vti7Ck0uT8&t=728s)

### Do not promise a fixed story from a competitive system

Head-to-head games cannot reliably reproduce a cinematic plot because players, dice, setup, and interpretation all affect outcomes. The hosts note that even a narrative campaign can miss its intended communication, and scripted outcomes may require players to cooperate rather than compete.

**Tradeoff to consider:** A shared retelling can create a memorable story but shifts the activity toward collaborative storytelling and may reduce replay value.

**Design prompt:** Be clear whether a scenario asks players to compete or jointly recreate a known story; do not assume a competitive result can guarantee a film-like arc.

[Supporting discussion, 27:16–27:24](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=8Vti7Ck0uT8&t=1636s)

### Carry consequences forward when victory is predetermined

A scenario can remain meaningful when the broad outcome is known if players determine a consequential secondary question. The hosts propose that heroes may escape regardless, while the number of enemies defeated determines what the next scenario contains.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Secondary objectives preserve agency and campaign impact, but can feel like consolation if they do not matter later.

**Design prompt:** For an intentionally lopsided or historical scenario, define a measurable outcome that affects later play even when the headline winner is fixed.

[Supporting discussion, 29:11–29:19](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=8Vti7Ck0uT8&t=1751s)

### Support narrative in ordinary pickup play

Even a non-narrative match can produce more satisfying stories when the rules make a few climactic moments more likely. The hosts recommend identifying moments the system should produce and using small protections or rewards so they arise in average play without guaranteeing them.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Light support retains tactical freedom, while strong guarantees risk short-circuiting competition.

**Design prompt:** Define narrative imperatives for the system, then tune rules to improve their frequency rather than force an exact sequence.

[Supporting discussion, 31:32–31:42](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=8Vti7Ck0uT8&t=1892s)

### Avoid requiring players to collaborate on a fixed outcome

A head-to-head campaign can become unsatisfying when players must agree in advance to recreate a known scene rather than pursue their goals. The hosts describe players discussing how to arrange a famous character encounter so both sides can enjoy a pre-known moment, which turns competition into negotiated reenactment.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Shared authorial intent can produce a desired scene, but limits the meaning of winning and reduces repeatability.

**Design prompt:** If a scenario depends on players preserving a story beat, decide whether it is truly competitive or should be presented as collaborative play.

[Supporting discussion, 25:50–25:57](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=8Vti7Ck0uT8&t=1550s)

### Distinguish dramatic emulation from historical simulation

Both realism and genre-faithful drama can be undermined when an outcome contradicts what the game has taught players to expect. The hosts compare an implausible bullet through walls with a protagonist dying from a minor accident before a promised heroic moment.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Protecting genre expectations improves coherence but may require bending ordinary probabilities or giving up gritty uncertainty.

**Design prompt:** Identify the fiction contract—physical realism, heroic emulation, or another target—and test whether unexpected results support or break it.

[Supporting discussion, 16:37–16:43](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=8Vti7Ck0uT8&t=997s)

**Differences in approach or unresolved questions**

- One host favors bounded narrative guarantees and rewards; the other warns that head-to-head games are poorly suited to controlled hero narratives and that imposed story can weaken competition. They converge on supporting selected moments without guaranteeing a complete plot.

**Episode-specific limits**

- Named titles and systems include ASR noise; Lord of the Rings and Gaslands are clear from context.

## 4 ways games create theme that you can use.

**Source:** [4 ways games create theme that you can use.](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=OVJ8SGDKoBI) · **Video ID:** `OVJ8SGDKoBI`

The hosts distinguish theme-related concepts and describe how a game's core mechanics, rewards, physical assumptions, and design references can communicate its central idea.

### Treat the central idea as a design constraint

Rules, components, writing, and even measuring tools all communicate what the game is about and should align with its central idea. The hosts frame the idea as the intended impression sent to players, with each component either reinforcing or weakening it.

**Tradeoff to consider:** A strong through-line gives coherence, but can become restrictive if the initial idea is not allowed to evolve.

**Design prompt:** Write a concise statement of the experience you want players to have and use it to evaluate both mechanics and presentation choices.

[Supporting discussion, 1:41–1:50](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=OVJ8SGDKoBI&t=101s)

### Use genre as a playtest checklist

Genre and background can define expected moments that help designers judge whether a game is delivering its promise. For a televised post-apocalyptic death race, the hosts ask whether cars blow up, flips happen, and players enjoy the chaos in the ways the genre suggests.

**Tradeoff to consider:** A checklist makes abstract intent testable, but risks reducing a genre to clichés if treated as mandatory beats.

**Design prompt:** Turn the game's reference points into observable playtest questions, then check both whether moments occur and whether they create the intended reaction.

[Supporting discussion, 4:20–4:26](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=OVJ8SGDKoBI&t=260s)

### Translate story beats into incentives

A rule that permits a thematic action is insufficient if players have no reason to choose it. The hosts describe identifying story beats, mapping them to physical events, creating rules that make those events possible, then rewarding players for causing them; wall-running is their example of an under-rewarded action.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Over-rewarding can make a desired action dominate, but under-rewarding makes it disappear from play.

**Design prompt:** Start with a thematic event, define the table action that expresses it, then test whether its payoff competes with safer alternatives; initially err toward making the signature behavior viable.

[Supporting discussion, 6:58–7:06](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=OVJ8SGDKoBI&t=418s)

### Foreground the core loop that expresses the conceit

The rules players think about most should carry the game's central fantasy rather than leave it to labels and flavor text. Gaslands foregrounds templates and skid dice to evoke driving; A Billion Suns foregrounds jump-point choices to evoke remote fleet command, while other procedures remain simpler or backgrounded.

**Tradeoff to consider:** A tightly linked core loop creates a clear identity, but can make secondary activities intentionally less rich.

**Design prompt:** Ask what players plan during downtime and what decisions dominate turns; connect those repeated decisions to the intended fantasy.

[Supporting discussion, 9:47–9:54](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=OVJ8SGDKoBI&t=587s)

### Theme is not achieved by naming alone

Calling a mechanic thematic does not make players feel the intended emotion or experience. The hosts criticize systems that use thematic names and conceits over rules that do not generate the corresponding behavior or feeling.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Names help orientation and imagination, but cannot substitute for mechanical consequences.

**Design prompt:** Playtest the actual decisions and emotional response; if the desired experience is absent, change the procedure or reward structure rather than only relabeling it.

[Supporting discussion, 11:36–11:42](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=OVJ8SGDKoBI&t=696s)

### Distinguish visible and off-screen space

A game's physical representation creates expectations about which actions must be shown on the table and which can remain abstract. The hosts contrast a tiny wizard on a large battlefield, whose movement must match visible scale, with unseen spells, drivers, or events inside ships that players accept through narrative description.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Off-screen space frees designers to imply larger events, but visible miniatures constrain plausible action and scale.

**Design prompt:** Identify each game's physical blind spots and decide which actions need tokens or models versus can be conveyed through abstract rules.

[Supporting discussion, 23:08–23:14](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=OVJ8SGDKoBI&t=1388s)

### Use objectives and contract rules to stage beats

A game can create a story sequence by giving players operational goals that evoke familiar scenes without narrating a fixed plot. A Billion Suns uses contracts around a diseased facility, rescue, escape to a jump point, and extraction; the mission steps create recognizable science-fiction beats through play.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Structured objectives reliably produce a scene, but too much sequencing can feel like a prewritten story imposed on a competitive game.

**Design prompt:** Translate a desired cinematic moment into concrete objectives and actions, then let players determine how successfully it unfolds.

[Supporting discussion, 26:22–26:29](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=OVJ8SGDKoBI&t=1582s)

### Preserve the spark with a mood board

Visual references can help a design team remember the feeling that first motivated a game. The host gathers toy cars, films, and comics into a photographed mood board and uses it to articulate the flavor and evocations desired.

**Tradeoff to consider:** References clarify tone but can bias a team toward imitation if treated as a literal template.

**Design prompt:** Collect a small set of varied references, including nonliteral ones, and use them to discuss the emotional target rather than copy their surface details.

[Supporting discussion, 29:36–29:42](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=OVJ8SGDKoBI&t=1776s)

### Tune incentives after establishing the desired beat

Rewarding a signature action can reveal whether a game supports its intended fantasy, but the first pass may overdo it. The hosts recommend initially making desired actions attractive, then reducing the reward if players repeat them to excess.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Strong incentives expose whether mechanics create the intended behavior; over-rewarding can make play monotonous.

**Design prompt:** Playtest toward both failure modes: nobody uses the signature action, or everyone does it constantly; adjust incentives between those extremes.

[Supporting discussion, 8:27–8:34](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=OVJ8SGDKoBI&t=507s)

### Let rules earn their evocative names

A name can help teach a rule and reinforce a world, but it cannot create the promised feeling by itself. The hosts distinguish useful labels such as Sharpshooter from a tabletop situation where distance, cover, and visibility make the shot feel exceptional.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Evocative names support memory and tone; a mismatch between label and outcome frustrates expectations.

**Design prompt:** Keep memorable names, then ensure the rule’s actual conditions and results make the name credible.

[Supporting discussion, 12:28–12:39](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=OVJ8SGDKoBI&t=748s)

### Align fictional claims with tabletop performance

When play contradicts the background or product promise, revise one side rather than forcing the contradiction onto players. A unit described as a devastating monster that dies immediately, or a sniper that excels at close range, breaks the expected relationship between fiction and rules.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Changing a name or background can preserve enjoyable mechanics, while retaining a false claim undermines trust.

**Design prompt:** Compare unit descriptions and packaging with observed playtest outcomes; rename, rewrite, or redesign the mismatch.

[Supporting discussion, 15:15–15:23](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=OVJ8SGDKoBI&t=915s)

### Use off-screen space to support scale and imagination

Narrative description can fill in events that the game does not physically represent. The speakers contrast a small wizard constrained by movement rules with ships, vehicles, or facilities where unseen crews and events can be described freely.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Off-screen details broaden story possibilities, while describing actions that contradict visible movement or scale breaks credibility.

**Design prompt:** Identify what the table expects to see represented, then use narration for the intentionally abstract or unseen parts.

[Supporting discussion, 23:01–23:10](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=OVJ8SGDKoBI&t=1381s)

### Reward players for creating the intended sequence

A game can suggest a story without dictating it by rewarding players for producing recognizable beats. The Billion Suns example links a facility crisis, a rescue, and an escape to objectives and contract rewards, letting players enact the sequence.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Structured beats make a scenario legible; too much prescription can turn play into reenactment rather than player-authored action.

**Design prompt:** Build objectives around a few moments the game wants to evoke, while leaving players room to decide how they reach them.

[Supporting discussion, 25:22–25:28](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=OVJ8SGDKoBI&t=1522s)

### Document the idea and its touchstones

A design document and a visual mood board can help a team preserve the inspiration behind a game. The hosts recommend stating design principles and assembling references, objects, and images that capture the intended flavor.

**Tradeoff to consider:** A mood board can clarify tone and guide decisions, but references should inspire rather than force literal reproduction.

**Design prompt:** Keep a compact collection of touchstones near the design goals and revisit it when mechanics or presentation drift.

[Supporting discussion, 29:44–29:51](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=OVJ8SGDKoBI&t=1784s)

**Differences in approach or unresolved questions**

- The hosts differ in how strongly they want to engineer narrative behavior and emotional response; one emphasizes rules that prompt beats, while the other cautions that labels alone do not deliver theme and highlights the limits of physical representation.

**Episode-specific limits**

- The episode title suggests four thematic lenses, but the transcript ranges across broader design advice and does not provide a neat four-part taxonomy.

## How you can create a world in some easy steps!

**Source:** [How you can create a world in some easy steps!](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=CPJCjUWxA2A) · **Video ID:** `CPJCjUWxA2A`

The hosts discuss practical worldbuilding for games, including starting from a conceit, using maps and microfiction, learning craft, and making settings usable by players.

### Start with a concrete conceit

A simple organizing image can generate a coherent setting more effectively than beginning with a generic fantasy encyclopedia. The host's underground world begins with turning a world map on its side into a vertical cross-section, then derives zones, climate, and inhabitants from depth.

**Tradeoff to consider:** A strong conceit focuses invention but can become a gimmick if it does not support playable conflicts and places.

**Design prompt:** Write a one-sentence setting premise and sketch its spatial logic; ask what distinct locations and pressures follow from it.

[Supporting discussion, 5:55–6:00](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=CPJCjUWxA2A&t=355s)

### Map to discover relationships

A rough map can turn a premise into concrete questions about geography, travel, ownership, and conflict. The host draws a schematic before writing extensive detail; later, place names and their relationships help him imagine who controls what and why.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Maps provide structure but can become a premature commitment to detail before the game needs it.

**Design prompt:** Sketch only major regions and connections first, then use gaps or adjacency to generate playable tensions.
