[Supporting discussion, 5:45–5:53](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=CPJCjUWxA2A&t=345s)

### Reveal setting through playable moments

Short in-world moments can make a setting feel inhabited while avoiding a front-loaded exposition dump. The host shifts from explanatory background to microfiction at a character's eye level, writing encounters and movements that reveal the world incrementally.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Microfiction can be difficult to write well and may distract if it does not support play; exposition can be more efficient for necessary facts.

**Design prompt:** Use brief scenes to convey sensory detail and lived consequences, and retain only those that clarify the game's places, conflicts, or tone.

[Supporting discussion, 7:35–7:43](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=CPJCjUWxA2A&t=455s)

### Worldbuilding through roleplay and conversation

Speaking or roleplaying through a setting can expose uncertainty that abstract planning leaves hidden. The host tests ideas in conversations and bedtime stories, imagining a character opening doors and encountering places to find what feels vivid.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Improvisation can generate energy and reveal gaps, but needs later selection and consistency checks.

**Design prompt:** Tell a short scene aloud or roleplay a location with collaborators; note moments that create excitement or force a useful world decision.

[Supporting discussion, 6:45–6:53](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=CPJCjUWxA2A&t=405s)

### Treat setting prose as a craft

Flavor text and world background require deliberate writing skill rather than being effortless filler between mechanics. The host describes struggling to write microfiction after years of technical prose, producing weak drafts and using feedback to improve.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Investing in craft improves clarity and atmosphere, but creates extra work beyond rules design.

**Design prompt:** Draft, solicit focused feedback, and revise setting prose; do not assume technical writing ability automatically transfers to fiction.

[Supporting discussion, 9:05–9:12](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=CPJCjUWxA2A&t=545s)

### Make the world buildable by players

A tabletop setting is scaffolding that other people must use to create armies, scenarios, and reasons to fight. The hosts contrast an opaque dreamlike setting with accessible locations and factions that give players understandable choices for building forces and campaigns.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Open, legible structures support player invention, while highly idiosyncratic lore may offer stronger authorial flavor but create a higher entry burden.

**Design prompt:** Check whether players can invent forces, motives, and scenarios within the setting; provide enough practical structure to make those acts easy.

[Supporting discussion, 24:16–24:22](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=CPJCjUWxA2A&t=1456s)

### Give game fiction operational coherence

Because players inhabit a game's world through their choices, its fiction should make practical sense at the level the game exposes. The hosts argue that tabletop players need more nuts-and-bolts understanding than readers of a novel because they create armies and act within the setting themselves.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Concrete coherence supports play and player invention but can limit the freedom of dreamlike or deliberately mysterious fiction.

**Design prompt:** Explain the causes, relationships, and capabilities needed for players to make meaningful in-world decisions; use ambiguity where it serves play rather than as a substitute for structure.

[Supporting discussion, 26:18–26:28](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=CPJCjUWxA2A&t=1578s)

### Align fiction with permitted and rewarded actions

When setting prose says an action is possible or admirable, the rules should let players perform it and make its value intelligible. The host criticizes fiction where every character wins constantly, arguing that tabletop outcomes should include setbacks and that claimed actions should be mechanically available.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Close alignment builds trust, but fiction may sometimes describe events beyond the rules' focus or scale.

**Design prompt:** Audit prominent setting claims against actual tabletop actions and outcomes; remove or reframe claims that the system cannot support.

[Supporting discussion, 31:42–31:49](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=CPJCjUWxA2A&t=1902s)

### Write for the game's purpose, not completeness

Worldbuilding may benefit the designer's understanding without all of it needing to appear in the player-facing book. The hosts allow exploratory self-indulgence as a discovery tool, then recommend cutting material that does not serve the game or its readers.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Private depth can resolve the world, while publishing every detail adds reading burden and obscures the playable core.

**Design prompt:** Create freely in drafts, then select only details that help players understand the setting, build forces, or make choices.

[Supporting discussion, 30:51–30:59](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=CPJCjUWxA2A&t=1851s)

**Differences in approach or unresolved questions**

- The hosts contrast structured worldbuilding with a more improvisational, atmospheric approach and acknowledge that both can work for different creators; they favor practical coherence for settings players must inhabit and extend.

**Episode-specific limits**

- The hosts explicitly say worldbuilding is an area where they are less expert than rules design, and frame the discussion as an in-progress reflection.
- Some source phrases and author names are unclear under ASR; the findings avoid uncertain attribution.

## Omega level origin story! Mike is the WORLD BUILDER!

**Source:** [Omega level origin story! Mike is the WORLD BUILDER!](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=KKijQHXVqEE) · **Video ID:** `KKijQHXVqEE`

The hosts consider how much setting detail different miniature games need and how fiction can support mechanics, player imagination, and hobby choices.

### Scale lore to the game's form

A character-led skirmish game and an open-ended army game benefit from different amounts and kinds of worldbuilding. Named characters with fixed relationships invite tight biography, while a looser army game benefits from broad spaces in which players can invent unusual forces and stories.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Specific lore deepens attachment but narrows player authorship; open lore supports conversion and invention but gives less authored context.

**Design prompt:** Decide how much of the setting players are meant to author themselves, and match detail to whether the game centers on named characters or player-built forces.

[Supporting discussion, 3:00–7:00](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=KKijQHXVqEE&t=180s)

### Use genre chords for a compact setting

A concise premise can let players supply familiar associations without extensive exposition. Gaslands uses a single alternate-history timeline point and a recognizable post-apocalyptic racing genre to trigger players' own images and references.

**Tradeoff to consider:** A short cue is efficient but may lead to varied interpretations; it works best when the game reinforces the same tone mechanically.

**Design prompt:** For a self-contained game, test whether a small number of setting cues reliably evokes the intended genre and use rules to reinforce them.

[Supporting discussion, 6:00–9:00](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=KKijQHXVqEE&t=360s)

### Provide richer worlds for long-term play

A setting intended to sustain campaigns, expansions, or broad army creation may need more connective material than a one-off game. The hosts distinguish a compact self-contained premise from worlds that support repeated products and ongoing player invention.

**Tradeoff to consider:** More setting creates room for continuity and attachment, but increases reading burden and can crowd out player-created material.

**Design prompt:** Scale published lore to the expected lifespan and extension of the game; add detail where it unlocks new play or hobby choices.

[Supporting discussion, 5:00–8:00](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=KKijQHXVqEE&t=300s)

### Make lore teach game use

Fiction can help players understand how a model or ability should behave, not merely provide backstory. The hosts praise an earlier Malifaux character whose narrative description subtly communicates the tactical pattern encoded in his abilities.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Mechanically informative fiction improves intuitive play, but subtle cues may be missed unless reinforced by clear rules text.

**Design prompt:** Write character or faction details that foreshadow actual tabletop behavior, then check whether players infer the intended strategy without relying on lore alone.

[Supporting discussion, 31:00–35:00](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=KKijQHXVqEE&t=1860s)

### Avoid lore stapled onto unrelated mechanics

Adding more story does not strengthen a setting if it no longer informs play or helps players interpret the rules. One host describes a game becoming more narratively expansive while losing the subtle connection between character fiction and tabletop interactions.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Standalone stories can delight readers, but may leave the game feeling like a competitive system with a separate novel attached.

**Design prompt:** Evaluate setting additions by whether they guide play, illuminate a mechanic, or create useful player meaning; distinguish those goals from fiction for its own sake.

[Supporting discussion, 34:00–37:00](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=KKijQHXVqEE&t=2040s)

### Let setting inspire models, terrain, and scenarios

A world can create hobby value by making players want to build new forces, tables, and situations. The hosts describe unusual environments and factions prompting new terrain, conversions, and tactical situations, such as subterranean lava-dwelling armies and tunnels.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Open-ended inspiration can refresh collections but needs enough concrete cues to become actionable rather than vague atmosphere.

**Design prompt:** Include distinctive biomes, conflicts, and faction premises that translate into buildable models and board features; connect them to scenario or rules possibilities.

[Supporting discussion, 37:00–40:00](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=KKijQHXVqEE&t=2220s)

### Use setting details as memory aids

A small piece of descriptive fiction can help players understand or remember a game's tactical relationships. The hosts use an enchanted mace that is good against undead as an example of a lore cue that supports intuition about a component's use.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Flavor can make rules memorable, but an intuitive implication must still match the actual mechanical effect.

**Design prompt:** Pair names, art, and short flavor text with a component's rules role; check that the combined presentation accurately signals how and when it is useful.

[Supporting discussion, 31:00–33:00](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=KKijQHXVqEE&t=1860s)

**Differences in approach or unresolved questions**

- One host values rich worldbuilding as an invitation to explore and create, while the other emphasizes keeping setting detail mechanically useful and protecting player space. Both accept that the right amount depends on the game and audience.

**Episode-specific limits**

- The source conversation is exploratory and the hosts repeatedly qualify their worldbuilding expertise.
- Some named games and character names are garbled by ASR; the findings describe examples by role where exact spelling is uncertain.

## Cognitive load, how much can YOU handle?

**Source:** [Cognitive load, how much can YOU handle?](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=GxV9cguZ9Y0) · **Video ID:** `GxV9cguZ9Y0`

The hosts distinguish complexity budget from cognitive load and discuss where players should spend attention, how to make game state legible, and how to handle calculations.

### Distinguish rules novelty from in-game thinking

Complexity budget is the amount of new system knowledge a game asks players to learn; cognitive load is what it asks them to think about during play. The hosts contrast learning unfamiliar rules with the live decisions, calculations, and tactical assessments players must perform at the table.

**Tradeoff to consider:** A game can teach a novel subsystem but keep its repeated decisions manageable, or use familiar rules while still creating heavy live calculation.

**Design prompt:** Assess learning burden and moment-to-moment burden separately; spend each where it supports the game's identity.

[Supporting discussion, 0:00–3:00](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=GxV9cguZ9Y0&t=0s)

### Spend attention on interesting decisions

Players should use mental effort on tactical choices and reading opponents rather than repetitive arithmetic or bookkeeping. The hosts identify calculation and record-keeping as two major sources of unproductive cognitive load.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Some arithmetic can support meaningful precision, but repeated low-value math crowds out strategic thought.

**Design prompt:** Audit common turns for what players calculate and remember; simplify or automate tasks that do not create an interesting decision.

[Supporting discussion, 2:00–4:00](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=GxV9cguZ9Y0&t=120s)

### Make persistent state visible

Players should not have to remember an uninteresting changing number when that state affects a model on the table. The hosts recommend visible tokens or other bookkeeping aids, while noting that too many widgets can clutter the table and obscure the narrative view.

**Tradeoff to consider:** More state markers improve recall and transparency but can produce visual noise and their own cognitive burden.

**Design prompt:** Use the smallest clear marker system for persistent state and test whether players can read the board without recalling hidden values.

[Supporting discussion, 5:00–10:00](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=GxV9cguZ9Y0&t=300s)

### Limit the number of active objects

The count of independently tracked and activated entities often matters more to cognitive load than the number of physical miniatures. The hosts describe large forces grouped into a manageable number of activatable formations, emphasizing the things whose position and status players must track.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Grouping reduces tracking but can hide meaningful individual differences or reduce control granularity.

**Design prompt:** Count independent decisions and state-bearing groups per turn; consolidate models where individual handling does not serve the design.

[Supporting discussion, 7:00–10:00](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=GxV9cguZ9Y0&t=420s)

### Make the table readable at a glance

A player should be able to understand the relevant public game state by looking at the table. The hosts use the idea of ‘grokking’ the board: visible conventions and state let players see positions, statuses, and tactical relationships without reconstructing them from memory.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Legibility supports faster decisions, but deliberate hidden information may be central to some games.

**Design prompt:** Define what players should be able to see immediately and provide consistent visual cues; hide information only when secrecy serves play.

[Supporting discussion, 8:00–11:00](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=GxV9cguZ9Y0&t=480s)

### Separate private choice from shared resolution

Complex resolution can be easier to handle when players first make a simple decision and then resolve consequences together. The hosts contrast deciding whether to shoot using approximate expectations with collectively rolling, counting hits, and applying explosions or other effects.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Shared resolution reduces private pre-action calculation but can slow the table and require clear procedures.

**Design prompt:** Keep the choice prompt intuitive where possible, then make complicated consequences visible and communal rather than requiring hidden calculations before action.

[Supporting discussion, 23:00–27:00](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=GxV9cguZ9Y0&t=1380s)

### Put complexity where the theme needs it

A game's limited novelty budget should go to the mechanics that express its distinctive idea. The hosts contrast simple familiar shooting in a pirate game with novel rules for ships rocking, and describe familiar mechanics freeing attention for a game's unique system.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Familiar procedures ease entry but can leave the signature fantasy underdeveloped; novel systems distinguish a game but demand learning.

**Design prompt:** Identify the core conceit and assign novel procedures to it; keep peripheral activities familiar unless their complexity also creates value.

[Supporting discussion, 0:00–3:00](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=GxV9cguZ9Y0&t=0s)

### Do not hide facts to test memory

Concealing a number because players must remember it creates frustration and rewards recall rather than game skill. The hosts warn that a player who memorizes the hidden number gains an advantage while others feel taxed by remembering it.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Open information may reduce mystery, but memory tests usually do not create useful tactical uncertainty.

**Design prompt:** Keep strategically relevant state visible unless its concealment creates meaningful choices for both players.

[Supporting discussion, 30:00–33:00](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=GxV9cguZ9Y0&t=1800s)

### Make record keeping easy for do-it-yourself play

If a game asks players to track a persistent state, show them how to record it with accessible materials. Printable trackers, clear paper-and-pencil procedures, or suitable tokens reduce ambiguity about how to note wounds and conditions.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Custom widgets can improve usability but may impose cost or availability burdens; vague instructions transfer design work to players.

**Design prompt:** Provide a low-cost default tracking method and clear conventions, even when optional premium tokens exist.

[Supporting discussion, 10:00–14:00](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=GxV9cguZ9Y0&t=600s)

### Budget cognitive load for the intended audience and occasion

Players have different appetites for mental effort, and the same player may want different demands in different sessions. The hosts contrast an intensive multi-hour strategy session with a lighter game chosen after a tiring week.

**Tradeoff to consider:** A high-load design can reward deep engagement; lowering load can sacrifice some strategic intricacy while improving accessibility.

**Design prompt:** Define the audience and expected play context, then test whether the required attention matches that promise.

[Supporting discussion, 14:00–17:00](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=GxV9cguZ9Y0&t=840s)

### Do not confuse busy work with meaningful depth

Many tracked interactions can create a feeling of complexity without giving players satisfying choices. The hosts distinguish interesting puzzles, such as interacting model abilities or varied victory conditions, from bookkeeping and state interactions that only add mental work.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Dense mechanics can support tactical texture when they change decisions; tracking without payoff exhausts attention.

**Design prompt:** For each repeated calculation or status, ask what decision or emotion it creates and remove it if it only tests memory.

[Supporting discussion, 15:00–20:00](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=GxV9cguZ9Y0&t=900s)

### Put calculation after commitment when possible

Players need a quick private estimate to decide whether an action is worthwhile; detailed arithmetic is easier once everyone can resolve it together. The hosts recommend simple input estimates and allow more involved shared calculations after dice are rolled and outcomes are public.

**Tradeoff to consider:** This keeps tactical choice fluid but may reduce exact pre-action forecasting; some expert systems intentionally prize precise calculation.

**Design prompt:** Keep decision-time math small and move multi-step arithmetic to the common resolution procedure or reference aids.

[Supporting discussion, 20:00–29:00](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=GxV9cguZ9Y0&t=1200s)

### Make complexity budget and cognitive load work together

Familiar procedures let players make intuitive decisions, leaving attention for a game’s distinctive mechanics. The hosts connect limited novel rules with lower decision-time calculation, so players can rely on experience for routine interactions.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Too much familiarity can make a game feel conventional; targeted novelty makes it distinctive but consumes more learning and attention.

**Design prompt:** Reserve new procedures for the central experience and keep surrounding systems recognizable where possible.

[Supporting discussion, 27:00–31:00](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=GxV9cguZ9Y0&t=1620s)

**Differences in approach or unresolved questions**

- The hosts acknowledge that some games deliberately embrace demanding calculation or complexity, citing Infinity as a distinctive case; their concern is copying such a niche experience without a design reason.

**Episode-specific limits**

- A middle section of the transcript was truncated in output, so findings emphasize clear opening and closing passages and themes repeated later.
- The ‘seven or nine’ group-size reference is an analogy from the hosts, not a universal hard limit or empirical rule.

## How much pressure can players handle?

**Source:** [How much pressure can players handle?](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=qTpiFtBmbhk) · **Video ID:** `qTpiFtBmbhk`

This first half of a two-part discussion focuses on complexity budget: the cost of learning novelty and how to choose where a game should innovate.

### Novelty has a learning cost

A game can be distinctive without making every subsystem unfamiliar; too much innovation can exhaust players before they appreciate the design. The hosts describe players recognizing familiar conventions and using them to understand most of a game quickly, leaving attention for its novel elements.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Familiar systems lower entry cost but can make a game feel derivative; novelty creates identity but increases learning effort.

**Design prompt:** Identify which rules are standard and which are genuinely new, then concentrate novelty on the mechanics most tied to the game's promise.

[Supporting discussion, 0:00–4:00](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=qTpiFtBmbhk&t=0s)

### Do not reinvent familiar procedures without payoff

A new mechanism is not valuable merely because it uses different steps when it produces the same decisions and outcomes as a known system. The hosts criticize reinvented movement or shooting that changes the moving parts but preserves the same probability and range relationships.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Reusing conventions speeds comprehension, though familiar procedures may need adaptation when the game demands a different effect.

**Design prompt:** For each novel subsystem, state what new outcome, choice, or interaction it enables; retain the familiar method when the result is equivalent.

[Supporting discussion, 3:00–7:00](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=qTpiFtBmbhk&t=180s)

### Use prior knowledge as a scaffold

Shared genre conventions let players spend less effort learning routine tasks and more effort appreciating the new design idea. The hosts describe familiar stat blocks and common board-game mechanisms as patterns players already recognize, even when names differ.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Conventions provide fast entry but can carry baggage or obscure a genuinely different design if copied uncritically.

**Design prompt:** Reuse well-understood procedures for supporting tasks, then clearly signal how a new mechanic differs and why that difference matters.

[Supporting discussion, 4:00–8:00](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=qTpiFtBmbhk&t=240s)

### Keep major innovations few

The hosts offer a rough heuristic that a standalone game should foreground only a small number of truly unfamiliar systems. They compare innovation to seasoning and suggest about three differentiating ideas as an upper bound before players struggle to appreciate them.

**Tradeoff to consider:** A low novelty count protects accessibility but is a personal rule of thumb, not a measured universal threshold.

**Design prompt:** Treat the number as a prompt to prioritize; test comprehension and retention with the intended audience rather than applying it as a fixed cap.

[Supporting discussion, 6:00–9:00](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=qTpiFtBmbhk&t=360s)

### Make novelty earn its cost

Unfamiliar rules are justified when they create a meaningfully different experience or interact with other systems in a necessary way. The hosts cite a bow mechanic that can bank shots around corners as a worthwhile innovation, unlike a new procedure with the same result as familiar shooting.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Distinctive interactions can be memorable, but each additional moving part raises learning demands.

**Design prompt:** Explain what the new procedure makes possible and how it connects to other mechanics; remove novelty that only changes terminology or steps.

[Supporting discussion, 7:00–10:00](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=qTpiFtBmbhk&t=420s)

### Signal where complexity matters

Rule depth and presentation can tell players which parts of a game deserve the most attention. The hosts contrast a game whose deployment and mission economy carry most innovation with simpler movement and shooting, letting players focus on the intended puzzle.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Concentrated complexity creates a clear focal point, while supporting systems may feel intentionally plain.

**Design prompt:** Use rulebook space and subsystem detail proportionally to the game's core decisions; make supporting procedures short and dependable.

[Supporting discussion, 27:00–31:00](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=qTpiFtBmbhk&t=1620s)

### Earn trust before breaking conventions

Players may accept a demanding new system when familiar procedures show that the designer is not adding difficulty arbitrarily. The hosts describe recognizable rules as a trust signal that lets readers see where the design's unusual mechanics begin and what they are for.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Trust can support experimentation, but cannot rescue novelty that is confusing or unrewarding.

**Design prompt:** Keep routine procedures legible and focus explanations on the mechanics that change the experience; show players what they gain from learning them.

[Supporting discussion, 23:00–28:00](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=qTpiFtBmbhk&t=1380s)

**Differences in approach or unresolved questions**

- The hosts agree on the cost of novelty but treat the ‘three innovations’ limit as a heuristic; one emphasizes breaking stale conventions, while the other stresses player trust and the burden of learning.

**Episode-specific limits**

- This is explicitly the first half of a two-part conversation and covers learning complexity more than in-game cognitive load.
- The suggested innovation count is subjective advice from the hosts, not a tested threshold.

## Do Wargames Need Psychology Rules?

**Source:** [Do Wargames Need Psychology Rules?](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=T5kJZYtUlds) · **Video ID:** `T5kJZYtUlds`

The hosts debate whether and how psychology rules contribute simulation, narrative, board pressure, and tactical variety, against their costs in agency, rules weight, cascading outcomes, and bookkeeping.

### Psychology is not mandatory for every game

Psychology rules serve particular themes and scales; machinery-centered or cinematic games may not benefit from human morale procedures. The hosts describe removing disorder from a spaceship game because it reduced unit options and effectiveness without adding fun.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Omitting psychology preserves control and action density, but may make human troops in frightening situations feel implausibly steady.

**Design prompt:** Identify whether morale is central to the game's subject and intended feel before adding a subsystem.

[Supporting discussion, 0:00–4:00](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=T5kJZYtUlds&t=0s)

### Protect agency with short, bounded effects

Psychology becomes more costly when it persistently removes a player's control or options. The hosts contrast a brief loss of control that creates a dramatic moment with prolonged restrictions that reduce decisions across many activations.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Brief effects preserve momentum and agency; stronger or longer effects can better represent collapse but risk disengagement.

**Design prompt:** Measure how long an effect lasts, what choices it removes, and when control returns; prefer bounded disruption unless sustained loss is the game's central purpose.

[Supporting discussion, 3:00–5:00](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=T5kJZYtUlds&t=180s)

### Use fear as spatial pressure

A psychology effect can shape movement and engagement without forcing units to flee or become inactive. Fear auras, dangerous zones, and passive fire discourage entry and project influence across space, functioning as area-control mechanics.

**Tradeoff to consider:** This preserves player choice while creating threat, but the effect may be psychological only in name if its fiction and rules diverge.

**Design prompt:** Consider whether morale can change where players are willing to move, rather than only subtracting actions after a test.

[Supporting discussion, 5:00–8:00](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=T5kJZYtUlds&t=300s)

### Make effects fit the subject's physical logic

A similar emotional pressure should be expressed through behavior appropriate to the game's subjects. The hosts observe that a frightened car cannot pivot and run, but may skid out of control; a spaceship or carpet also needs a fitting response.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Subject-specific expression strengthens fiction but may require different rules for machines, humans, and fantastical creatures.

**Design prompt:** Translate the emotional premise into plausible actions for the represented entity instead of copying a human flee rule across all subjects.

[Supporting discussion, 6:00–9:00](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=T5kJZYtUlds&t=360s)

### Psychology can improve simulation and character expectation

Morale rules help when players expect the represented people or creatures to react to danger. The hosts argue that modern or historical soldiers in terrifying conditions may seem inhuman without psychology, while fantasy caricatures create strong expectations about how they behave.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Simulation can deepen plausibility, but the needed level depends on the audience's genre assumptions and the game's tone.

**Design prompt:** Use the game's setting and player expectations to decide what reactions need representation; do not assume all genres need equal realism.

[Supporting discussion, 10:00–13:00](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=T5kJZYtUlds&t=600s)

### Unpredictability can create stories

A morale outcome may be enjoyable when it produces memorable events and gives players a story to recount. The hosts note that unpredictable behavior can create emergent narrative and sometimes let a player attribute failure to the game rather than their decisions.

**Tradeoff to consider:** The same randomness can feel like an amusing story or an arbitrary loss depending on player preference and whether counterplay existed.

**Design prompt:** Test whether surprise creates a story players value or merely removes their ability to make meaningful choices.

[Supporting discussion, 12:00–15:00](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=T5kJZYtUlds&t=720s)

### Watch for morale cascades

Breaking effects can accelerate an ending, but can also make an early leader impossible to catch. One failed unit may trigger further breaks and removals, creating a chain reaction that ends the game quickly.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Cascades produce dramatic collapse and reduce cleanup time; they amplify leads and can erase comeback opportunities.

**Design prompt:** Measure both pacing benefits and win-more effects in playtests, especially when morale failures compound across nearby units.

[Supporting discussion, 12:00–14:00](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=T5kJZYtUlds&t=720s)

### Offer deliberate ways to accept or mitigate risk

A severe morale risk is easier to accept when players choose whether to pay for protection or build a force that tolerates it. The hosts discuss immunity or mitigation options that let players knowingly accept a low-probability catastrophic result when they decline protection.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Choice supports ownership of risk, but buying immunity can become a tax or narrow viable army construction.

**Design prompt:** If a psychology result can remove a major unit, consider visible countermeasures and test whether they are optional choices rather than mandatory purchases.

[Supporting discussion, 13:00–17:00](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=T5kJZYtUlds&t=780s)

### Psychology opens a legible design vocabulary

Morale is a familiar domain that can differentiate troops, scenarios, and environments through understandable reactions. Fear, panic, refusal, leadership, and scenario conditions provide a set of expected hooks players can readily interpret.

**Tradeoff to consider:** The familiar domain offers expansive design space, but each test trigger, state, and exception adds rules and bookkeeping.

**Design prompt:** Use the subsystem to create distinct tactical puzzles only when those choices matter; keep triggers and tracked states simple enough to execute reliably.

[Supporting discussion, 16:00–20:00](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=T5kJZYtUlds&t=960s)

**Differences in approach or unresolved questions**

- One host is wary of psychology systems that remove choices and favors effects expressed through the game's core mechanics; the other stresses simulation and dramatic cascade. They agree that psychology is a high-risk, theme-specific tool, not a required subsystem.

**Episode-specific limits**

- The episode generalizes from the hosts' own designs and examples; it does not establish player responses as universal.
- Several references are ASR-corrupted (including a nonstandard term for a separate system); those unclear terms are excluded.

## Writing Wargame Psychology Rules

**Source:** [Writing Wargame Psychology Rules](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=LP7Dj0_zYN8) · **Video ID:** `LP7Dj0_zYN8`

The hosts classify common psychology effects and examine their triggers, passive influence, narrative uses, and edge-case rules.

### Classify by what triggers the behavior

A useful design taxonomy distinguishes why a unit acts unpredictably rather than relying only on the name printed in a rulebook. The host proposes fear from interaction with a threatening model, panic from an event, refusal from an inherent trait, break tests from unit collapse, and a grab bag of frenzy or betrayal effects.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Categories help compare designs, but real games may use the same label for different procedures.

**Design prompt:** Describe each effect by trigger and resulting behavior; use familiar labels cautiously and explain the actual rule.

[Supporting discussion, 0:00–4:00](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=LP7Dj0_zYN8&t=0s)

### Separate passive fear from triggered panic

An aura that continuously changes opponents' behavior is mechanically different from a response to a specific frightening event. Fear can prevent an approach or attack around a threatening model, while panic follows a discrete event such as a large part of a unit being destroyed.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Passive effects influence many decisions and can be very powerful; event triggers are more bounded but may interrupt play.

**Design prompt:** Track how often an effect applies and whether it is automatic, tested, or triggered; balance recurring auras accordingly.

[Supporting discussion, 3:00–8:00](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=LP7Dj0_zYN8&t=180s)

### Count how often an effect applies

A rule's impact depends partly on the number of opportunities it has to affect play, not only its single resolution. The hosts highlight that an always-on aura can influence many units throughout a game, unlike a one-time event response.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Broad passive effects are easy to execute but may outweigh their apparent small modifier; narrow effects are less powerful but may be overlooked.

**Design prompt:** Estimate occurrences per game and affected models when assessing a rule's strength; test cumulative board-wide effects.

[Supporting discussion, 6:00–9:00](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=LP7Dj0_zYN8&t=360s)

### Include positive morale when it serves the fiction

Psychology need not mean only fear or loss of control; inspiration can represent a leader's influence and cinematic confidence. The hosts discuss leadership auras that provide a passive buff, while noting that positive effects usually improve reliability rather than make units unpredictable.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Positive effects widen the emotional range but may become ordinary buffs without a meaningful morale decision.

**Design prompt:** Decide whether morale represents emotional behavior, leadership, or both; keep the mechanical purpose clear across positive and negative effects.

[Supporting discussion, 8:00–11:00](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=LP7Dj0_zYN8&t=480s)

### Make special behavior justify its rule weight

Unusual psychology rules are valuable when they create distinctive character or decisions that ordinary movement and combat cannot provide. The hosts discuss frenzy, betrayal, squabbling, target attraction, and avoidance as colorful but often isolated procedures.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Specific effects can make factions memorable, but a large collection of one-off rules is hard to learn and remember.

**Design prompt:** Keep an unusual behavior when it changes play in a way central to the faction or scenario; otherwise fold it into a shared rule or omit it.

[Supporting discussion, 33:00–39:00](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=LP7Dj0_zYN8&t=1980s)

### Design faction behavior from its premise

A faction's distinctive psychology can express its character through target choice, aggression, cowardice, or internal conflict. The hosts contrast berserkers rushing toward enemies with cowardly or fractious troops, and identify target priority as an underexplored behavior.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Behavioral rules can create strong identity but may override a player's tactical plan and need to be tested for fairness.

**Design prompt:** Choose a behavior players can recognize from the faction premise and make its trigger and consequence clear enough to plan around.

[Supporting discussion, 34:00–38:00](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=LP7Dj0_zYN8&t=2040s)

### Keep taxonomy as a tool, not a rules mandate

The five categories are an organizing scheme from the speaker, not universal terminology or an instruction to include all five. The hosts note that fear, panic, and other labels vary across games and that effects can overlap in practice.

**Tradeoff to consider:** A taxonomy supports analysis but can obscure a design if applied rigidly.

**Design prompt:** Use the categories to inspect an effect's function, then choose terminology and procedures that fit the game's own system.

[Supporting discussion, 3:00–8:00](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=LP7Dj0_zYN8&t=180s)

### Treat loss of control as a real cost

Psychology often reduces player agency, so its narrative or tactical benefit should justify preventing a chosen action. Fear, refusal, and break effects can turn off an intended move or attack; leadership tests may gate access to stronger actions.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Unreliable behavior can create simulation and drama, but arbitrary denial can feel like losing toys or being punished for pursuing a fun option.

**Design prompt:** For each control-limiting rule, ask whether it creates enough decision tension or narrative payoff to offset the lost action.

[Supporting discussion, 20:00–22:00](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=LP7Dj0_zYN8&t=1200s)

### Choose simulation or abstraction deliberately

The right treatment of fleeing or broken units depends on the experience the game wants to deliver. Players may remove defeated units to streamline competitive play or move them under loose rules to enact collapse and pursuit.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Simulation preserves spectacle and spatial consequences but demands additional movement and adjudication; abstraction speeds resolution.

**Design prompt:** Decide whether the retreat itself is a core play moment; simplify or remove it when it adds no desired decision or story.

[Supporting discussion, 28:00–33:00](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=LP7Dj0_zYN8&t=1680s)

### Expect uncontrolled movement to create adjudication demands

Broken or panicked units move under behavior rules rather than direct player control, which makes precise table procedures difficult. The speakers note that informal movement can create disputes over exact positions and unintended tactical opportunities.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Loose interpretation can suit a casual narrative game; tight competitive play needs clearer procedures and may avoid such rules.

**Design prompt:** Test involuntary movement with close-charge and blocking edge cases, and specify enough procedure for the game’s desired precision.

[Supporting discussion, 21:00–25:00](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=LP7Dj0_zYN8&t=1260s)

### Use psychology to reconnect strategic play with individual stories

Psychological mechanics can help players imagine the small human experiences represented by abstract units. The hosts describe panic, inspiration, refusal, and fear as ways to evoke soldiers reacting to danger or leadership.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Narrative texture can deepen identification, but players may prefer consistent tactical control over individual behavior stories.

**Design prompt:** Use these rules when human response is a central part of the intended experience; consider non-restrictive inspiration when positive morale matters.

[Supporting discussion, 10:00–15:00](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=LP7Dj0_zYN8&t=600s)

### Keep one-off targeting rules contained

Highly specific psychological reactions can add character, but each isolated rule carries a learning and reference cost. The hosts discuss units compelled toward a lure or a particular target as unusual effects that may apply to only one spell or case.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Such rules produce flavorful behavior but can become a grab bag of exceptions rather than a reusable system.

**Design prompt:** Reserve bespoke target priorities for distinctive cases and check whether a common procedure could express the same behavior.

[Supporting discussion, 35:00–39:00](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=LP7Dj0_zYN8&t=2100s)

**Differences in approach or unresolved questions**

- The hosts differ on whether positive inspiration belongs within psychology; they agree it is a plausible morale effect but note that it usually behaves as a straightforward buff rather than unpredictable action.

**Episode-specific limits**

- The episode's categories are drawn largely from familiar Warhammer examples and are explicitly offered for convenience.
- ASR garbles several names and phrases; unsupported spellings have been omitted.

## Something from Nothing

**Source:** [Something from Nothing](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=CCSUM-fHN2Y) · **Video ID:** `CCSUM-fHN2Y`

The hosts examine game effects that cancel actions or leave the state unchanged, and propose ways to make success and failure advance play instead.

### Audit moments where nothing happens

A failed action or defense can consume time and attention without changing the game state, creating a dead moment. The hosts point to missed turns, predictable saving throws, and counterspells that cancel a built-up action as examples.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Cancellation is simple and can reward control, but repeated null results weaken the sense that decisions and dice matter.

**Design prompt:** Track how often an exchange ends with no changed position, resource, risk, or future option; ask whether the same result can create a consequence instead.

[Supporting discussion, 0:00–4:00](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=CCSUM-fHN2Y&t=0s)

### Make failure chaotic rather than inert

Failure can produce an uncontrolled consequence instead of simply preventing the attempted event. The hosts propose that success grant the active player control or an added effect, while failure introduces a chaotic outcome for the actor.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Consequences keep the state moving and can reward adaptive skill, but too much randomness can overwhelm the intended tactical structure.

**Design prompt:** For common failed actions, test a consequence that changes risk, position, or resources without erasing the entire action sequence.

[Supporting discussion, 3:00–8:00](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=CCSUM-fHN2Y&t=180s)

### Convert negation into a cost or redirect

When a defensive rule cancels a dramatic action, consider allowing it to occur with a cost, altered target, or follow-on effect. The hosts suggest a spell might happen but be redirected, cause damage, or grant a future bonus rather than disappearing entirely.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Partial effects preserve momentum but may weaken defensive identity or require additional resolution rules.

**Design prompt:** Give denial mechanics a meaningful exchange—such as redirecting, delaying, or taxing an effect—when full cancellation creates repeated no-change outcomes.

[Supporting discussion, 6:00–9:00](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=CCSUM-fHN2Y&t=360s)

### Let skill operate on changing situations

A changing or chaotic state can preserve opportunities for skilled players to adapt, whereas hard denial may eliminate both good and bad responses. The hosts argue that adaptable players can exploit unexpected results, while cancellation can remove an opponent's chance to respond at all.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Chaos can reward improvisation and reduce deterministic shutdown, but may also privilege players with greater experience or flexibility.

**Design prompt:** Test whether unpredictable consequences create decisions for both sides and preserve room for counterplay rather than deciding the result outright.

[Supporting discussion, 8:00–12:00](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=CCSUM-fHN2Y&t=480s)

### Do not remove models automatically when objectives drive play

In an objective game, a model's removal may not be necessary for the core victory condition and may simply reduce future play. The hosts question why attacks must kill or remove units when moving or displacing them could alter control of the objective while keeping both forces active.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Persistent models preserve choices and cinematic action, while removals can create clear attrition and shorten an ending.

**Design prompt:** Check whether casualty removal contributes to objectives, pacing, or theme; consider pushes, pulls, or reconfiguration when positioning is the more interesting outcome.

[Supporting discussion, 26:00–30:00](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=CCSUM-fHN2Y&t=1560s)

### Preserve capability until a dramatic terminal event

Progressively weakening a damaged unit can make its remaining turns less interesting than preserving its capabilities while increasing its risk. A Billion Suns keeps a damaged ship combat-effective but makes attacking risk additional damage, allowing the owner to choose whether to press on toward an explosion.

**Tradeoff to consider:** The approach sustains agency and creates a dramatic final risk, but may be less realistic if gradual system failure is central to the premise.

**Design prompt:** Compare degradation with escalating-risk models; favor the one that creates meaningful choices throughout the unit's final state.

[Supporting discussion, 32:00–36:00](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=CCSUM-fHN2Y&t=1920s)

### Turn failure into spectacle

A failed action can trigger an entertaining chain reaction that keeps attention and produces an event worth recounting. Gaslands movement and wipeout outcomes turn mistakes into spins, collisions, flips, and explosions rather than simply disabling a vehicle.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Spectacle makes failure active, but explosive chains can increase randomness and accelerate elimination.

**Design prompt:** Connect failure consequences to the game's central physical fantasy and ensure they create further choices or visible events.

[Supporting discussion, 34:00–37:00](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=CCSUM-fHN2Y&t=2040s)

### Question inherited cancellation rules

A familiar rule is not automatically a good rule if its only rationale is that it has always been used. The hosts challenge conventions such as forbidding a second reroll and ask whether the prohibition removes a potentially fun choice without adding value.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Conventions can provide quick comprehension and safeguards, but unexamined dogma can suppress useful play.

**Design prompt:** For every inherited prohibition, identify the problem it prevents and compare that benefit with the choices it removes.

[Supporting discussion, 35:00–37:00](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=CCSUM-fHN2Y&t=2100s)

**Differences in approach or unresolved questions**

- The hosts disagree on some inherited dice conventions and acknowledge that a no-reroll rule may have a valid purpose; their shared method is to ask what it accomplishes rather than retain it by habit.

**Episode-specific limits**

- The episode advances a design preference for active consequences, not a universal ban on cancellation or model removal.
- The transcript refers to roleplaying narrative dice and named games, but the discussion does not provide a complete comparative rules analysis.

## Bloat in tabletop wargames and how to avoid it.

**Source:** [Bloat in tabletop wargames and how to avoid it.](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=jbVEKA0tReM) · **Video ID:** `jbVEKA0tReM`

The hosts distinguish bloat from power creep and discuss content expansion, combinatorial complexity, optional material, and editorial restraint.

### Separate bloat from power creep

Power creep makes successive additions stronger; bloat adds too many options or procedures regardless of their relative power. The hosts contrast increasingly powerful releases with accumulated rules that make a game sprawling, hard to enter, and less satisfying to navigate.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Both can require resets, but their causes and remedies differ: one concerns balance over time, the other excess scope.

**Design prompt:** Diagnose whether the problem is escalating power or too much material before choosing a correction.

[Supporting discussion, 0:00–4:00](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=jbVEKA0tReM&t=0s)

### Remove options that duplicate or do not find a role

New content is not valuable when it duplicates an existing option or fails to create a useful play pattern. The hosts cite Gaslands weapons and vehicles removed from a later edition because they did not have a niche or were not fun.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Cutting weak options makes the core easier to use, but may disappoint players who enjoyed a niche item.

**Design prompt:** Evaluate additions by their distinct gameplay role and actual table value; prune duplicates and content that never becomes a satisfying choice.

[Supporting discussion, 5:00–8:00](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=jbVEKA0tReM&t=300s)

### Content expands interaction surface rapidly

Each new option creates possible interactions with every existing option, so the cost of expansion can grow faster than the option count. The hosts describe combinations multiplying as weapons, skills, factions, and subskills are added, increasing both learning burden and potential failure cases.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Broad combinations create emergent strategy, but raise testing and comprehension costs.

**Design prompt:** Assess additions against all relevant existing systems and track the interactions they introduce, not only the new rule in isolation.

[Supporting discussion, 8:00–12:00](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=jbVEKA0tReM&t=480s)

### Do not turn learning into homework

A game becomes inaccessible when players must continuously study a large catalog merely to make competent decisions. The hosts describe bloated games as requiring players to memorize extensive options and opponent content before play.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Knowledge depth rewards long-term mastery, but can make casual entry feel like a job and advantage established players.

**Design prompt:** Set an accessible baseline and make advanced content findable; do not require exhaustive memorization of every release for a fair basic game.

[Supporting discussion, 10:00–13:00](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=jbVEKA0tReM&t=600s)

### Optional labels do not control community usage

Calling content noncanonical or optional may not prevent players or organizers from incorporating it into standard play. The hosts note that communities decide how to treat supplements; an option that enters normal lists can affect the core game even if the designer labels it extra.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Optional modules can contain experimentation safely when communities keep them separate, but the author cannot guarantee that boundary.

**Design prompt:** Consider how content will be adopted by groups and tournaments, and design clear modular boundaries rather than relying only on a disclaimer.

[Supporting discussion, 29:00–33:00](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=jbVEKA0tReM&t=1740s)

### Estimate expansion by blast radius

The same addition can be modest in a modular game and disruptive in a tightly shared system. The hosts contrast options that slot into isolated factions with a universal new weapon that becomes available to every Gaslands player.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Modularity contains interactions but can fragment content; global additions are easier to share but affect more matchups.

**Design prompt:** Map which units, lists, or procedures can use an addition and prioritize testing where it reaches the broadest part of the game.

[Supporting discussion, 31:00–34:00](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=jbVEKA0tReM&t=1860s)

### Design for a satisfying range, not exhaustive choice

A strong option list need not contain every possibility or match the designer's personal optimized choices. The hosts discuss including alternatives for different play styles while using a holistic sense of whether players will miss obvious choices and whether lists feel satisfying.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Curated breadth supports imagination, but holistic judgment is subjective and may miss balance issues.

**Design prompt:** Use playtest feedback and audience expectations alongside editorial judgment to decide whether an option adds a distinct desirable experience.

[Supporting discussion, 34:00–38:00](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=jbVEKA0tReM&t=2040s)

### End content cycles deliberately

A game may benefit from a clear stopping point and a new cycle instead of indefinite additions. The hosts suggest ending a line and beginning a new edition, world, or era to include fresh ideas without carrying every old option forward.

**Tradeoff to consider:** A reset reduces accumulated complexity but may invalidate investments and fragment the player base.

**Design prompt:** When expansion no longer improves the core, consider a deliberate new cycle with explicit carry-forward choices rather than endless accretion.

[Supporting discussion, 36:00–40:00](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=jbVEKA0tReM&t=2160s)

### Test additions against distraction, dilution, and duplication

An expansion option earns its place when it adds a distinct play experience without obscuring existing strengths. The speakers assess whether a new option distracts from core mechanics, makes faction identities less distinct, or duplicates an existing choice.

**Tradeoff to consider:** More options can increase expressive range, but redundant choices add selection and learning costs without meaningful variety.

**Design prompt:** Compare the option with existing choices and remove it if it lacks a unique role or weakens faction differences.

[Supporting discussion, 12:00–16:00](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=jbVEKA0tReM&t=720s)

### Set option counts around editorial intent and learnability

There is no universal correct number of options; the useful range depends on scope, memory, and the intended player. The hosts describe choosing a roster size that feels manageable to explain and master, while recognizing that veterans may want more.

**Tradeoff to consider:** A compact set aids onboarding; a broader set serves experienced players but increases the material new players must learn.

**Design prompt:** Pick a deliberate launch scope, then observe where players feel both constrained and overloaded before expanding.

[Supporting discussion, 19:00–25:00](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=jbVEKA0tReM&t=1140s)

### Treat optional content as a community convention

Calling material optional does not guarantee that players will treat it as separate from the core game. The speakers note that local groups and tournament organizers decide whether supplemental options are permitted or expected.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Optional modules can contain experimental or niche content, but they may still create community-wide balance and knowledge demands.

**Design prompt:** Label intended use clearly and check how actual groups adopt the material rather than assuming the designation will contain it.

[Supporting discussion, 27:00–31:00](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=jbVEKA0tReM&t=1620s)

### Use personal taste as an editorial signal, not a universal rule

Designers can use their own excitement and ability to explain an option as a useful test of scope. The discussion frames authorial choice as deciding what one wants to create and enthusiastically share, while collaboration contributes different design strengths.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Personal taste gives a coherent voice, but one person’s tolerance for complexity should not define every audience.

**Design prompt:** State who the game is for, invite collaborators or playtesters with different preferences, and edit toward the intended audience.

[Supporting discussion, 23:00–28:00](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=jbVEKA0tReM&t=1380s)

**Differences in approach or unresolved questions**

- The hosts differ on how much extra content is useful and whether aesthetic ‘mouth feel’ is a sufficient guide; they agree that content should be edited and that optional status does not automatically prevent bloat.

**Episode-specific limits**

- The episode offers judgment and examples rather than a measurable threshold for bloat.
- Some mid-transcript material was truncated in the capture; findings rely on clearly legible opening and later passages.

## Spotting Features and Fixing Flaws in Your Game

**Source:** [Spotting Features and Fixing Flaws in Your Game](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=Jrf_g89eprg) · **Video ID:** `Jrf_g89eprg`

The hosts discuss distinguishing a productive quirk from a flaw, evolving early mechanics, and using player behavior and audience needs to diagnose problems.

### Observe player adaptation, not just the stated rule

A mechanic that appears to reward a behavior can become a flaw if experienced players avoid it to succeed. Early Gaslands play punished the racer in front, so skilled players stayed behind; tuning preserved danger while making the finish tense.

**Tradeoff to consider:** A catch-up feature can create drama, but excessive punishment distorts player incentives and stalls the race.

**Design prompt:** Watch what experienced players actually do and whether the intended behavior remains attractive; treat avoidance as evidence that the mechanic's incentives need revision.

[Supporting discussion, 0:00–3:00](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=Jrf_g89eprg&t=0s)

### A flaw may evolve into the game's signature

An unintended weakness can become a feature when refinement turns it into a reliable source of the game's intended excitement. Gaslands' dangerous lead eventually produces close finishes and last-moment explosions, turning a runaway disadvantage into a race climax.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Preserving a quirk can create identity, but only if players still have reason to engage with it rather than avoid it.

**Design prompt:** Trace the behavior a flaw creates, then adjust surrounding rules until that behavior produces the intended payoff without making optimal play monotonous.

[Supporting discussion, 1:00–3:00](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=Jrf_g89eprg&t=60s)

### Challenge presumed core mechanics

Early rules that seem essential may be scaffolding, not the game's true design heart. The hosts describe removing original ideas from A Billion Suns after play revealed other mechanics as more compelling, including a complex two-table procedure that proved easy to use.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Removing foundational-looking rules can strengthen the game but risks losing an idea the designer still values.

**Design prompt:** Return to the design brief after prototypes exist; test whether each supposed pillar remains necessary and be willing to discard it.

[Supporting discussion, 3:00–7:00](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=Jrf_g89eprg&t=180s)

### Use the pitch as a starting set of hypotheses

A design can begin with several exciting hooks without requiring every hook to survive development. The hosts suggest proposing a small set of distinctive features, implementing them, and allowing better ideas to emerge during iteration.

**Tradeoff to consider:** A clear initial promise guides early work, but treating it as a contract can prevent discovery.

**Design prompt:** Write down the initial hooks, then label them as hypotheses; compare them with what playtesting actually makes fun.

[Supporting discussion, 6:00–9:00](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=Jrf_g89eprg&t=360s)

### Separate scaffold from design principle

A temporary rule used to get a prototype running should not be confused with a principle the game must preserve. The hosts describe scaffolding mechanics and note the need to distinguish them from governing design principles that should constrain revisions.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Keeping a scaffold may make testing easier, but mistaking it for a principle blocks improvement; discarding true principles can dilute the game.

**Design prompt:** Document why each major rule exists and whether it is provisional or a design commitment, then revisit that status as the prototype matures.

[Supporting discussion, 8:00–11:00](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=Jrf_g89eprg&t=480s)

### Do not dismiss feedback as wrong play

A designer should investigate flaws instead of assuming dissatisfied players are using the game incorrectly. The hosts criticize designers who knowingly ignore problems by saying that affected players are not the intended audience.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Not every preference must be accommodated, but dismissing recurring friction loses evidence about the game's usability and appeal.

**Design prompt:** Classify feedback by severity, frequency, and target audience; fix tractable problems and state deliberate tradeoffs clearly.

[Supporting discussion, 22:00–25:00](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=Jrf_g89eprg&t=1320s)

### Design for the actual audience's entry barriers

A feature can be an obstacle for newcomers even when experienced miniature gamers take it for granted. Gaslands attracted non-miniature gamers, for whom sourcing and building terrain was the largest barrier; generic objects could function as track features.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Low-specificity terrain improves accessibility and setup, but gives up some simulation detail and visual variety.

**Design prompt:** Identify assumptions your core audience overlooks and make minimal components or flexible substitutes possible for newcomers.

[Supporting discussion, 25:00–28:00](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=Jrf_g89eprg&t=1500s)

### Build a distinctive feature from constraints

A constraint can become an advantage if the game turns it into a clear and satisfying identity. The hosts argue that the same lead-risk mechanic that once discouraged front-running became a defining climactic feature after adjustment.

**Tradeoff to consider:** A deliberate quirk can attract devoted fans while repelling others; its audience fit should be understood rather than denied.

**Design prompt:** Ask which players a quirk frustrates and which it excites, and consider whether sharpening it creates a coherent feature for the intended group.

[Supporting discussion, 24:00–27:00](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=Jrf_g89eprg&t=1440s)

### Distinguish a core compromise from a fixable defect

Some limitations are inseparable from a game’s premise; others can be improved without losing the intended experience. The hosts contrast Gaslands’ deliberate arcade logic with adjustable collision rules and other playtest issues.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Accepting a premise preserves coherence, while treating every criticism as inherent can leave avoidable problems unresolved.

**Design prompt:** Ask whether a concern conflicts with a stated design principle or merely reflects an imperfect implementation, then test changes accordingly.

[Supporting discussion, 10:00–15:00](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=Jrf_g89eprg&t=600s)

### Offer alternatives that feel worthwhile

A tactic becomes a design problem when players feel foolish for choosing anything else. Feedback on a repeated overwatch approach leads to proposals such as split objectives or escalating pressure that make other choices useful.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Leaving dominant strategies available supports player freedom; counterpressure can add scenario demands and reduce straightforward play.

**Design prompt:** Test whether alternative tactics deliver their own reward, and change incentives or objectives when they feel strictly inferior.

[Supporting discussion, 18:00–23:00](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=Jrf_g89eprg&t=1080s)

### Make the physical setup legible to new audiences

A miniature game’s terrain requirements can become a major entry barrier for people unfamiliar with the hobby. Gaslands reduces the need for specialized terrain by letting ordinary objects function as obstacles in an arcade-style race.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Flexible props improve accessibility, while less granular terrain simulation may reduce realism.

**Design prompt:** Identify what a newcomer must gather and prepare before play; make components easy to substitute where exact models do not matter.

[Supporting discussion, 25:00–28:00](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=Jrf_g89eprg&t=1500s)

**Differences in approach or unresolved questions**

- The hosts differ on whether a problematic behavior is itself a feature or merely a flaw needing correction; they converge on testing its actual effect on incentives and revising surrounding rules.

**Episode-specific limits**

- The hosts present design anecdotes, not a formal diagnostic checklist or controlled playtest data.
- Some middle sections were truncated in output; findings rely on legible opening, later examples, and the transcript's clear closing discussion.

## Designing Your Wargame's "Meta"

**Source:** [Designing Your Wargame's "Meta"](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=D7u-kmwuY04) · **Video ID:** `D7u-kmwuY04`

The hosts define list-building metagames, discuss healthy evolution and stagnation, compare update mechanisms and faction structures, and consider player psychology.

### Define the meta as interaction among choices

A list-building meta is the strategic game players create by selecting forces and responding to other players' selections. The hosts illustrate a rock-paper-scissors cycle in which a popular rock list prompts paper lists, which then create a new opening for scissors.

**Tradeoff to consider:** This interaction creates evolving puzzles but can make a nominally balanced roster feel unstable as choices shift.

**Design prompt:** Analyze how lists answer and expose one another, not only the isolated strength of each option.

[Supporting discussion, 0:00–4:00](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=D7u-kmwuY04&t=0s)

### Healthy metas keep producing answers

A healthy meta is one where dominant strategies provoke viable counters and the resulting questions continue to change. The hosts define an evolving meta as a cycle of a strong list, a counter, and further responses rather than a permanent best choice.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Constant change sustains discovery but can become exhausting if balance shifts too frequently or unpredictably.

**Design prompt:** Check whether players can identify and test answers to strong strategies; distinguish meaningful evolution from random turbulence.

[Supporting discussion, 3:00–6:00](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=D7u-kmwuY04&t=180s)

### Use updating carefully

Online points updates can alter list incentives without a new edition, but require evidence and community trust. The hosts discuss changing values through a shared digital list builder; one stresses that the real challenge is generating enough tournament data to make informed adjustments.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Updates can address a dominant build quickly, but frequent changes can confuse players and require sustained analysis and maintenance.

**Design prompt:** If values are mutable, publish one clear source of truth and a transparent update process; update only when evidence and capacity support it.

[Supporting discussion, 4:00–8:00](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=D7u-kmwuY04&t=240s)

### Do not assume local reports represent the whole scene

Small gaming groups can have very different perceptions of what is overpowered because their metas are separate and sample sizes are limited. The hosts describe one group calling an option dominant while another group finds the same option weak, with no larger circuit to reconcile the claims.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Local knowledge is valuable, but may not generalize; broad data is harder for small publishers to collect.

**Design prompt:** Record which communities and matchups support a balance claim; avoid changing rules solely from an isolated pocket meta.

[Supporting discussion, 7:00–11:00](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=D7u-kmwuY04&t=420s)

### Stagnation is different from stability

A solved or stagnant meta has no discoverable answers, while a stable meta may still be enjoyable if its choices remain satisfying. The hosts distinguish a few lists with no counterplay from a fixed matchup that remains enjoyable, citing a replayable historical board-game scenario.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Continual novelty is not necessary for every audience, but a dominant unanswered option threatens competitive variety.

**Design prompt:** Ask whether players can still discover meaningful counters and enjoy repeat matchups before labeling an unchanging meta a failure.

[Supporting discussion, 29:00–32:00](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=D7u-kmwuY04&t=1740s)

### Seed clues and counters in the option set

Players need enough visible clues to understand how to respond to a powerful strategy. The hosts propose that a dominant list should have a non-obvious but discoverable counter; factions and differentiated options create a learning path for building that answer.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Discoverable counters reward exploration, but obscure answers can make a strong list feel unbeatable.

**Design prompt:** For each powerful play pattern, ensure the rules or roster contain a counter players can plausibly discover through play or reading.

[Supporting discussion, 25:00–31:00](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=D7u-kmwuY04&t=1500s)

### Faction differentiation can support continued learning

Distinct factions give players room to interpret matchups as a strategic puzzle rather than a single set of identical choices. Faction lists let players adapt an identity-specific force against a countering opponent; even shared spell options can signal different play styles.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Faction asymmetry creates richer matchup questions but increases balance work and the chance that a player feels mismatched.

**Design prompt:** Use differentiated options when they create meaningful learning and counterplay; ensure each faction retains tools to respond outside its favored matchup.

[Supporting discussion, 31:00–35:00](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=D7u-kmwuY04&t=1860s)

### Use variety to buffer self-blame after a loss

Distinct forces can help players interpret defeat as a matchup or list-building problem they can revisit, rather than proof that the game is broken or they are incapable. The hosts suggest that faction differences create a story of counterplay and give players a reason to revise lists and try again.

**Tradeoff to consider:** This can sustain engagement, but may also conceal genuine imbalance behind an excuse that the player simply chose poorly.

**Design prompt:** Offer meaningful roster variation while independently checking whether the supposed counters work; do not rely on player self-attribution as balance evidence.

[Supporting discussion, 34:00–36:00](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=D7u-kmwuY04&t=2040s)

### Let solutions travel between player groups

Cross-pollination helps a game’s metagame evolve when local groups otherwise remain isolated. Players encountering other lists and tactics can test whether a local dominant option has counters beyond their own group.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Community sharing supports discovery, but broad coordination can be difficult or may overcentralize the scene.

**Design prompt:** Support tournaments, forums, or shared list discussions at a scale suited to the community, and avoid treating one local report as universal.

[Supporting discussion, 17:00–20:00](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=D7u-kmwuY04&t=1020s)

### Match balance maintenance to available evidence

Frequent points updates are only useful when the designer can gather enough reliable play data to guide them. The speakers contrast a large tournament data pipeline with an indie designer’s lightweight shared spreadsheet and community feedback.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Online point changes are feasible, but unexplained updates can confuse players and weak samples can steer changes poorly.

**Design prompt:** Choose a transparent update process and state whether values are fixed or subject to mutual agreement and revision.

[Supporting discussion, 7:30–9:00](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=D7u-kmwuY04&t=450s)

### Create bounded choices that invite counterplay

A meta can emerge from a small number of constrained choices when no player can take every answer at once. Racing, ramming, and shooting styles counter one another; players must decide which threats to prepare for.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Interlocking counters create replayability, while unrestricted access to all tools can erase the need to adapt.

**Design prompt:** Design a few viable approaches with clear strengths and vulnerabilities, then restrict selections enough to preserve meaningful tradeoffs.

[Supporting discussion, 32:00–34:00](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=D7u-kmwuY04&t=1920s)

### Provide an accessible first counter-question

A player who discovers one plausible answer to a dominant tactic is more likely to keep exploring the game. The hosts recommend designing a common early build path that meets an obvious obstacle with a non-obvious but findable response.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Hints and teachable counters support confidence, but overly obscure solutions can still make players feel helpless.

**Design prompt:** Playtest the initial list-building path and ensure at least one counter can be found from the information the game provides.

[Supporting discussion, 28:00–31:00](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=D7u-kmwuY04&t=1680s)

**Differences in approach or unresolved questions**

- One host believes indie designers can maintain shared online point values with little effort; the other emphasizes the substantial data and analysis resources required for trustworthy balance updates.

**Episode-specific limits**

- The discussion is conceptual and does not establish a quantitative method for measuring meta health.
- The suggested counter-discovery pathway is an authorial hypothesis, not evidence that all players respond to loss in the same way.

## Designing Factions and List Building in Wargames

**Source:** [Designing Factions and List Building in Wargames](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=-0W6yuqPcAw) · **Video ID:** `-0W6yuqPcAw`

The hosts discuss why list building appeals, when to design it, its hobby and product benefits, its competitive risks, and alternatives such as presets.

### Design the engine before optimizing lists

List building is an early player activity but often a late design task because its balance depends on stable core mechanics. The hosts recommend first testing one force against itself so movement, shooting, and combat can be judged without faction asymmetry obscuring problems.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Early lists can help get a prototype running, but premature optimization multiplies variables before the engine is understood.

**Design prompt:** Use provisional forces to test the core game; refine roster breadth and faction identity after the common rules are stable.

[Supporting discussion, 1:00–4:00](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=-0W6yuqPcAw&t=60s)

### Make the base game fun without factions

A faction system cannot reliably rescue an uninteresting core play loop. The hosts warn that faction options are naturally exciting, but players may never reach them if the first match is dull.

**Tradeoff to consider:** A strong shared engine creates a dependable foundation, though asymmetry can later add depth and replay variety.

**Design prompt:** Test symmetrical forces before adding faction rules and ask whether the game is already engaging in its simplest form.

[Supporting discussion, 3:00–5:00](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=-0W6yuqPcAw&t=180s)

### List building is play outside the table

Constructing and revising lists can itself be a substantial solo game that extends engagement between matches. Players compare options, plan matchups, and tune forces with paper, calculators, or builders, much like deck construction.

**Tradeoff to consider:** This offers deep armchair play but rewards time and expertise before the match begins.

**Design prompt:** Decide whether pregame optimization is a central intended activity or whether the game should move players quickly to shared table play.

[Supporting discussion, 10:00–12:00](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=-0W6yuqPcAw&t=600s)

### Lists can create hobby loops

Distinct factions and options can motivate new modeling, painting, and repeat play cycles. The hosts describe players completing one themed team, then starting another, with new lists prompting new cars, models, and refinements.

**Tradeoff to consider:** A roster can sustain a community, but more releases also increase production, balance, and learning demands.
