**Design prompt:** When adding a faction or list option, consider the concrete hobby project and play experience it invites, not only its rules novelty.

[Supporting discussion, 10:00–12:00](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=-0W6yuqPcAw&t=600s)

### List systems support modular growth

A stable core engine plus modular lists can support recurring content and repeated matchup discovery. The hosts compare factions and army options to expandable product lines where new content interacts with existing choices and shifts the meta.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Modularity keeps a game fresh, but each addition introduces balance questions and can create an arms race.

**Design prompt:** If planning expansions, preserve a simple core and define how new options enter the existing ecosystem; budget for interaction testing.

[Supporting discussion, 7:00–12:00](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=-0W6yuqPcAw&t=420s)

### Internet optimization can erase the intended puzzle

Publicly shared best lists can let players outsource list-building decisions and sharply reduce the depth designers expected them to explore. A copied tournament list can let a newcomer use an expert's optimized build, while opponents face its power without sharing the preparation.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Access to strong lists helps learning and community sharing, but can turn matches into tests of list access rather than live adaptation.

**Design prompt:** Make the tabletop engine engaging even when lists converge; consider whether pregame depth is robust to published solutions.

[Supporting discussion, 31:00–36:00](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=-0W6yuqPcAw&t=1860s)

### Offer constrained or preset forces for quick starts

Presets and limited menus can help time-poor players reach play without requiring a full optimization exercise. The hosts discuss ready-made retinues, bounded random options, and faction menus that roughly control force strength.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Presets improve access and speed but reduce personalization; random forces may occasionally produce a poor or frustrating game.

**Design prompt:** Provide a quick-start roster or small constrained menu alongside full construction when list-building is not the main attraction.

[Supporting discussion, 37:00–40:00](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=-0W6yuqPcAw&t=2220s)

### Differentiate lists when the meta needs interpretation

Faction asymmetry can give players a framework for interpreting defeat and searching for a counter, rather than concluding the whole game is broken. Distinct forces create a matchup puzzle and give players a reason to revise their faction strategy after losing.

**Tradeoff to consider:** This can sustain exploration, but may mask actual balance problems or create hard counters.

**Design prompt:** Use distinct lists to create meaningful matchup questions, then independently verify that the apparent counters actually work.

[Supporting discussion, 34:00–37:00](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=-0W6yuqPcAw&t=2040s)

### Use a piloting goal to shorten a hesitant first turn

A prepared force with clear intentions can help players start acting instead of facing an empty decision space. List combinations give players a plan for how units support one another; scenarios and objectives can also point toward the first useful actions.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Preparation can make opening turns decisive, while requiring players to learn and assemble a full list before play.

**Design prompt:** If a game’s first turn tends to stall, provide example lists or objectives that establish a concrete opening goal.

[Supporting discussion, 11:00–15:00](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=-0W6yuqPcAw&t=660s)

### Let scenario variety carry part of the list challenge

The breadth of scenarios affects how much strategic coverage a list must provide. A list designed for one known battlefield can specialize; randomized or varied scenarios make narrow strategies riskier across a series.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Broader scenario coverage supports adaptability but can make list construction more complex.

**Design prompt:** Decide whether players build for a fixed matchup or a rotating scenario pool, and tune list constraints to that expectation.

[Supporting discussion, 14:00–17:00](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=-0W6yuqPcAw&t=840s)

### Design list puzzles for the audience that enjoys solving them

Some players find combinations and stronger lists rewarding; others do not engage with optimization as a puzzle. The discussion contrasts deliberate combo discovery with a more theme-first style of play and design.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Hidden synergies reward mastery but may favor puzzle solvers and widen the gap between experienced and casual players.

**Design prompt:** Make list-building depth an intentional feature and provide clear entry paths for players who prefer to learn through tabletop play.

[Supporting discussion, 17:00–22:00](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=-0W6yuqPcAw&t=1020s)

### Weigh list building against onboarding cost

A list system can add solo preparation and replay value while making it harder to start a first game. Players may need the core rules and multiple force guides to understand a pickup match, whereas preset or shared forces reduce preparation.

**Tradeoff to consider:** More pregame choices create personalization and hobby activity but expand the reading burden and opponent-knowledge requirements.

**Design prompt:** Offer a beginner route such as preset lists or a restricted selection before requiring the full catalog.

[Supporting discussion, 22:00–25:00](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=-0W6yuqPcAw&t=1320s)

**Differences in approach or unresolved questions**

- One host favors list building as a hobby and solo play loop; the other emphasizes the risk that online optimization turns matches into homework or pre-decides outcomes. Both value a fun engine and recognize preset alternatives.

**Episode-specific limits**

- The conversation is split across list-building and meta topics; this review includes only the list-building themes, with meta-specific analysis in the neighboring episode.

## Designing Better Campaign Progression Systems

**Source:** [Designing Better Campaign Progression Systems](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=8rLqTB5EEg4) · **Video ID:** `8rLqTB5EEg4`

The hosts examine campaign rewards and setbacks, runaway progression, player attendance, bounded rewards, and unlock-based systems.

### Start with the purpose of progression

A progression system should exist because the game needs a persistent arc, not because leveling is assumed to belong in every campaign. The hosts identify common rewards such as better equipment, advancement, treasure, injuries, and losses, and ask what each is meant to do.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Progression can make characters and battles memorable, but adds rules and may not improve a game that already works as one-off play.

**Design prompt:** State the persistent experience progression is meant to create and test whether rewards or setbacks actually support it.

[Supporting discussion, 0:00–3:00](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=8rLqTB5EEg4&t=0s)

### Progression can memorialize player-created stories

Players may want rules and models to preserve events that made a battle feel personal. A character losing an eye, surviving a duel, or taking a signature weapon becomes a continuing identity when recorded in rules and represented on the model.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Persistent scars and equipment strengthen attachment, but can force a beloved miniature into an unwanted mechanical penalty.

**Design prompt:** Let players turn memorable events into persistent identity while considering whether cosmetic records can preserve the story without permanently weakening the character.

[Supporting discussion, 5:00–8:00](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=8rLqTB5EEg4&t=300s)

### Guard against runaway leader

Rewards that increase the winner's future strength can make later campaign games less competitive and less worth playing. A small initial advantage compounds when the winner gains more resources and the loser loses them, making future outcomes increasingly predictable.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Rewarding success makes achievement feel consequential, but can destroy participation for players who fall behind.

**Design prompt:** Simulate several consecutive wins and losses, not just one advancement step; inspect whether both players still have a plausible reason to play the next game.

[Supporting discussion, 8:00–12:00](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=8rLqTB5EEg4&t=480s)

### Do not confuse setback with meaningful drama

Injuries and losses create stories only when players retain enough agency and attachment to continue. The hosts describe campaigns where characters accumulate damage or die, which may be flavorful but can leave a player with a weakened force and no appealing next step.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Hard setbacks create stakes, while softer consequences preserve participation and narrative continuity.

**Design prompt:** Test the emotional and mechanical effect of each loss over multiple sessions; provide recovery, replacement, or alternate goals when setbacks compound.

[Supporting discussion, 12:00–16:00](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=8rLqTB5EEg4&t=720s)

### Build catch-up into the reward curve

A campaign can preserve competition by ensuring that rewards for winning do not create a widening force-strength gap. The hosts discuss brakes on leaders, boosts to lagging players, and capped rewards that limit how much one good result can widen progression.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Catch-up preserves future play but may make success feel less valuable or artificially equalize outcomes.

**Design prompt:** Bound winner gains and model both the incentive to win and the loser’s next-game prospects; explain any catch-up as part of the campaign's intended competition.

[Supporting discussion, 14:00–18:00](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=8rLqTB5EEg4&t=840s)

### Support irregular attendance

Campaign rules should account for real schedules so a missed session does not permanently eject a player from the group. The hosts describe reserve income and optional events that let absent players keep pace or rejoin without being punished for family or work obligations.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Attendance resilience simplifies group scheduling but can reduce the narrative weight of showing up to every event.

**Design prompt:** Provide a fair way for absent players to earn baseline resources or re-enter at the group's current level.

[Supporting discussion, 33:00–36:00](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=8rLqTB5EEg4&t=1980s)

### Unlock options rather than raw power

Progression can broaden a player's choices without making their force strictly stronger than an opponent's. A Billion Suns unlocks ship classes and technology paths, but players still pay for ships in each game; access to more choices does not automatically add fielded strength.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Choice-based progression limits runaway power, but broader options can still improve flexibility and matchup answers.

**Design prompt:** Consider unlocking tools or branches instead of permanent stat increases, then test whether flexibility itself creates a substantial advantage.

[Supporting discussion, 41:30–42:00](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=8rLqTB5EEg4&t=2490s)

### Use caps to bound rewards

A strict maximum on rewards can dampen the advantage of an exceptional result without removing all benefit for success. The hosts describe capping resource gains even when players earn many qualifying outcomes, keeping a strong performance from multiplying the campaign gap.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Caps control snowballing but may make additional achievements feel irrelevant.

**Design prompt:** Set reward ceilings based on multi-game progression simulations and communicate what gains above the cap mean narratively, if anything.

[Supporting discussion, 38:00–40:00](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=8rLqTB5EEg4&t=2280s)

### Make advancement follow what a character did

A reward connected to a model’s actual actions makes progression feel like a record of its story. The hosts suggest awarding growth in a skill when a character performs the relevant action, with a short checklist to track it.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Action-linked advancement improves narrative fit but requires clear triggers and some tracking.

**Design prompt:** Tie a small set of earned advances to observable events, and use a concise checklist rather than a large progression ledger.

[Supporting discussion, 17:00–22:00](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=8rLqTB5EEg4&t=1020s)

### Give every player a meaningful reward path

Progression can reward both success and effort so that a setback does not leave a player with nothing to anticipate. The speakers propose multiple achievement boxes where each leads to a useful but different reward.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Broad reward access reduces discouragement, while overly generous or uncapped rewards can still widen the campaign gap.

**Design prompt:** Offer alternate achievements that grant comparably valuable choices and connect each to what happened in play.

[Supporting discussion, 20:00–24:00](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=8rLqTB5EEg4&t=1200s)

### Separate campaign victory from roster growth

A player should be able to progress without automatically moving closer to winning the campaign. Gaslands Refuel separates televised championship goals from wasteland resource progression and adds a resistance victory path.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Parallel tracks reduce runaway leadership and preserve goals for trailing players, but add campaign structure and bookkeeping.

**Design prompt:** If a campaign needs long-term growth, consider making growth unlock options while a separate objective determines the winner.

[Supporting discussion, 26:00–33:00](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=8rLqTB5EEg4&t=1560s)

### Make campaign rewards fit the campaign’s story

The more complex a progression system becomes, the more closely its rewards should express events and character identity. The speakers contrast random advancement with the distinct campaign structures used for a short branching story and a longer resource campaign.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Bespoke progression tells a stronger player-specific story, but takes more design and testing effort than generic random rewards.

**Design prompt:** Choose the depth of advancement based on campaign length and how much narrative continuity the game promises.

[Supporting discussion, 22:00–30:00](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=8rLqTB5EEg4&t=1320s)

### Make long campaigns resilient to missed sessions

Campaign structures should account for real-life absences so players can return without feeling permanently left behind. The hosts describe asynchronous wasteland games and banked resources alongside required shared events.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Flexible attendance helps a group persist, but can create gaps in campaign pacing or shared narrative.

**Design prompt:** Identify which sessions require everyone and provide a fair way for absent players to rejoin.

[Supporting discussion, 32:00–36:00](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=8rLqTB5EEg4&t=1920s)

**Differences in approach or unresolved questions**

- The hosts recognize the appeal of dramatic injury and winner rewards, while warning that these can undermine continued play; their examples favor resilient attendance and capped or option-based progression as alternatives.

**Episode-specific limits**

- The source is a broad discussion and does not provide a single complete progression formula.

## Designing Narrative Wargame Campaigns

**Source:** [Designing Narrative Wargame Campaigns](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=SkPcJk244sA) · **Video ID:** `SkPcJk244sA`

The hosts distinguish narrative campaigns from progression, compare linear, branching, and map structures, and discuss how campaigns can organize games without undermining participation.

### Separate narrative structure from force progression

A campaign can connect stories without changing the army's capabilities, and progression can exist without a scripted story. The hosts distinguish a sequence of scenarios that recreates an arc from systems that improve or diminish forces over time.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Combining them can enrich persistence but also compounds balance and writing complexity.

**Design prompt:** Decide independently whether players need a connected narrative, persistent advancement, or both; avoid bundling them by convention.

[Supporting discussion, 7:00–9:00](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=SkPcJk244sA&t=420s)

### Choose campaign structure for the story

Linear, branching, and map campaigns offer different amounts of author control and player choice. Linear campaigns present a fixed order, branching campaigns route players based on outcomes, and maps let players contest locations or select paths.

**Tradeoff to consider:** More branching offers agency but multiplies scenario-writing and testing; linear sequences are easier to author but constrain outcomes.

**Design prompt:** Choose the lightest structure that supports the intended story and the group's appetite for organizing repeated games.

[Supporting discussion, 5:00–9:00](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=SkPcJk244sA&t=300s)

### Start a linear campaign with a clear story arc

A fixed sequence is difficult to write until the designer knows what story or historical progression it should deliver. The hosts describe recreating historical engagements or a known fictional arc as a clear basis for ordered scenarios.

**Tradeoff to consider:** A defined arc gives purpose and coherence, but limits replay once players know the sequence and outcome.

**Design prompt:** Outline the intended arc before building scenarios; ensure each battle has a distinct purpose in the sequence.

[Supporting discussion, 8:00–12:00](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=SkPcJk244sA&t=480s)

### Use branching outcomes to change the next question

A result matters more when it changes the next scenario or its conditions rather than simply awarding a point. The hosts describe campaign branches where a fortress holding or falling changes the location or difficulty of the next battle.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Branches make outcomes consequential but increase content requirements and can make one route less developed.

**Design prompt:** Keep branches legible and ensure each path leads to a playable next episode; test the less likely paths too.

[Supporting discussion, 6:00–9:00](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=SkPcJk244sA&t=360s)

### Use a map to shape encounters, not add a second game

A campaign map can organize who fights, where, and why without needing a full strategic game layered on top. The hosts prefer maps that determine which forces meet and what scenario table applies, rather than adding resource production and tactical map battles.

**Tradeoff to consider:** A lighter map keeps focus on the miniature game; a fuller strategic layer may appeal to players but requires balancing a second system.

**Design prompt:** Define what the map contributes and keep its procedures limited to that role unless the strategic game is itself a core design goal.

[Supporting discussion, 47:00–54:00](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=SkPcJk244sA&t=2820s)

### Beware snowballing from territory count

Even a map that gives no direct combat bonus can create runaway advantage when holding more spaces makes a player numerically stronger. The hosts note that occupying more nodes can itself create a points lead, even if each node offers no additional benefit.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Territory control creates visible stakes but may make losing players feel they cannot recover.

**Design prompt:** Check whether map ownership translates into more resources, options, or matches; cap or counterbalance advantages that accumulate too quickly.

[Supporting discussion, 45:00–48:00](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=SkPcJk244sA&t=2700s)

### Judge a campaign by whether it increases desire to play

A campaign should make players look forward to the next game rather than make routine matches feel like a losing league obligation. The hosts distinguish story campaigns that create anticipation from ranking systems that can make players avoid opponents after falling behind.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Competition can motivate a championship, but persistent standings may reduce attendance or willingness to play.

**Design prompt:** Ask whether the campaign adds excitement beyond the matches players would already schedule; design recovery or low-stakes participation if standings persist.

[Supporting discussion, 48:00–51:00](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=SkPcJk244sA&t=2880s)

### Avoid combining two mastery contests casually

A campaign that links strategic map play and tabletop battles can let one player's dominance in either layer overwhelm the other. A player strong at both systems can dominate the campaign, while skill at one minigame can make the other layer irrelevant.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Multiple scales create richness, but demand that each layer remain balanced and meaningful for different players.

**Design prompt:** Keep a map layer organizational unless prepared to design and test both games as an integrated system.

[Supporting discussion, 51:00–54:00](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=SkPcJk244sA&t=3060s)

### Reward completion across the whole campaign

A campaign needs a reason for players to continue even when they are no longer likely to win. Linear stories can keep players together through a fixed ending, while branching paths can preserve a goal or minor victory for those who fall behind.

**Tradeoff to consider:** A fixed narrative sustains shared pacing but may offer little payoff if players drop out; open competition can crown a winner sooner.

**Design prompt:** Define what completion means and give participants a reason to stay through later games after setbacks.

[Supporting discussion, 10:00–14:00](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=SkPcJk244sA&t=600s)

### Make each linear scenario worth playing

Players should have an appealing experience in every chapter rather than wait for one distant “cool” scenario. Distinct scenario twists and hobby projects can give each step a fresh attraction and prepare players for the next installment.

**Tradeoff to consider:** A strong finale gives a narrative arc, but withholding all novel content until late can make earlier sessions feel like a grind.

**Design prompt:** Give each scenario a distinct hook and spread terrain or miniature projects across the campaign.

[Supporting discussion, 13:00–22:00](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=SkPcJk244sA&t=780s)

### Compress branching stories into a few threads

A fully branching campaign grows rapidly in writing effort and is difficult to finish. The hosts recommend hourglass points and a small set of thematic lanes that switch based on results instead of branching every scenario freely.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Fewer branches reduce content burden, while each outcome gives players less control over the overall story.

**Design prompt:** Plan a beginning, middle, and end, then use a few outcome-sensitive routes that reconverge at key moments.

[Supporting discussion, 29:00–35:00](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=SkPcJk244sA&t=1740s)

### Choose scenario specificity to match campaign structure

The scenario format should fit whether the campaign is linear, branching, or map-based. Linear arcs benefit from crafted scenes; reusable scenarios suit branches, and map campaigns can draw from tables linked to terrain or narrative context.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Bespoke encounters can strongly express story but take more design and testing; reusable encounters increase flexibility.

**Design prompt:** Build a scenario pool with an appropriate level of specificity and validate it in the game system before linking it into a campaign.

[Supporting discussion, 34:00–40:00](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=SkPcJk244sA&t=2040s)

### Use maps to organize players and stakes

A campaign map can explain why two forces meet and what kind of encounter they face. Locations or sectors can determine which players can engage, which scenario table applies, and what narrative stakes surround a battle.

**Tradeoff to consider:** This gives a campaign structure and choice without requiring a separate tactical game on the map; territory ownership can still cause escalation.

**Design prompt:** Use map features to select opponents and frame tabletop scenarios, while checking whether positional advantages snowball.

[Supporting discussion, 54:00–56:00](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=SkPcJk244sA&t=3240s)

### Do not assume a league creates campaign motivation

Competition tracking can reduce enthusiasm if it only records that a player is already behind. The hosts distinguish a campaign that makes participants eager for the next session from a league layered over games they already play.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Rankings can identify the strongest player, but persistent standings can make trailing players disengage.

**Design prompt:** Measure success by whether the campaign increases interest in future games, not only by whether it produces a ranking.

[Supporting discussion, 46:00–51:00](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=SkPcJk244sA&t=2760s)

### Keep crossover campaign layers from rewarding one skill twice

Combining a map game and tabletop game can magnify the advantage of players who master both. Winning the strategic layer may provide an advantage in the skirmish layer, letting one expert dominate both contests.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Interdependent layers create depth, but can compound uneven experience and leave others unable to compete.

**Design prompt:** If campaign choices affect battle conditions, limit their power so success in one layer does not decide the other automatically.

[Supporting discussion, 50:00–54:00](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=SkPcJk244sA&t=3000s)

**Differences in approach or unresolved questions**

- The hosts differ slightly on whether ranking leagues count as campaign systems; they agree that narrative campaigns should increase interest in future games, while competitive scoring can reduce it for some groups.

**Episode-specific limits**

- The transcript is a broad discussion of narrative structures and does not prescribe one best format.
- A large middle portion was truncated in output; examples are selected from clear opening and closing passages, and specific campaign anecdotes are not exhaustively captured.

## Your Game Needs A Catch-Up Mechanic

**Source:** [Your Game Needs A Catch-Up Mechanic](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=jxTuWF1a8jE) · **Video ID:** `jxTuWF1a8jE`

The hosts examine when trailing players need help, distinguish catch-up from continued engagement, and discuss objectives, reserve resources, and battle structure.

### Diagnose the reason for catch-up

Catch-up is most useful when players disengage, downtime grows, or outcomes become functionally decided well before the game ends. The hosts identify multi-player elimination and long periods without meaningful action as signals that a game may need comeback or re-engagement tools.

**Tradeoff to consider:** A catch-up rule can restore participation, but may distort a game whose objectives do not depend on attrition.

**Design prompt:** Observe when players stop making meaningful decisions and whether the result is already mathematically settled; target that specific problem.

[Supporting discussion, 0:00–4:00](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=jxTuWF1a8jE&t=0s)

### Use rewards to keep eliminated players engaged

A catch-up resource can give a player a way back into active play after temporary removal, even if it does not guarantee victory. Gaslands audience votes let players who wipe out spend earned resources to return a car and remain involved rather than disengage.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Re-entry keeps players at the table but can weaken elimination consequences or prolong a race.

**Design prompt:** If a mechanic temporarily removes a player, consider a bounded return path that restores meaningful choices without undoing every consequence.

[Supporting discussion, 2:00–5:00](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=jxTuWF1a8jE&t=120s)

### Avoid catch-up when the objective system already keeps play live

A game may not need a conventional comeback bonus if a player can still affect objectives and outcomes despite losing units. In A Billion Suns, losing ships does not necessarily mean losing the mission because utility and objective interactions matter beyond combat strength.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Objective play can preserve agency, but can still reach a mathematically closed state or contain loops that need correction.

**Design prompt:** Assess whether trailing players still have plausible ways to influence the scoring conditions before adding explicit catch-up rewards.

[Supporting discussion, 3:00–7:00](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=jxTuWF1a8jE&t=180s)

### Distinguish comeback from spectacular late play

A trailing player may still enjoy a dramatic final action even when it is unlikely to change the winner. Late deployment in A Billion Suns can let a player spend resources on a powerful fleet, creating a last-stand spectacle that may occasionally reverse a close result.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Allowing late power creates memorable reversals, but also permits wasteful or frustrating deployments after a player believes the game is lost.

**Design prompt:** Decide whether late actions should offer a real comeback chance, a narrative finale, or both; use social expectations when rules do not constrain them.

[Supporting discussion, 6:00–10:00](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=jxTuWF1a8jE&t=360s)

### Use objectives to resist table elimination

Scoring goals can keep a player relevant when combat losses would otherwise remove their ability to participate. Objectives may be hidden or distributed so opponents cannot simply identify and eliminate every source of the trailing player's remaining value.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Objective structure preserves strategic relevance but does not prevent full table elimination in every game.

**Design prompt:** Check whether a losing force retains objectives or alternate ways to score; do not assume objectives alone solve the engagement problem.

[Supporting discussion, 31:00–36:00](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=jxTuWF1a8jE&t=1860s)

### Let movement and formation create natural catch-up

Some battle structures contain self-balancing effects that isolate successful units or make them less useful elsewhere. In rank-and-flank games, pursuing a broken unit can pull the victor out of position; free-moving skirmish forces may instead reach threats across the board quickly.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Natural positional costs reduce need for artificial catch-up but depend on movement scale and table geometry.

**Design prompt:** Model movement, range, and activation together to see whether a successful attack opens or closes opportunities elsewhere.

[Supporting discussion, 29:00–34:00](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=jxTuWF1a8jE&t=1740s)

### Do not assume older battle structures solve modern skirmish problems

Catch-up built into slow rank-and-flank movement may disappear in highly mobile small-table games. Skirmish units can cross the board in a turn or two and threaten multiple areas, reducing the positional isolation that once balanced successful charges.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Mobility gives tactical freedom but can accelerate snowballing after a strong attack.

**Design prompt:** Evaluate catch-up at the actual game scale, with its model count, range, and movement; do not import assumptions from a different battle format.

[Supporting discussion, 31:00–36:00](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=jxTuWF1a8jE&t=1860s)

### Keep the goal as engagement, not guaranteed victory

A catch-up tool can succeed by giving the trailing player meaningful activity even if it does not equalize win probability. The hosts suggest extra resources, headwinds, or other choices that let a player remain active and preserve a close-feeling result.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Engagement tools may feel cosmetic if they cannot affect the outcome, while strong equalizers may make success feel unearned.

**Design prompt:** Choose whether the problem is boredom, mathematical hopelessness, or both; tune the intervention to improve agency without promising a comeback.

[Supporting discussion, 35:00–39:00](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=jxTuWF1a8jE&t=2100s)

### Give behind players a new resource before the game ends

A late-game resource can matter more to a player who has lost options and provide a route back into action. Gaslands audience votes arrive during play and are more useful to a trailing driver than to the leader.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Midgame resources create comeback opportunities, but they do not guarantee that a player can recover or win.

**Design prompt:** Consider an earned or contested resource that becomes available after setbacks and lets a player re-enter meaningful decisions.

[Supporting discussion, 20:00–26:00](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=jxTuWF1a8jE&t=1200s)

### Consider a formal way to call a game

Players may need a rule-defined way to stop once the game is no longer enjoyable or meaningfully contested. A concession or retreat can end the current match in exchange for a clear cost, such as conceding remaining objectives or rewards.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Formal exits prevent awkward reliance on social agreement, while they may shorten games that still have useful tactical decisions.

**Design prompt:** Specify when players may concede and what the outcome means for scores, campaign resources, and surviving models.

[Supporting discussion, 15:00–19:00](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=jxTuWF1a8jE&t=900s)

### Maintain stakes through tournament or campaign scoring

Even a player who cannot win the current match may still care about points that affect a larger competition. Point differentials can determine tournament placement, while campaigns attach future rewards or consequences to present choices.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Long-term stakes keep decisions meaningful, but may make a single loss affect the whole season.

**Design prompt:** Choose whether the game should motivate tactical scoring, campaign survival, or immediate victory, then make those stakes explicit.

[Supporting discussion, 18:00–22:00](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=jxTuWF1a8jE&t=1080s)

### Distinguish rubber bands from leader penalties

A catch-up resource and a leader penalty create different player experiences. A rubber band gives more help to players behind; a headwind makes being ahead harder, while dogpiling relies on other players to punish the leader.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Direct help can preserve agency; leader penalties or social targeting can feel punitive or politicized.

**Design prompt:** Name the intended catch-up effect and avoid relying on opponents to gang up as the sole balancing procedure.

[Supporting discussion, 21:00–25:00](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=jxTuWF1a8jE&t=1260s)

**Differences in approach or unresolved questions**

- The hosts differ on whether a late overwhelming deployment is a problem requiring rules control or a valid social and narrative choice; they also distinguish true catch-up from exciting but possibly futile play.

**Episode-specific limits**

- The discussion does not provide a universal formula or assert that every miniature game needs a catch-up mechanic.
- The transcript output was truncated in its middle; distinct visible opening and closing passages support the findings.

## Designing Tabletop Catch Up Mechanics

**Source:** [Designing Tabletop Catch Up Mechanics](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=CuEZVnOLuls) · **Video ID:** `CuEZVnOLuls`

The hosts explain why board games often use catch-up rules, when miniatures games might benefit, and how Gaslands uses multi-purpose resources to keep players engaged.

### Prevent games from being decided long before they end

Catch-up mechanics can preserve interest when a player has no realistic route to victory but must still play through the remaining turns. The hosts describe board-game headwinds that make later scoring increasingly costly and can compress a large lead into a close-looking finish.

**Tradeoff to consider:** The game feels competitive longer, but the apparent closeness may be cosmetic and can obscure the true size of a lead.

**Design prompt:** Decide whether you want actual comeback chances or simply a satisfying ending; do not mistake visual closeness for restored agency.

[Supporting discussion, 2:00–5:00](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=CuEZVnOLuls&t=120s)

### Multiplayer creates kingmaking risk

Catch-up has added value in games with three or more players, where someone who cannot win may influence which opponent does. A losing player can redirect effort toward helping or harming another player, producing an unsatisfying winner.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Catch-up can reduce kingmaking, but may also give a trailing player disproportionate influence.

**Design prompt:** Test multiplayer turn order and endgame choices separately from two-player play; consider whether losing players retain meaningful but bounded agency.

[Supporting discussion, 3:00–6:00](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=CuEZVnOLuls&t=180s)

### Do not punish a player for taking the lead

A catch-up tool should help trailing players remain involved without making success itself a liability. The hosts reject an early Gaslands version in which the front runner was effectively guaranteed to lose, calling it a defect that discouraged racing.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Pressure on leaders can keep a pack together, but if it reverses the incentive to lead it destroys the core competition.

**Design prompt:** Check whether the leader still has a reason to pursue the game's primary goal; reward or constrain based on position without making winning behavior irrational.

[Supporting discussion, 22:00–25:00](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=CuEZVnOLuls&t=1320s)

### Use flexible resources as catch-up and tactical currency

A resource earned during play can be more useful to a trailing player while remaining an optional bonus for the leader. Gaslands audience votes can be spent to change gear and re-enter activity; leading players can use the same resource for other advantages.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Multi-use resources preserve choice and avoid an explicit rubber band, but players may spend them poorly or fail to recognize their catch-up value.

**Design prompt:** Consider giving players an in-game resource that has baseline tactical uses and a stronger recovery use when behind.

[Supporting discussion, 23:00–28:00](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=CuEZVnOLuls&t=1380s)

### Make recovery resources arrive after the setback

A catch-up resource is harder to remove from the player if it is granted in response to events rather than issued only at setup. Audience votes enter the game intermittently, sometimes after things go badly, so a later setback does not erase the recovery tool as it could with a starting bonus.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Event-based grants can feel reactive or arbitrary; a starting pool is simpler but can be depleted before the player needs it.

**Design prompt:** Test when recovery resources are awarded and whether the trailing player still has access when the game begins to slip away.

[Supporting discussion, 26:00–30:00](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=CuEZVnOLuls&t=1560s)

### Do not add a comeback rule when victory conditions already preserve agency

Objective-focused games may remain playable without equalizing combat strength if losses do not prevent scoring. The hosts explain that a force with fewer ships may still perform well by using utility assets and interacting with objectives.

**Tradeoff to consider:** This avoids extra catch-up rules, but can fail if a player's remaining options are mathematically insufficient or key routes are blocked.

**Design prompt:** Evaluate how combat losses affect access to objectives and whether a trailing player can still take consequential actions before adding a separate bonus.

[Supporting discussion, 3:00–7:00](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=CuEZVnOLuls&t=180s)

### Separate continued play from guaranteed comeback

A game can preserve dramatic participation without guaranteeing that a losing player will win. The hosts value late deployments and last-stand actions that can occasionally reverse a close game or create a memorable but futile finale.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Spectacle sustains attention but can look like throwing the game if players can make unrestricted late choices.

**Design prompt:** Specify whether late resources are intended to rebalance, offer a last chance, or create a narrative send-off; test how opponents perceive each use.

[Supporting discussion, 7:00–11:00](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=CuEZVnOLuls&t=420s)

### Match catch-up to game structure

Movement scale and formation rules can provide natural or absent comeback opportunities. Slow rank-and-flank units can be pulled out of position after a successful charge; mobile skirmish units may instead threaten multiple parts of a small table quickly.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Natural positional limits reduce special rules, but may be missing in free-flowing games.

**Design prompt:** Evaluate how many turns it takes for a unit to affect distant objectives and whether successful attacks leave the victor exposed or free to snowball.

[Supporting discussion, 29:00–31:39](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=CuEZVnOLuls&t=1740s)

### Design for engagement as well as score

The most useful catch-up outcome may be a player staying active, making choices, and enjoying the ending rather than changing the winner. The hosts propose extra resources and headwinds as ways to keep a trailing player involved and let them describe a close loss.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Engagement may not satisfy players who want strict competitive fairness; strong balancing can reduce the consequences of earlier play.

**Design prompt:** Choose the goal—retention, agency, close scoring, or actual comeback—and design the mechanic around that goal.

[Supporting discussion, 31:37–31:39](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=CuEZVnOLuls&t=1897s)

**Differences in approach or unresolved questions**

- One host is more comfortable with late, spectacular deployments and social judgment; the other is concerned with fairness and designed catch-up. They agree that an early version that made the leader lose was a flaw.

**Episode-specific limits**

- This is the first half of a two-part discussion; later material on specific problems in Gaslands and A Billion Suns is reviewed in the companion episode.
- Some examples and exact game terms are garbled by ASR; the findings use clear contextual passages.

## Dice spend mechanics in miniatures wargames.

**Source:** [Dice spend mechanics in miniatures wargames.](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=H3piagVcDyU) · **Video ID:** `H3piagVcDyU`

The hosts examine dice assignment as input randomness, its action economy, mitigation, communication, and how to prevent poor rolls from blanking a turn.

### Dice assignment changes randomness timing

Output randomness resolves an action after choosing it, while dice spending makes a random roll shape the actions available beforehand. Players roll a pool and assign results to actions or powers, as in Saga battle boards or action dice systems.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Input randomness creates planning puzzles but can withhold actions that players would otherwise always be able to attempt.

**Design prompt:** Decide whether the random pool should determine action availability or only success; account for that agency difference explicitly.

[Supporting discussion, 0:00–4:00](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=H3piagVcDyU&t=0s)

### Protect basic actions from unusable pools

A poor roll should still allow baseline participation when core actions such as moving or fighting consume dice. Relic Blade allows any die for basic utility actions, while higher results unlock more powerful or specialized choices.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Flexible low-value uses prevent a dead turn but can reduce the tension of having to adapt to the pool.

**Design prompt:** Separate baseline actions from premium actions so every result has some useful assignment.

[Supporting discussion, 3:00–6:00](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=H3piagVcDyU&t=180s)

### Give weak dice broad utility and strong dice specific power

A pool becomes more interesting when low results remain useful in flexible ways and high results unlock potent, narrower effects. The hosts describe low dice enabling movement or blocking while high dice are reserved for powerful attacks or abilities.

**Tradeoff to consider:** This creates a choice among general utility and power spikes, but requires careful calibration so weak results are not still dead weight.

**Design prompt:** Map each result's flexibility and strength; test whether a pool with mostly low values still offers meaningful tactical options.

[Supporting discussion, 5:00–8:00](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=H3piagVcDyU&t=300s)

### Build mitigation into the system

Dice spending needs ways to reshape bad pools because even usable results may not match a player's intended plan. Examples include sacrificing a die to reroll the remaining pool, combining results, changing faces, or converting one result into another.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Mitigation preserves agency but can add a second layer of rules and dilute randomness if too generous.

**Design prompt:** Include clear mitigation options and test how often they rescue plans without making the initial roll irrelevant.

[Supporting discussion, 7:00–10:00](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=H3piagVcDyU&t=420s)

### Make the pool express the turn's strategic texture

A variable pool is most satisfying when it changes the style of the turn rather than simply deciding whether the player gets to act. The hosts recommend pools that push players toward an aggressive, defensive, or movement-focused plan depending on results.

**Tradeoff to consider:** A changed plan creates adaptation, but can frustrate players if the intended action is central and unavailable.

**Design prompt:** Define several viable plans that different pools can support; avoid pools that routinely turn a player's whole turn into inactivity.

[Supporting discussion, 35:00–38:00](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=H3piagVcDyU&t=2100s)

### Use symbols to soften numeric hierarchy

Custom icons can make results feel like categories of opportunity rather than an obvious ranking from worst number to best. Saga's custom dice use icons, which the hosts say flatten the emotional perception of rolling low numbers even when their probabilities differ.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Icons reduce immediate numerical comparison but require custom components and learning their meanings.

**Design prompt:** Consider symbolic results when the design wants players to see qualitative action types instead of simply valuing the largest number.

[Supporting discussion, 8:00–11:00](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=H3piagVcDyU&t=480s)

### Make assignments visible

Players need physical ways to see which dice remain available and which have been committed to actions. Battle boards, summary cards, and moving dice between a planning area and a committed area externalize choices that would be hard to hold in memory.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Visual aids improve planning but add table footprint and component needs.

**Design prompt:** Prototype how dice are laid out and reassigned, and provide concise reference aids for action thresholds and combinations.

[Supporting discussion, 34:00–38:00](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=H3piagVcDyU&t=2040s)

### Let tactical state shape the next pool

A feedback loop between model positions and dice results can make the miniatures game inform the resource-allocation puzzle. The hosts suggest that units engaged in melee or positioned in a role could affect which dice or powers become available next turn.

**Tradeoff to consider:** This ties spatial play to planning, but can compound an early advantage if successful positioning improves future pools.

**Design prompt:** When linking board state to dice generation, test both thematic payoff and whether the loop snowballs or creates a useful positional incentive.

[Supporting discussion, 31:00–34:00](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=H3piagVcDyU&t=1860s)

**Differences in approach or unresolved questions**

- The hosts note that dice assignment can create an enjoyable adaptation puzzle, but differ in how much randomness is acceptable before it denies the player's intended turn; they agree mitigation is necessary.

**Episode-specific limits**

- Specific system names are sometimes garbled by ASR; Saga, Relic Blade, Silver Tower, and War of the Ring are identifiable from context.
- The transcript includes an unfinished design example and does not establish balanced numeric thresholds.

## Borrowing Mechanics: Push Your Luck.

**Source:** [Borrowing Mechanics: Push Your Luck.](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=E0DIj1aOY4I) · **Video ID:** `E0DIj1aOY4I`

The hosts define push-your-luck systems and examine their use in miniatures games, especially activation, shared resources, snowball risks, and table theater.

### Distinguish push-your-luck from ordinary uncertain resolution

Push-your-luck gives players a choice to repeat a risky action for greater gain while increasing the chance of losing accumulated value. The hosts contrast blackjack-like risk escalation with ordinary dice rolls that simply determine whether an action succeeds.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Voluntary risk creates tension and agency, but only when the player controls whether to continue.

**Design prompt:** Make the decision to stop or press explicit and ensure each additional attempt meaningfully changes both reward and bust risk.

[Supporting discussion, 1:00–4:00](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=E0DIj1aOY4I&t=60s)

### Watch for leader snowball

A trailing player may feel forced to take greater risks than the leader, making failure compound the existing gap. The hosts compare a conservative leader to a trailing player who must keep pushing and therefore busts more often.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Risk-taking makes recovery possible, but can create a self-reinforcing loss cycle.

**Design prompt:** Test the same risk choice from ahead and behind; consider equalizing stakes or making failure costs independent of current score.

[Supporting discussion, 6:00–9:00](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=E0DIj1aOY4I&t=360s)

### Keep a safe baseline action

A push-your-luck system should not make a player feel they must gamble just to participate. The hosts describe activation systems where the safest option still provides useful activations, leaving extra pushes as optional risk.

**Tradeoff to consider:** A robust safe path protects agency, but may reduce the tension if it is always the best choice.

**Design prompt:** Ensure the low-risk choice still produces a meaningful turn, then make extra attempts attractive but not mandatory.

[Supporting discussion, 8:00–12:00](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=E0DIj1aOY4I&t=480s)

### Put risk around a central shared resource

A push-your-luck choice feels more consequential when it changes a resource that both sides value. The hosts suggest a shared pool that affects activations, deployment, reinforcements, or command points so that risking more has a visible game-wide consequence.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Shared stakes sharpen tension but can create kingmaking or tempt players to end the game early to harm an opponent.

**Design prompt:** Use a common resource only when both players understand its value and the consequences of adding to or exhausting it.

[Supporting discussion, 23:00–26:00](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=E0DIj1aOY4I&t=1380s)

### Bound how far players can push

A clear upper limit can keep risk decisions tense without allowing a single turn to consume the whole game. The hosts praise systems where the safe option is substantial and the number of additional activations or pushes remains limited.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Caps prevent runaway turns, but may make the risk feel decorative if the ceiling is too low.

**Design prompt:** Set a maximum risk range and test whether players face a real choice at multiple points rather than routinely selecting the same number.

[Supporting discussion, 18:00–24:00](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=E0DIj1aOY4I&t=1080s)

### Make busting change the table state

A failed push can be more engaging when it creates a visible consequence instead of merely cancelling the accumulated action. The hosts recommend turning bust outcomes into positional or other knock-on effects that produce theater among the miniatures.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Active consequences keep the scene moving, but can create extra randomness that affects uninvolved players.

**Design prompt:** Design bust outcomes that fit the miniatures' physical space and create further decisions; avoid a dead result that simply wastes setup and time.

[Supporting discussion, 30:00–33:00](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=E0DIj1aOY4I&t=1800s)

### Solo play tolerates harsher risk more easily

A solo opponent avoids some fairness and collusion problems because risk does not push another human player over the same cliff. The hosts describe noise or repeated actions increasing a monster's chance to detect or attack the solo character.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Solo systems can use sharper risk escalation, though they still need meaningful player choices and understandable consequences.

**Design prompt:** When adapting push-your-luck to competitive play, revisit any penalty that was harmless against an automated opponent.

[Supporting discussion, 26:00–30:00](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=E0DIj1aOY4I&t=1560s)

### Engineer suspense as performance

A visible, escalating risk can make the table watch a roll together and create a memorable dramatic moment. The hosts describe Gaslands dice rolls where everyone anticipates either an impressive maneuver or a spectacular wipeout.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Theater can enliven play, but repeated low-stakes rolls become boring if little depends on them.

**Design prompt:** Reserve escalation for moments with clear stakes and consequences; use the physical board to make success and failure visually legible.

[Supporting discussion, 31:00–33:00](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=E0DIj1aOY4I&t=1860s)

**Differences in approach or unresolved questions**

- The hosts agree that push-your-luck can create suspense but differ in appetite for how central it should be; one emphasizes careful limits and competitive fairness, while the other highlights spectacle and positional consequences.

**Episode-specific limits**

- The hosts use a strict design meaning of push-your-luck and acknowledge that everyday speech may use the term more broadly.
- Some cited game names are garbled by ASR; examples are summarized by mechanic where identification is uncertain.

## Borrowing mechanics: Dexterity.

**Source:** [Borrowing mechanics: Dexterity.](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=R5fY2BwgubE) · **Video ID:** `R5fY2BwgubE`

The hosts discuss translating balancing, stacking, throwing, and flicking dexterity mechanics into miniatures games, including component variation, play surfaces, and model care.

### Make physical actions express the fiction

Dexterity procedures can make a game's central physical premise present in the player's hands, not only in its written rules. Space Gits uses dice stacks to represent drunken orcs and dice placement to represent movement, attacks, and displacement.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Physical play increases tactile fun but transfers some control from the player to the geometry and objects on the table.

**Design prompt:** Choose physical actions that embody the setting and test whether the resulting loss of precision feels expressive rather than arbitrary.

[Supporting discussion, 0:00–4:00](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=R5fY2BwgubE&t=0s)

### Use rising physical tension as a persistent loop

A stack can create an escalating tension arc that keeps a player engaged even when the larger score or battle is going poorly. Dice added for actions and damage make movement increasingly precarious; a fallen orc needs help and can be looted by the opponent.

**Tradeoff to consider:** The tower creates ongoing suspense and a nonlethal consequence, but can make accumulated damage harder to manage physically.

**Design prompt:** Tie stacking height to a meaningful game state and make failure produce a visible next decision rather than simply remove the model.

[Supporting discussion, 1:00–4:00](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=R5fY2BwgubE&t=60s)

### Treat shared components as a design constraint

A dexterity game can behave differently depending on the size, weight, grip, and manufacture of player-supplied components. The hosts compare small dice, casino dice, grippy rubber dice, and intentional variation in Jenga blocks; each changes balance and sliding difficulty.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Allowing ordinary dice lowers cost and improves access, while standardized components make skill and difficulty more consistent.

**Design prompt:** Decide whether component variation is part of the fun or an uncontrolled balance problem; specify size or surface requirements if consistency matters.

[Supporting discussion, 12:00–15:00](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=R5fY2BwgubE&t=720s)

### Specify the surface when friction drives play

In a dexterity game, table texture can function like terrain and alter the difficulty of movement. The hosts contrast smooth neoprene with rough sand-and-PVA boards, noting that stacked dice may slide easily on one and barely move on the other.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Surface variation can create emergent terrain but makes results less reproducible and may invalidate intended movement.

**Design prompt:** Test across common table surfaces and state acceptable materials or acknowledge that surface texture is a deliberate variable.

[Supporting discussion, 14:00–17:00](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=R5fY2BwgubE&t=840s)

### Let random movement create characterful control loss

A movement action can be random in both distance and direction to make impaired control visible. Space Gits tosses three dice; each die's face determines distance and its landing position gives the direction, sometimes sending an orc staggering away from the intended route.

**Tradeoff to consider:** This makes drunkenness legible and playful, but reduces tactical precision and must not erase all meaningful planning.

**Design prompt:** Use a physical randomizer when uncertainty itself expresses the fiction; keep enough choice in order or destination selection for players to adapt.

[Supporting discussion, 3:00–6:00](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=R5fY2BwgubE&t=180s)

### Match physical risk to miniature value

Dexterity play near finely painted miniatures creates a tension between tactile interaction and protecting valuable objects. The hosts note that thrown dice, sliding stacks, and falling towers can damage carefully painted models, even though miniatures are meant to be handled.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Protective limits reduce physical chaos but can blunt the mechanic; inexpensive or robust models permit more aggressive play.

**Design prompt:** Consider model durability, table spacing, and component weight as part of the rules environment; make the degree of rough handling clear to players.

[Supporting discussion, 17:00–23:00](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=R5fY2BwgubE&t=1020s)

### Build several scales of tension

A persistent dexterity challenge can provide small moments of suspense inside a larger game arc. The hosts compare a stacking tower's steadily rising tension to nested gameplay loops, letting a player remain absorbed in a local challenge even when the race or battle is slipping away.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Secondary loops retain engagement but can distract from the main objective or create excessive downtime.

**Design prompt:** Use a compact physical loop that resolves quickly and continues to matter, so it adds tension without replacing the core game.

[Supporting discussion, 26:00–30:00](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=R5fY2BwgubE&t=1560s)

### Use dexterity to create visible action

Physical dice and tokens can make actions visible in a way that abstract rolls or measurements do not. A die placed on the table marks a shot's direction and endpoint, allowing players to trace its path and see the result among the models.

**Tradeoff to consider:** The spatial performance adds spectacle, but requires careful handling and can expose models to accidental impact.

**Design prompt:** Choose physical components whose locations communicate actions and consequences; make the table state part of the feedback players see.

[Supporting discussion, 22:00–25:00](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=R5fY2BwgubE&t=1320s)

### Decide whether dexterity should reward learned skill

Physical uncertainty can be mostly random or allow players to improve through practice; the distinction shapes the game’s feel. The hosts contrast chaotic dice placement with dexterity games where players gradually get their eye in.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Skill growth rewards repetition, while random outcomes preserve unpredictability and reduce the advantage of practiced technique.

**Design prompt:** State which outcomes players can influence, then test whether the actual table surface and dice make that influence perceptible.

[Supporting discussion, 4:00–8:00](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=R5fY2BwgubE&t=240s)

### Prototype component tolerances early

A physical mechanic can behave differently with components that appear interchangeable. Dice size, weight, grip, and intentional manufacturing variation change stacking and sliding difficulty.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Generic components lower cost and ease access, but increase variance in the experience; tightly specified parts improve consistency.

**Design prompt:** Test common component sizes and surfaces, then either specify acceptable materials or embrace variation as part of the design.

[Supporting discussion, 10:00–14:00](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=R5fY2BwgubE&t=600s)

### Make tactical symbols match their physical language

A dexterity system should use gestures and symbols that communicate the intended tactical choices. The prototype replaced swipe, shove, and stab when their cancel relationships created the wrong combat dynamic.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Familiar gestures are easy to understand, but changing them to fit the desired interactions can require teaching new meanings.

**Design prompt:** Test the physical action, icon, and resolution relationship together rather than treating the symbol as decoration.

[Supporting discussion, 16:00–20:00](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=R5fY2BwgubE&t=960s)

### Turn rolling into a visible event on the battlefield

A physical randomizer can also communicate where an action happens. In the prototype, dice placement determines bullet direction and makes the path visible among models.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Tangible resolution adds spectacle and spatial information, but it can risk collisions with delicate miniatures.

**Design prompt:** Use physical random placement when it adds useful shared information, and protect models through component scale and clear handling expectations.

[Supporting discussion, 22:00–26:00](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=R5fY2BwgubE&t=1320s)

**Differences in approach or unresolved questions**

- The hosts have different preferences for dexterity formats: one enjoys balancing but dislikes flicking, while the other sees several physical formats as promising. Both recognize that the fit depends on game tone and component handling.

**Episode-specific limits**

- Space Gits is described as an alpha at the time of the discussion, so its mechanics and handling requirements were still provisional.
- ASR mangles some game names, including the cited dexterity example; uncertain names are omitted.

## Borrowing mechanics: Deckbuilding.

**Source:** [Borrowing mechanics: Deckbuilding.](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=DEXPYDM-brM) · **Video ID:** `DEXPYDM-brM`

The discussion explores how deck construction and in-game deckbuilding can transfer to miniature games, including narrative effects, campaign growth, and physical-card constraints.

### Distinguish deck construction from deckbuilding

Pre-game deck construction gives players control before play; deckbuilding changes a deck during play. The hosts use Magic as a construction example and Dominion as a builder, then relate both to cards already used for resolution.

**Tradeoff to consider:** The distinction clarifies when choices happen, while importing either system risks making the card layer dominate miniature play.

**Design prompt:** Name the timing and purpose of deck changes before borrowing the mechanic; test whether players can understand it from familiar card actions.

[Supporting discussion, 2:00–6:00](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=DEXPYDM-brM&t=120s)

### Keep imported systems close to the table

A borrowed mechanic feels integrated when its choices remain connected to models and battlefield events. Cards held in front of players and familiar actions such as discarding or replacing a card are presented as immediate and legible.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Rich card decisions can add depth, but detached sideboards and token administration pull attention away from miniatures.

**Design prompt:** Tie a card change to an observable event or model state, and avoid a remote subsystem unless its payoff justifies the attention cost.

[Supporting discussion, 6:00–10:00](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=DEXPYDM-brM&t=360s)

### Use deck shaping for progression without simple power escalation

Deck changes can represent a force changing its specialties rather than merely becoming stronger. The hosts propose replacing cards associated with one capability by cards for another as the battle develops.

**Tradeoff to consider:** This creates an arc and tactical adaptation, but risks confusion if card capabilities do not map cleanly to units and actions.

**Design prompt:** Use replacement or reshaping when the intended story is a shift in emphasis; spell out which battlefield event permits it.

[Supporting discussion, 10:00–14:00](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=DEXPYDM-brM&t=600s)

### Represent attrition through changing activation decks

A declining deck can show reduced operational capacity without removing miniatures. Removing activation cards as units are hit makes surviving forces slower or less reliable while models remain on the table.

**Tradeoff to consider:** It preserves physical presence and narrative continuity, though a shrinking deck may also reduce player options or create uneven activation frequency.

**Design prompt:** If using card attrition, connect each removed card to a clear effect and test whether the remaining units still have meaningful choices.

[Supporting discussion, 12:00–16:00](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=DEXPYDM-brM&t=720s)

### Choose a coherent scale for deck effects

Effects should operate at a scale that makes narrative sense for the deck they alter. The hosts contrast a card representing one adventurer with a force-wide deck, where a wound to one alien may not coherently affect every model.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Force-wide morale or environmental effects can fit a shared deck; individual wounds need a more localized representation.

**Design prompt:** State whether a deck belongs to a character, unit, or force, then restrict inserted conditions to effects that plausibly apply at that scale.

[Supporting discussion, 14:00–18:00](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=DEXPYDM-brM&t=840s)

### Use cards to express faction identity economically

Shared card procedures can make factions feel distinct without giving every unit a separate rules package. The examples include factions adding or removing cards, or introducing cards into an opponent’s deck to represent environmental or morale pressure.

**Tradeoff to consider:** A common framework makes asymmetry easier to learn, but broad deck effects can feel arbitrary unless their fiction and targets are clear.

**Design prompt:** Give factions a small set of consistent deck interactions and explain what battlefield condition each represents.

[Supporting discussion, 16:00–20:00](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=DEXPYDM-brM&t=960s)

### Constrain explosive card chains in miniature games

A dramatic deck “kick” can create excitement, but its swing must not bury the miniature game. The hosts compare chains of card draws and extra actions in deckbuilders with the risk of an entire army gaining a huge burst at once.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Big turns create memorable peaks, while excessive cascades make positioning and model relationships secondary.

**Design prompt:** Limit frequency, scope, or payoff of chain effects; ensure a successful combo still asks players to make battlefield decisions.

[Supporting discussion, 18:00–23:00](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=DEXPYDM-brM&t=1080s)

### Let card geometry interact with the battlefield

Physical cards can function as templates and spatial tools, not only as abstract modifiers. The hosts suggest transparent cards and mention card-based mines, covering fire, and grenades, with placement tied to models.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Physical templates link card play to position, but custom components raise production costs and require careful table handling.

**Design prompt:** Use card geometry when placement is central to the mechanic; prefer an ordinary deck when lowering entry barriers matters more.

[Supporting discussion, 21:00–26:00](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=DEXPYDM-brM&t=1260s)

### Carry deck changes across campaign games

Persistent deck evolution can make a campaign’s shared history tangible. Players can add, remove, or swap cards between games so the deck records prior events and changes as a warband develops.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Persistence rewards continuity, but permanent changes can complicate balance and make the game harder to restart or share.

**Design prompt:** Define whether campaign card changes are reversible, who owns the evolving deck, and how new or returning players can join.

[Supporting discussion, 24:00–30:30](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=DEXPYDM-brM&t=1440s)

### Prototype with standard components before custom production

A normal deck of cards offers a low-cost test bed for many deck mechanics. The hosts note that suits, colors, face cards, and card orientation can create varied functions without a bespoke deck.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Standard components lower barriers and improve accessibility, while limiting the range of tailored effects and presentation.

**Design prompt:** Prototype the core loop with a standard deck; commission custom cards only when their physical features materially improve play.

[Supporting discussion, 23:00–30:00](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=DEXPYDM-brM&t=1380s)

**Episode-specific limits**

- The discussion gives examples and design preferences, not measured comparative playtest results. The hosts distinguish their own co-op/solo design interests from universal recommendations.

## Quarterbacking, Kingmaking and other political problems.

**Source:** [Quarterbacking, Kingmaking and other political problems.](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=G-TdPPaswXo) · **Video ID:** `G-TdPPaswXo`

Political influence and fairness problems in high-interaction multiplayer games.

### Identify the tested skill

In multiplayer games, persuasion can overwhelm the tactical skills the game was intended to test unless politics is itself part of the design goal. A player who persuades others to direct their actions can effectively control multiple players' resources.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Table talk creates social interaction, but can displace strategic play and reward social influence over rules mastery.

**Design prompt:** State whether negotiation is intended gameplay; if not, build procedures that protect independent decisions.

[Supporting discussion, 3:16–4:41](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=G-TdPPaswXo&t=196s)

### Use objectives to explain targeting

Visible objectives can provide an in-game reason to attack the current leader and reduce the need for arbitrary social explanations. A player on a scoring point becomes the obvious target; when they leave it, the reason to attack them can shift to someone else.

**Tradeoff to consider:** This channels conflict but can create predictable dogpiling around the objective.

**Design prompt:** Use clear, contestable objectives to direct attention in free-for-all games and test whether players can recover after becoming the target.

[Supporting discussion, 12:05–13:15](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=G-TdPPaswXo&t=725s)

### Make standings legible

A clear, visible account of who is winning can give players a common reason for choosing targets and limit ambiguity-driven politics. Race metaphors or clearly marked scores help identify the leader and justify directing pressure toward them.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Transparency may reduce hidden strategic information that some two-player games rely on.

**Design prompt:** In multiplayer games, expose enough score and threat information to support defensible targeting decisions.

[Supporting discussion, 15:43–17:00](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=G-TdPPaswXo&t=943s)

### Hide plans to reduce quarterbacking

Simultaneous or concealed planning can prevent players from directing one another's turns and can create bluffing opportunities. Players commit orders before seeing others' choices; table talk then cannot guarantee that the proposed action is what the speaker will actually do.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Hidden plans reduce negotiation control but make coordination and explanation harder.

**Design prompt:** Test a simultaneous commitment phase when open sequential turns make it easy for one player to quarterback others.

[Supporting discussion, 39:39–40:48](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=G-TdPPaswXo&t=2379s)

### Use uncertainty to curb quarterbacking

Hidden tactical effects can make confident advice risky and discourage one player from claiming certainty over another player's decision. The example uses hidden cards that can change a combat after an ally has been told it is safe.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Hidden information checks overconfidence but may create surprise swings or rules overhead.

**Design prompt:** Use concealed resources only when they create meaningful uncertainty and do not make outcomes feel arbitrary.

[Supporting discussion, 23:48–25:08](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=G-TdPPaswXo&t=1428s)

### Kingmaking creates conflicting expectations

A player who can no longer win may decide another player’s victory, creating uncertainty over whether to seek revenge, improve placement, withdraw, or support someone else. The functionally eliminated player still has resources capable of changing the result.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Clear standings make targeting easier to justify, but also make kingmaking easier; hiding standings can reduce kingmaking while undermining targeting clarity.

**Design prompt:** State what continued play is intended to reward, and test the conflict between legible standings and kingmaking.

[Supporting discussion, 28:07–29:28](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=G-TdPPaswXo&t=1687s)

**Episode-specific limits**

- Examples and proposed solutions are discussed as options; no general tested fix is offered for every multiplayer format.
- Social preferences vary, and some games intentionally make negotiation the primary skill.

## UKGE walk around.

**Source:** [UKGE walk around.](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=_sNvD5WpKPQ) · **Video ID:** `_sNvD5WpKPQ`

Convention walk-through naming stands, games, and terrain vendors.

**Episode-specific limits**

- Entire transcript read during this audit: a convention walk-through identifying stands and briefly praising terrain convenience, without substantial game-design discussion.
- Brief personal product impressions are not comparative evidence.

## Team Break down! Falling out happens.

**Source:** [Team Break down! Falling out happens.](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=6gJmR67sJYA) · **Video ID:** `6gJmR67sJYA`

Team objectives, divergent player motivations, and ways to coordinate units in multiplayer games.

### Teams can want different experiences

Teammates may diverge when one values narrative spectacle and another wants to optimize the win, even if they began with shared expectations. A scenario may reward one player's preferred action while another's signature moment depends on an uncertain roll or inefficient move.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Multiple modes of fun broaden appeal but can split a team's goals and create resentment.

**Design prompt:** Make scenarios reward the intended play style and avoid forcing a teammate to sabotage their own desired experience for another's payoff.

[Supporting discussion, 6:15–7:37](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=6gJmR67sJYA&t=375s)

### Require shared contribution

Team objectives can make players combine forces instead of running adjacent individual games. The Hobgoblin example requires both teammates' units to contribute near objectives or to a kill, encouraging shared deployment and coordination.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Joint requirements promote teamwork but may feel restrictive when one teammate is out of position or unable to act.

**Design prompt:** Use objectives that need contributions from multiple teammates when coordinated play is central, and ensure each player can participate.

[Supporting discussion, 15:54–17:12](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=6gJmR67sJYA&t=954s)

### Spend support resources on allies

A resource that can only help teammates creates a reason to monitor and support their actions. Hidden fortune cards or buffs are applied to teammates' units rather than the owner's, making attention and communication necessary.

**Tradeoff to consider:** This increases cooperation but can enable quarterbacking or leave a player with no useful target.

**Design prompt:** Test ally-directed resources for both coordination and player agency, including turns when a teammate has no eligible unit.

[Supporting discussion, 16:57–18:05](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=6gJmR67sJYA&t=1017s)

### Deliver the promised fun

A team design should fulfill the experience it promises; a signature moment that repeatedly fails can cause players to abandon the shared goal. The discussion proposes making a special ability happen through a limited resource rather than an unreliable test when its spectacle is central.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Guaranteed moments improve reliability but may reduce uncertainty or tactical risk.

**Design prompt:** If the game's promise depends on a dramatic action, make access to that action reliable enough that players can experience it.

[Supporting discussion, 20:25–21:36](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=6gJmR67sJYA&t=1225s)

### Give cooperative groups interactions
