Team play can include concrete exchanges such as helping, repairing, passing objectives, or recovering a fallen ally's resource. Interactive tokens or resources leave a trace after a piece is removed, giving teammates something to retrieve or use.

**Tradeoff to consider:** These interactions deepen teamwork but add procedures and can create recovery tasks that slow the match.

**Design prompt:** Add a small number of clear ally-to-ally interactions that reinforce the team's shared objective.

[Supporting discussion, 32:23–33:39](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=6gJmR67sJYA&t=1943s)

### Keep team roles distinct but aligned

Different roles can create tension and individuality within a team, but victory conditions must still point players toward a compatible shared outcome. The episode discusses nuanced team ranking and competing player motivations, alongside clear shared goals.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Individual distinctions add texture but can undermine cooperation if a teammate's best outcome conflicts with the team's.

**Design prompt:** Before adding semi-cooperative scoring, state how personal and team rewards interact and test late-game choices for conflict.

[Supporting discussion, 9:22–10:47](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=6gJmR67sJYA&t=562s)

**Episode-specific limits**

- The speakers disagree in emphasis about formal modeling of team play versus keeping hobby and rules more separate; their approaches are both presented.
- Specific mechanisms described are examples from individual games, not proven fixes across genres.

## How to avoid a piggy in middle in your games.

**Source:** [How to avoid a piggy in middle in your games.](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=VyfFkJ7pko8) · **Video ID:** `VyfFkJ7pko8`

Structural causes and possible remedies for multiplayer dogpiling and kingmaking.

### Multiplayer breaks zero-sum attrition

A third player changes the incentives of two-player attrition: attacking one rival may weaken the attacker while leaving an untouched opponent stronger. Players can exploit the two weakened forces after they spend actions against each other.

**Tradeoff to consider:** The format can create shifting alliances, but the tactical balance of a duel does not carry over automatically.

**Design prompt:** Rebuild victory conditions, turn order, and deployment for multiplayer rather than simply adding a third force to a duel map.

[Supporting discussion, 1:50–2:54](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=VyfFkJ7pko8&t=110s)

### Constrain target selection carefully

Limiting who can be attacked can interrupt a stable two-against-one pattern, but a forced target must remain reachable and useful. Triumph and Treachery's chosen-enemy rule restricts targets by phase; variants include target limits or random exclusions.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Restrictions disrupt dogpiles but can waste a turn if no legal target is available.

**Design prompt:** When constraining target choice, guarantee each player has a viable action and state how neutral players are treated.

[Supporting discussion, 4:24–5:38](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=VyfFkJ7pko8&t=264s)

### Reward the player under pressure

A scoring model can make being attacked worthwhile if the threatened player also earns points for fighting back. The cited close-combat game awards points for kills on both sides, so the central player can gain from a dogpile even if later eliminated.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Compensation can make pressure tolerable but may leave the target sitting out the end of the match.

**Design prompt:** Check whether a pressured player gains meaningful progress or agency, and separately address downtime after elimination.

[Supporting discussion, 6:06–7:21](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=VyfFkJ7pko8&t=366s)

### Distribute contests across the table

Multiple objectives, separated zones, or costly movement between areas can prevent a game from collapsing into a single central dogpile. Players must split resources and choose which objectives or levels to contest; relocation has a visible resource cost.

**Tradeoff to consider:** This creates parallel decisions but risks fragmentation or making some areas irrelevant.

**Design prompt:** Test whether the map provides multiple worthwhile contests and whether moving between them is predictable and costly enough to matter.

[Supporting discussion, 26:57–28:12](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=VyfFkJ7pko8&t=1617s)

### Signal special moves in advance

A costly special move is fairer and more satisfying when opponents can see it coming and plan around it. An available resource or ability is visible before use, giving rivals time to change position or deny its value.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Telegraphing reduces surprise but makes counterplay possible and turns success into a respected outplay.

**Design prompt:** Make powerful relocations or attacks legible through visible resources, cooldowns, or clear declarations.

[Supporting discussion, 28:25–29:26](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=VyfFkJ7pko8&t=1705s)

**Episode-specific limits**

- Several remedies are discussed as ideas rather than established best practices; the authors note that some prior rules did not fully work.
- The examples are specific to tabletop multiplayer and may not apply to low-interaction games.

## Is modelling for advantage a game breaker?

**Source:** [Is modelling for advantage a game breaker?](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=o-BYSCBxu9Q) · **Video ID:** `o-BYSCBxu9Q`

The tension between visual modeling freedom and miniature geometry that affects gameplay.

### Choose when the model matters

If line of sight, cover, or measurement depends on a figure's sculpt, hobby choices can create unintended advantages or disadvantages. Large banners or unusual poses may be easier to see or harder to protect under direct-geometry rules.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Letting miniatures affect play gives them physical meaning but can punish creative modeling or proxy choices.

**Design prompt:** Audit every rule that uses model shape and decide whether the sculpt or the base should determine the outcome.

[Supporting discussion, 3:04–4:21](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=o-BYSCBxu9Q&t=184s)

### Make hobby expectations explicit

Unspoken assumptions about acceptable modeling can create disputes when players interpret visual freedom differently. Describe intended hobby practice explicitly and illustrate what a suitable playing table looks like, instead of relying on shared assumptions.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Guidance reduces ambiguity but can feel restrictive if it dictates too much about player creativity.

**Design prompt:** State which physical features count for rules and provide examples of reasonable modeling choices.

[Supporting discussion, 24:00–25:13](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=o-BYSCBxu9Q&t=1440s)

### Modeling can be part of mechanics

A game may deliberately use the shape, pose, or attachments of a model as a gameplay element and invite players to build with that in mind. The discussion cites games where models or added components determine capacity, templates, or spatial effects.

**Tradeoff to consider:** This can make hobby work integral and inspiring, but requires careful rules to distinguish intended expression from unfair optimization.

**Design prompt:** If modeling affects play, document the intended build constraints and make the design goal clear before players invest in models.

[Supporting discussion, 13:58–15:11](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=o-BYSCBxu9Q&t=838s)

### Separate concerns as a valid choice

Designers can also keep modeling and game mechanics loosely connected, using images and examples to celebrate hobby work without letting sculpt dimensions decide outcomes. The speakers contrast a hard rules link with a softer connection through photography and thematic presentation.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Separation protects visual freedom but may make miniatures feel like decoration on standardized bases.

**Design prompt:** Choose a deliberate balance between model-as-tool and model-as-art rather than relying on unspecified convention.

[Supporting discussion, 26:31–27:46](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=o-BYSCBxu9Q&t=1591s)

### Use tabletop examples

Photographs of correctly assembled and played tables help communicate how the game expects miniatures and terrain to be used. A hobby section can pair practical modeling guidance with images of play and readable example layouts.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Visual examples clarify expectations but cannot resolve every unusual model or terrain dispute.

**Design prompt:** Include annotated examples of measurement, facing, and terrain where physical geometry materially affects rules.

[Supporting discussion, 24:28–25:41](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=o-BYSCBxu9Q&t=1468s)

**Differences in approach or unresolved questions**

- The speakers differ: one prefers modeling to matter directly to gameplay; the other values separating modeling from game rules.

**Episode-specific limits**

- The episode reports design opinions and anecdotes, not a measured frequency or severity of modeling disputes.
- Rules vary: base measurements, line of sight, and custom modeling should be judged in the context of each game's geometry.

## How to balance the CURSE of making failure impactful while not building win more.

**Source:** [How to balance the CURSE of making failure impactful while not building win more.](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=Ya77wPgJ-1A) · **Video ID:** `Ya77wPgJ-1A`

Failure consequences, snowballing, player agency, and alternative ways to create an arc.

### Failure without snowballing

A failure consequence can matter without directly increasing the opponent's power or removing the failing player's remaining choices. Knockdown costs activations and position, but the model can recover and continue pursuing the objective.

**Tradeoff to consider:** This preserves agency but still disrupts tempo and can create a temporary setback.

**Design prompt:** Test setbacks that change position, tempo, or resource use before defaulting to elimination or a direct opponent bonus.

[Supporting discussion, 13:54–15:17](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=Ya77wPgJ-1A&t=834s)

### Early failures can flatten the game

A decisive early advantage can make both players' remaining turns automatic, leaving the disadvantaged player with an insoluble problem and the leader with no challenge. Alpha strikes reduce the opponent's options, which in turn makes the leader's next decisions easier and compounds the imbalance.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Severe punishment makes mistakes consequential but can end meaningful play well before the formal game ends.

**Design prompt:** Look for early states that sharply reduce available responses and test whether both players still face meaningful decisions afterward.

[Supporting discussion, 6:24–7:33](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=Ya77wPgJ-1A&t=384s)

### Objectives can reduce setup punishment

A later-changing objective can keep a poor deployment from deciding the game before the main maneuvering begins. A secondary mission system changes what matters after deployment, creating new positioning choices and room to adapt.

**Tradeoff to consider:** This softens an early mistake but adds uncertainty and may dilute the importance of deployment skill.

**Design prompt:** If deployment is intended as setup rather than the decisive contest, test objectives or events that change which positions matter.

[Supporting discussion, 6:52–8:00](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=Ya77wPgJ-1A&t=412s)

### Removal is not the only meaningful cost

Failure can create an actionable setback while keeping the affected piece and its choices in play. Knocked-down pieces require ally actions to recover or are shifted away from position, producing tempo and placement costs.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Recovery states preserve the force but need clear procedures and may extend the game.

**Design prompt:** Compare removal with temporary incapacitation, displacement, or recovery costs where each piece carries several meaningful options.

[Supporting discussion, 12:27–13:41](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=Ya77wPgJ-1A&t=747s)

### Use waves rather than a one-way collapse

Alternating periods of focus and recovery can make late turns dramatic while letting both sides re-enter the contest. Knockdowns create a slow-motion focus on standing figures, then recovery can restore several actors and shift the game's momentum again.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Waves can sustain engagement, but if setbacks do not change effectiveness the game may circle without progress.

**Design prompt:** When avoiding attrition, ensure temporary states alter capabilities and that the game still has a credible route to an ending.

[Supporting discussion, 18:27–19:48](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=Ya77wPgJ-1A&t=1107s)

### Frame setbacks as grants

The speaker prefers framing a setback as a benefit to the opponent, illustrated by converting a cover penalty into a defense bonus that he describes as statistically identical in his system. Reframe a negative shooting modifier as a positive defense modifier when the underlying mechanic actually preserves the probability.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Positive framing may improve the emotional experience but does not change the underlying balance or outcome.

**Design prompt:** Review player-facing language for whether it describes an opponent's gain or the player's loss, and choose the clearer, less punitive framing when appropriate.

[Supporting discussion, 20:47–22:07](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=Ya77wPgJ-1A&t=1247s)

**Episode-specific limits**

- The speakers' preferences about attrition and failure are design opinions; they acknowledge that mass-battle games may benefit from removal.
- Cited game anecdotes are not controlled evidence that the proposed mechanics improve enjoyment.

## The CURSED problem of choosing between proscription and permission.

**Source:** [The CURSED problem of choosing between proscription and permission.](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=2vQNDHc71xs) · **Video ID:** `2vQNDHc71xs`

How rule wording creates permission, prohibition, and incentives around edge cases and table conduct.

### Prohibition can create permission

A rule that assigns a cost to a behavior may imply that the behavior is allowed and worth considering, even when the designer only meant to handle accidents. The dice-tower example prices intentional interference, which makes it legible as a strategic option rather than an unthinkable breach of table conduct.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Explicit handling rules cover real accidents but can turn them into cost-benefit choices.

**Design prompt:** When prohibiting harmful behavior, avoid framing it as a purchasable action unless players are genuinely meant to choose it.

[Supporting discussion, 6:11–7:32](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=2vQNDHc71xs&t=371s)

### Omit behavior that needs no game meaning

Not every undesirable real-world action needs a game rule; prohibition can bring irrelevant behavior into the game's conceptual space. The speakers compare concise positive instructions with a rulebook that lists forbidden actions, noting that the latter invites edge-case negotiation.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Omission keeps the game focused but may not address a behavior that is common or physically unavoidable.

**Design prompt:** Write rules for recurring, consequential interactions and rely on ordinary table conduct for actions outside the game's intended activity.

[Supporting discussion, 1:22–2:45](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=2vQNDHc71xs&t=82s)

### Handle physical accidents naturally

When a physical accident is inevitable, a simple state-based resolution may work better than a penalty that players can exploit. The episode contrasts penalizing a knocked-over dice tower with making an opponent's tower immune to accidental knocks or treating a topple as an advantage to the affected player.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Natural resolution may feel less realistic, but avoids strategic incentives and keeps play moving.

**Design prompt:** For common table accidents, choose the rule that restores a fair state with minimal calculation and no profitable deliberate misuse.

[Supporting discussion, 8:36–9:43](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=2vQNDHc71xs&t=516s)

### Price rules invite optimization

Turning conduct into a cost-reward proposition means some players will choose it whenever its price is worth paying. A player can weigh a penalty against the advantage from intentionally nudging a model or interfering with a piece.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Costs make behavior measurable but legitimize it as a tactical option.

**Design prompt:** Before adding a penalty, ask whether the rule would make a player consider doing the prohibited act on purpose.

[Supporting discussion, 22:47–24:03](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=2vQNDHc71xs&t=1367s)

### Rules should fit the central meaning

An exception that contradicts a game's core mechanic can feel arbitrary; consider whether a different outcome better fits the fiction and play incentives. If a dice tower falling means an orc is downed, the speakers consider whether an opponent's accidental knock should instead leave that model safe or even help it recover.

**Tradeoff to consider:** An exception improves fairness but can complicate a simple thematic rule.

**Design prompt:** Test rare edge cases against the central fiction and incentives, then choose a resolution players can remember and apply consistently.

[Supporting discussion, 29:25–30:44](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=2vQNDHc71xs&t=1765s)

### Do not outsource assumptions to folk law

Unwritten expectations about nudged models, line of sight, or cocked dice can create disputes when different players bring different conventions. A concise official procedure can make common edge cases fair and predictable without attempting to codify every possible misuse.

**Tradeoff to consider:** More explicit rules add text, while leaving a common issue unstated creates inconsistent table rulings.

**Design prompt:** Identify frequent physical edge cases and publish a clear default, while avoiding exhaustive rules for implausible behavior.

[Supporting discussion, 25:08–26:21](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=2vQNDHc71xs&t=1508s)

**Episode-specific limits**

- Some examples are intentionally provocative thought experiments; do not treat them as claims about typical player conduct.
- A specific solution depends on the game's physical components and whether the mishap is likely, consequential, and repeatable.

## Bringing together experience levels is a CURSED problem!

**Source:** [Bringing together experience levels is a CURSED problem!](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=KDfghDhCAWY) · **Video ID:** `KDfghDhCAWY`

Balancing an inviting first session with replay depth, mastery, and competitive skill gaps.

### First play versus mastery

A design can prioritize a gentle first session or a demanding mastery curve; the right balance depends on the audience and product promise. The episode contrasts lightweight games with complex systems that reveal their interactions only after repeated plays.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Accessibility lowers the barrier, while complexity and hidden depth can reward long-term study.

**Design prompt:** Choose where the game sits on the learning spectrum and communicate that expectation before players invest in it.

[Supporting discussion, 5:57–7:12](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=KDfghDhCAWY&t=357s)

### Hobby investment raises onboarding stakes

A poor first game can feel especially costly when players have already spent money and time assembling or painting a force. The investment creates an expectation that the first session will demonstrate the game's appeal, not only its eventual depth.

**Tradeoff to consider:** A steep learning curve may support mastery but risks alienating players before they experience it.

**Design prompt:** For games with high setup or collection costs, make the first session easy to run and rich enough to justify further investment.

[Supporting discussion, 4:10–5:23](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=KDfghDhCAWY&t=250s)

### Depth is not mandatory for every game

A game with only a few plays of depth may still satisfy its audience if it offers a strong first experience and clear value. The speakers ask whether an indie game needs dozens of plays or can prioritize a delightful short experience.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Limited depth may reduce long-term replay for some players while improving focus and approachability for others.

**Design prompt:** Set a realistic replay target based on price, investment, and intended use; avoid adding complexity solely to imply longevity.

[Supporting discussion, 6:38–7:48](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=KDfghDhCAWY&t=398s)

### Put mastery in observable play

A distinctive physical procedure can give players a satisfying skill to develop across sessions without requiring hidden rules knowledge. The speakers cite movement templates as a concrete form of spatial judgment that improves through play.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Physical mastery is intuitive and visible but may still be difficult for newcomers and can privilege spatial aptitude.

**Design prompt:** When adding depth, consider a repeatable skill players can practice at the table rather than relying only on pre-game study.

[Supporting discussion, 10:57–12:14](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=KDfghDhCAWY&t=657s)

### Make improvement understandable

A useful mastery loop lets players understand why they lost and make a manageable adjustment, rather than feeling they must discard their whole force. Make weaknesses legible so experience leads to a small model or equipment change and a return to play.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Visible improvement can motivate repeat play, but an initial game still needs something exciting enough to invite return.

**Design prompt:** After a first game, check whether a newcomer can identify one useful next change without replacing most of their collection.

[Supporting discussion, 17:34–18:56](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=KDfghDhCAWY&t=1054s)

### Physical randomness can leave room for mastery

Even a chaotic physical procedure may offer learnable skill if players can influence its outcomes through technique and terrain judgment. Space Kits dice rolling is presented as difficult to control, but terrain can trap dice and experience can improve the odds.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Physical dexterity can offer an immediately visible learning curve, but the speakers acknowledge accessibility barriers and differing preferences.

**Design prompt:** Test whether the physical procedure offers a visible, enjoyable improvement curve and whether the intended audience can use it.

[Supporting discussion, 17:00–18:22](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=KDfghDhCAWY&t=1020s)

**Differences in approach or unresolved questions**

- The speakers differ over whether onboarding versus mastery is genuinely cursed or simply difficult: one points to games finding a workable middle, while the other argues that maximizing both extremes requires compromise. They also note different preferences among dexterity activities.

**Episode-specific limits**

- The discussion uses personal preferences and game examples; it does not measure learning curves or retention.
- The speakers recognize distinct audiences for both welcoming games and deliberately punishing ones.

## The CURSED nature of list building!

**Source:** [The CURSED nature of list building!](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=L2QHtCoEmYM) · **Video ID:** `L2QHtCoEmYM`

Tradeoffs between pre-game roster construction and tactical play, including balance and hobby commitment.

### List building can decide before play

Pre-game roster choices can create decisive matchups before either player reaches the table, shifting the game's main challenge away from tactical play. A force may lack the tools to answer an opponent's list, making even excellent in-game decisions insufficient.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Roster mastery rewards preparation but can make the match feel predetermined.

**Design prompt:** Test whether plausible lists can interact competitively and decide how much the game should reward pre-game choices versus table decisions.

[Supporting discussion, 11:50–13:06](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=L2QHtCoEmYM&t=710s)

### Reactive drafting reduces lock-in

Building a force after learning the scenario can make roster choice responsive and create a social, strategic pre-game activity. The Malifaux example lets players select from a collection once faction, objectives, and deployment are known; drafting is also discussed as a model for Magic.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Reactive selection rewards owning more options and can lengthen setup, while locked lists are simpler to prepare.

**Design prompt:** Consider scenario-aware selection or a bounded draft when the game wants roster strategy without long-term list lock-in.

[Supporting discussion, 5:24–6:47](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=L2QHtCoEmYM&t=324s)

### Clarify the tournament contract

Players can accept different list-building expectations when an event clearly states whether it prioritizes competitive optimization, narrative composition, or another style. Composition scores and format restrictions can set expectations before participants arrive.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Constraints reduce some forms of optimization but may feel arbitrary without a shared purpose.

**Design prompt:** Publish the event's roster and theme expectations clearly rather than relying on informal assumptions about what lists are acceptable.

[Supporting discussion, 17:48–18:55](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=L2QHtCoEmYM&t=1068s)

### Perfect balance can erase choice

If every option is equally good in every context, roster selection can become busywork rather than an exploration puzzle. The speakers distinguish meaningful discovery of stronger and weaker choices from an idealized system where any random selection works.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Balance prevents hopeless choices, but excessive uniformity removes the purpose and expressive value of selection.

**Design prompt:** Aim for situational choices with identifiable strengths and weaknesses instead of universal equivalence.

[Supporting discussion, 20:09–21:29](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=L2QHtCoEmYM&t=1209s)

### Teach roster risk before purchase

A visibly weak option can teach players to evaluate a roster before they spend money and time building it, though designers should avoid hidden traps. The discussion suggests making poor choices recognizable in the rules and leaving room for creative players to find surprising uses.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Clear tradeoffs teach; obscure traps can turn hobby investment into an expensive punishment.

**Design prompt:** Make roster strengths and risks legible before assembly, and test whether unusual builds have plausible use cases.

[Supporting discussion, 24:37–25:52](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=L2QHtCoEmYM&t=1477s)

### Reduce granularity to contain homework

Roster systems can preserve expression with broad choices instead of requiring detailed equipment decisions for every model. Chunked selections such as leader, faction, and subfaction can generate a distinctive force while reducing point-by-point configuration.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Coarser choices lower preparation burden but provide less control and fewer optimization paths.

**Design prompt:** Set roster granularity according to how central list building is; do not assume every weapon or model needs a separate priced choice.

[Supporting discussion, 27:03–28:21](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=L2QHtCoEmYM&t=1623s)

### Hobby decisions have different reversibility

Roster choices can become physical commitments when players assemble and paint specific equipment, making errors more costly than abstract deck-building mistakes. A player may choose a weapon for appearance and later discover it is weak after spending money and time on the miniature.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Irreversible modeling can make decisions feel weighty to some players and stressful or punishing to others.

**Design prompt:** Make physical options flexible or disclose their gameplay implications before players build models around them.

[Supporting discussion, 32:54–34:00](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=L2QHtCoEmYM&t=1974s)

**Differences in approach or unresolved questions**

- The speakers acknowledge both substantial enjoyment in solo list building and a preference for minimizing that homework. They discuss strong balance as a possible threat to discovery while recognizing the alternative of balanced forces that require different styles of play.

**Episode-specific limits**

- List-building and balance practices are discussed through selected games and anecdotes; no universal balance standard is established.
- Rules and prices mentioned reflect the speakers' experience at recording time and should not be treated as current edition guidance.

## This game is CURSED!

**Source:** [This game is CURSED!](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=DR_YX-BuHic) · **Video ID:** `DR_YX-BuHic`

How conflicting design goals create tradeoffs, with examples of late-game tension, decision weight, and randomness.

### Name the incompatible goals

A cursed problem occurs when two desirable goals undermine each other, and repeated attempts to satisfy both can trap a design in endless revision. Each fix moves the design toward one goal while breaking the other, producing a loop of patching and reversal.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Tradeoffs are unavoidable; the useful work is deciding what balance suits the intended game.

**Design prompt:** State the competing goals plainly, then choose a deliberate point between them instead of searching for a perfect solution.

[Supporting discussion, 1:15–2:30](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=DR_YX-BuHic&t=75s)

### Late-game tension versus decision weight

Promising that the game can be won on the last roll can conflict with the claim that every earlier decision matters. A random final score can overwhelm the strategic value of prior turns, while allowing any turn to end the game changes the expected experience.

**Tradeoff to consider:** A late surprise heightens suspense but may make earlier play feel irrelevant; a highly consequential opening can reduce comeback chances.

**Design prompt:** Choose how much weight to give early decisions and final uncertainty, then make that balance visible in scoring and game length.

[Supporting discussion, 2:33–3:51](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=DR_YX-BuHic&t=153s)

### End-now design is a valid niche

A game can intentionally allow a decisive early victory when that suits its audience and competitive identity. The War Machine example allows a powerful early strike to end the match, treating that possibility as a deliberate tournament feature.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Fast decisive outcomes reward preparation and execution but split audiences and can shorten play sharply.

**Design prompt:** If early victory is part of the promise, signal it clearly and ensure the players value that risk.

[Supporting discussion, 4:31–5:49](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=DR_YX-BuHic&t=271s)

### Randomness can reset context

A limited amount of randomness can make players reinterpret the board and adjust, while excessive randomness removes strategic control. A surprising event changes a piece's position or state; skilled play includes anticipating and exploiting possible outcomes.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Recontextualization creates lively moments but needs limits so planning remains meaningful.

**Design prompt:** Add randomness where it creates fresh decisions, and test whether players can learn to adapt to its effects.

[Supporting discussion, 16:59–18:05](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=DR_YX-BuHic&t=1019s)

### Decide which choices should matter

Not every decision needs equal consequence; a game can emphasize some choices while using other moments to introduce uncertainty or spectacle. The speakers contrast gritty tactical choices with splashy physical outcomes that shake up a previously understood board state.

**Tradeoff to consider:** Consistency supports mastery, while disruption creates surprise; the design needs an intentional mix.

**Design prompt:** Map where players should feel control and where the game may surprise them, then align mechanics with that rhythm.

[Supporting discussion, 17:40–18:52](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=DR_YX-BuHic&t=1060s)

**Episode-specific limits**

- The episode uses the speakers' definition of a cursed problem and illustrative game anecdotes, not a formal design framework.
- Claims about specific games are personal interpretations and may vary by edition, scenario, or play group.
